# 7. Offers, pricing and bundles

## What an offer is and how buyers judge value

### Definition of an offer

- An offer is the full set of what a visitor is shown to convince them to buy. A discount may be part of it, but the offer also includes everything else presented to persuade the purchase. [L0408.007]
- Anything that makes the buyer more likely to say yes, such as a bonus, a bundle or a guarantee, counts as part of the offer. An offer is more than a product on sale at a discount. [L0757.014]
- (Older advice, Source B 2021-22) In marketing, an offer is the full package a buyer sees when deciding whether to buy from you: the product, the business behind it, and everything that persuades them. A discount or bundle is only one possible part of that package. [L0411.002]
- (Older advice, Source B 2021-22) A complete offer has nine elements: marketing messages for the product, the product itself, product price, shipping or delivery, a USP package, risk reversal, customer service, trust factors, and bonuses with value delivery or perceived value. [L0411.021]
- (Older advice, Source B 2021-22) Resellers compete mainly on the business. Own-product brands compete more on the product. [L0411.021]
- (Older advice, Source B 2021-22) A total offer includes the product and its benefits, the marketing message, the price, the unique selling proposition, risk reversal (protection if something goes wrong), shipping or delivery arrangements, customer service, trust factors such as reviews, and any special offer such as a discount, sale price or free gift. A discount alone is only one small part of the offer. [L0414.017]
- (Older advice, Source B 2021-22) The USP package is how the unique selling proposition is presented on the page. It can overlap with other offer elements, such as risk reversal, but it can also be entirely separate. [L0411.025]
- The offer is more than the price. Branding, the unique selling point, bundle or BOGO structure, and the perceived value of the whole deal all shape it. Add-to-cart rates depend on these elements. A weak offer is often the first reason a landing page does not convert. [L1260.016, L1345.012]
- Customers buy an offer, not just a product. The same item can look far more valuable with a free bonus, free shipping and quantity discounts, without any change to the item or its base price. [L0860.001]
- The offer must be relevant to the buyer's problem. High perceived value does not require adding more items. Speaking directly to the prospect's situation raises perceived value more than a generic "buy this" message. [L0643.008]

### The value equation

- Customer perceived value follows a ratio. Dream outcome and perceived likelihood sit in the numerator; time delay and effort and sacrifice sit in the denominator. This ratio determines purchasing decisions, and all four components influence whether a customer buys.
  - Sum form: `(Dream outcome + Likelihood of achieving it) / (Time required + Effort and sacrifice)` [L0233.001]
  - Product form: `Value = (Dream outcome × Perceived likelihood of achievement) / (Time delay × Effort and sacrifice)`. Raise the first two factors and shrink the last two to make an offer more valuable. For a product, show customers how little time and effort they need. [L0502.002, L0549.025]
  - The worked example in [L0233.004] uses the sum form.
- The four factors, judged from the buyer's point of view:
  - **Dream outcome:** what the buyer wants to become. [L0502.003]
  - **Perceived likelihood:** how possible the buyer believes it is to reach that outcome. [L0502.003]
  - **Time delay:** how long it takes. [L0502.003]
  - **Effort and sacrifice:** what the buyer must invest. [L0502.003]
- Dream outcome examples: a necklace promises looking stylish and expressing identity; fitness promises a great body. Identify your product's dream outcome and communicate it throughout the offer structure, naming and product description. [L0233.002]
- Build perceived likelihood through high-quality customer reviews, before-and-after photos, testimonials and comparative evidence showing results. [L0233.003]
- Worked example: a gym membership scores `(100) / (50) = 2`. Supplements for the same goal score `(100) / (10) = 10`. The outcome promise is identical, but supplements have 5x the perceived value because they require less time and effort. [L0233.004]
- Gyms and supplements sell the same dream outcome, but supplements win larger demand because they promise faster results with almost no effort. A six-pack through training can take two to three years or more, which makes the time delay and effort very high. [L0502.004]
- Numeric check: a numerator of 10 divided by a denominator of 2 gives 5. Lowering the denominator while keeping the numerator high raises the result. [L0502.043]
- As time and effort approach zero, perceived value approaches infinity. Sources differ on how this compares with the other levers:
  - Cutting time and effort matters as much as strengthening the promise. [L0502.002]
  - This asymptotic multiplier drives purchasing decisions more powerfully than improving the dream outcome or the likelihood of success. Products that need minimal time and effort will substantially outperform identical-outcome products with more friction. [L0233.022]
- Shorten the time delay by promising short delivery, such as one to two weeks instead of months. Fast competitors such as Amazon can offer same-day shipping, so the gap matters. [L0502.006]
- Sales copy should shrink the time, effort and sacrifice in the buyer's eyes, for example by showing a simple three-step process. This makes a high price feel justified. [L0919.037]
- Buyers pay for the value they expect to get. If the offer delivers little perceived value, selling and convincing people to spend money on the store becomes very difficult. [L0502.001]
- Buyer types differ:
  - Impulse buyers purchase quickly from a clear offer.
  - Comparison buyers check other stores.
  - Facebook and TikTok ads draw attention. Google Ads and comparison-shopping buyers look for the best deal.
  - The store offer has to convince both types. [L0502.031]
- Before launching a landing page or offer, ask whether you would buy it yourself at that price with that offer, and why someone else should. Add as much value as possible until the answer is yes. [L0502.013]
- Perceived value created through brand, presentation and marketing angles lets a product sell at a much higher price than its cost suggests. Prefer products with high perceived value potential. [L0708.007]

### Making the offer valuable (CURVE and bonuses)

- A CURVE offer stands for Clear, Unique, Risk-free and Valuable. Each letter is a test the offer must pass, and the offer should be reviewed against all four. [L0411.007] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- **Valuable:** the customer perceives more value or benefit than from competitors. Improving one element while keeping the others equal can make the whole offer more valuable. [L0411.020] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- **Unique:** position the total offer so it is perceived as unique within the market. A different offer from competitors raises conversion more than changing a button colour. An average presentation can sell a great offer, but a poor offer cannot be saved by a great presentation. [L0414.018] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- **Clear:** simple offers convert best because shoppers spend mental effort understanding complicated deals. A complex offer such as "50% off your third item" loses buyers. A simple, obvious deal makes customers act quickly. [L1404.017]
- An offer works best when the buyer would feel unreasonable refusing it, usually through a clear saving or added value tied to their problem. [L1131.059]
  - A bonus that duplicates the main product adds little, because a buyer who needs one item does not want a second one.
  - A complementary product that solves a related problem feels more valuable. Example: an organiser for a wallet buyer. [L1131.059]
- Bundles and products with extra value, such as a case or accessories that help the buyer complete the job the product solves, increase the perceived value of the offer. [L1218.030]
- Perceived value makes a product look more valuable than a competitor's without a large product change. Ways to add it:
  - bonus ebooks
  - a coupon code
  - a lifetime guarantee
  - bundling the product with a smaller item from the supplier [L1153.029]
- Irresistible offers maximise perceived value so the customer feels they are getting a bargain. Tools include discounts, free gifts, free resources, money-back guarantees and bundles that stack value. [L0919.020]
- Discounts raise conversion rates because a visitor perceives a discount as a valuable offer. This is the underlying reason behind most promotion types used to increase sales. [L1400.010]
- Explain the cost to develop the offer, such as expert teams, lab testing and the number of repetitions a product went through. This turns a feature list into a story of value. Identical features and benefits written with development detail sound more valuable than the same product described briefly. [L1370.071]

### Offer types

- Offer types available to an e-commerce store, with their variants:

| Offer type | Variants or notes |
|---|---|
| Percentage off | Sitewide, specific SKUs, tiered (e.g. spend $100, get 20% off after that) |
| Dollar off | Sitewide or specific SKUs |
| Buy one get one (BOGO) | Free, or discounted (e.g. 30% off the second item) |
| Mystery box | Bundle of unknown contents sold as a 'trust us' pack |
| Free express shipping | Upgraded shipping at no cost, used before cut-off dates |
| Bundle deals | Multiple products sold together, often combined with % or $ off |
| Subscribe and save | Recurring orders with a discount |
| Gift with purchase | Free product from another range added to a paid order |
| Post-purchase upsell or cross-sell | Offer made after checkout |
| Lifetime warranty | Extended guarantee as the offer |
| 100-day free trial | Long trial period as the offer |
| Just pay shipping | Product free at checkout, customer pays shipping only |
| Exclusive memberships | Paid or sign-up access to exclusive deals |
| Loyalty program | Points or perks for repeat customers |
| Purchase to go in draw | Purchase entitles the buyer to a prize draw | [L1404.034]

- Monetary offer types with example forms:

| No. | Offer type | Example form |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Percent off each purchase | 10% off each purchase |
| 2 | Spend X, save X | Save $X when the order is over $Y |
| 3 | Buy one, get one free | Second item at no charge |
| 4 | Free expedited shipping | Faster shipping at no charge |
| 5 | Free white glove delivery | Premium delivery service at no charge |
| 6 | Free extended warranty | Longer warranty at no charge |
| 7 | Entered to win a prize | Entry into a prize draw |
| 8 | Make an offer | Shopper names a price | [L0757.018]

- The offer must fit the product, the audience and its price point. For low-ticket items a free second item can be affordable because the extra cost is small. Weak offers such as BOGO at 20 percent off are seen as boring and ineffective. [L1469.005]
- Offers improve ad conversion. A brand running a promotion can afford to spend more on Meta ads than a competitor selling the same product without one, and still win the customer. [L1404.003]
- Offers reduce customer acquisition cost and lift revenue, clear slow inventory, create urgency and generate buzz and word of mouth. The main goal is revenue growth, driven by scarcity, urgency and the confidence buyers feel while a promotion runs. [L1404.002]
- Offers can roughly double revenue and make forecasting harder, because a well-matched offer can cause a sharp jump in sales. Plan for offers as a regular growth lever rather than an occasional event. [L1404.001]
- (Source D 2026) Strong offers are the primary lever for scaling. A well-designed offer combined with existing ad quality and conversion rates can increase ROAS from 2x to 4x overnight and CVR from 1.5% to 2.5%. [L0232.001]

### The no-brainer gap

- A no-brainer offer creates a large gap between perceived value and price. This is achieved by adding a lot of value, lowering the price, or a combination of both. Focus on increasing value, since a strong offer can make even average copy convert. [L1365.034]
- Value stack: total value = sum of the stated value of each item in the offer, shown as value rather than price. The final price is a fraction of that total, so the gap between total value and final price makes the offer feel irresistible. Example: items with a stated total value of $12,000 offered for $990 show a gap of about twelve times the price. [L1365.038]
- Bundle build-up: list every component of a bundle with its standalone price, add up the total hard value, then show a much lower total price. Name, value and state each bonus as free. Example: a training package whose parts were valued at over $5,200 but priced at $3,895 for a 24-month contract, with bonuses listed as free. [L1370.072]
- Anchoring: build perceived value by anchoring a higher price against a comparable product that offers a similar result, such as a hedge fund strategy costing six figures, so your price looks like a good deal. [L1365.036]
- Scarcity, by limiting the number of units available, raises perceived value and adds urgency. [L1365.036]

### Why price alone is a weak lever

- Price is part of the offer, but competing only on price is a weak strategy. If the rest of the offer matches competitors, a slightly lower price can make the total offer stronger. [L0411.023] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Cutting the price to attract buyers makes the product look cheap, since shoppers assume the best products in a category are not the cheapest. A lower price lowers perceived value and can reduce sales, which is why many businesses price themselves out of the market. [L1365.035]
- Never build a store around the lowest price. Raise the price by increasing the value you provide, since higher prices often signal higher quality and build trust. [L0788.051]
- Price should match what the buyer believes the product is worth. Compare against what competitors charge for similar products, and aim to make the buyer feel they get more for a higher price. Products with high perceived value include electronics, advanced products and items made from premium materials. [L1215.003]
- Sources differ on when to price competitively:
  - Very simple products, such as a basic knife, cannot gain much perceived value, so price them competitively. [L1217.063]
  - Products with several features or benefits can justify higher prices. [L1217.063]
- Price shock: a price noticeably higher than competitors' or unfamiliar pricing can lead to abandonment. Check product pricing against the market and make the offer attractive before lowering the price. [L0640.043]

#### Further details

- The 40-40-20 principle (credited to Ed Meyer, adapted for e-commerce) splits the chance of conversion this way: 40% from reaching the right audience at the right time (traffic), 40% from the offer, and 20% from how the offer is presented (website design). Most people focus only on the 20% presentation part. [L0414.016]

## Choosing a price range and ticket size

### Why price point changes the work

- Building and marketing a store takes the same time and effort whatever the average price. Niche selection, supplier research, website creation and ad investment stay constant, whether products average $10 or $1,000. [L0011.001]
- At 10 sales per day, an average price of $10 gives $100 daily revenue and about $30 daily profit. The same 10 sales at $1,000 average give $10,000 daily revenue and about $3,000 daily profit. With constant work, price point is the primary variable determining profitability. [L0011.003]
- At 30% net margin, a $10 cell phone case yields $3 per sale and a $1,000 stand-up paddleboard yields $300 per sale, a 100x difference per transaction. [L0012.003]
- Higher-priced products need more visitors to convert into buyers, so their cost per acquisition is higher. Product price therefore determines what acquisition cost is acceptable. [L0440.041]
- A $50 product with $20 profit needs many more sales than a $3,000 product with $900 profit to reach the same total. A high-ticket product with a clear price and a niche-matched audience makes every sale meaningful. (Older, Source B 2021-22.) [L0458.041]
- The higher the price point, the longer the customer journey and the more touch points a prospect needs before buying. Around $30 to $40, the whole journey can happen within minutes on a single site visit. (Older, Source B 2021-22.) [L0481.011]
- A very low price can signal low quality and lower perceived value. For products where value perception matters, a higher price can increase sales, and the right price depends on the product. [L0388.006]
- For products buyers judge by price, a higher price can signal quality and increase demand. Know whether your category rewards low prices or premium signaling before setting the price. [L1261.102]
- High prices create resistance and limit the reachable market, so price must match the scale of sales needed. High-volume products can be priced low, while low-consumption products need a high margin per sale. [L1261.101]
- Narrowing the audience to a specific problem can support a higher price than broad positioning, because a specific audience accepts a price a general audience would not. [L1194.035]
- Online buyers have a practical spending ceiling when paying by card without much deliberation, so very high prices reduce conversion. [L1147.028]

### Minimum price floors

The sources give different floors because they describe different models. Use the floor that matches your situation.

| Floor | Context | Source |
|---|---|---|
| $200 for core products (higher preferred) | Core niche of a dropshipping store; products at $50, $100 or $150 should be avoided even if they sell easily | [L0474.001] |
| $200 for small parcels (UPS/FedEx) | Shipping-cost profitability | [L0016.005] |
| $1,000 or higher | Large or heavy freight items, per the same rule | [L0016.005] |
| About $600 to $700 or more | Large and heavy items should ideally sell for this (older, 2019) | [L0356.035] |
| About $600 or less not worth pursuing | Large heavy products sold with free shipping; price must cover COGS and full shipping and leave net profit | [L1180.013] |
| $200 minimum, about $80 exception | Products sold to customers (2025). Exception: add-on and cross-sell products on high-ticket pages, minimum around $80 | [L0472.056] |
| $90 minimum | High-ticket products; not recommended for beginners, viable for experienced operators with a sufficient testing budget | [L0209.032] |
| $20 bare minimum | Facebook and TikTok ad costs keep rising and thin margins leave no room for testing (Source C) | [L0708.006] |
| $25 minimum on paid platforms | Beginners: margin will not cover customer acquisition costs; aim for a 3x to 4x markup | [L1174.014] |

Floor rules in more detail:

- Product prices should not go below 200 USD (older, 2019). [L0356.035]
- Do not list products below 200 dollars, and prefer prices higher than that (older, 2021). [L0968.080]
- Target minimum selling price is about $200 per product, so enough margin remains after shipping (2023). [L0855.038]
- As a guideline, the minimum product price is about $200 to keep margins solid. Large or heavy items should be above that. Avoid products that sell for around $50 through paid ads, since they are too hard to make profitable (2023). [L0804.013]
- Expensive items should be at least about $200 and ideally more. A range of $500 to $1,000 keeps customer service load manageable. Products above $2,000 bring more customer service work (older, 2020). [L0468.102]
- Only add products priced at 200 dollars or more. Items at 100 to 200 dollars were not worth the effort in that business (older, 2021). [L1279.054]
- Target a selling price between $25 and $200. The range leaves room for impulse purchases and enough margin for paid ads (Source C). [L1174.013]
- Target about 50 USD or more where possible, because these products give better profit margins. [L0363.016]
- Accessories and add-ons can be priced as low as about 50 dollars when they ship from the same supplier as the main product, adding no extra shipping or acquisition cost. Products that are hard to ship or source should not be priced that low (older, 2022). [L0665.060]
- Avoid individual products priced as low as $50 (older, 2020). [L0852.050]

Where the floors conflict:

- The $200 floor applies to Source A's core-product model, where a product receives paid traffic. [L0474.001, L0804.013]
- The $20 and $25 floors come from Source C's paid-social context, where cheap products must be made profitable through higher average order value. [L0708.006, L1174.014, L1107.032]
- The $50 accessory floor applies only when the add-on ships from the same supplier and adds no extra cost. [L0665.060]
- The $80 add-on floor applies only on high-ticket pages. [L0472.056]

### Sweet spots by channel

| Channel | Sweet spot | Conditions | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| TikTok | About $20 to $50 | Young audience; this is the ideal range | [L1382.014, L1436.021] |
| TikTok, beauty and health | Somewhat higher than $20 to $50 | Staying within $20 to $50 is safer | [L1382.014, L1436.022] |
| TikTok, home decor | Unlikely to work at $120 to $190 | Priced at $120 or $190 | [L1382.014, L1436.021] |
| TikTok, wider range | 20 to 40 USD can also work well | Coach reported up to 100k revenue from this range | [L0363.016] |
| Facebook, front-end offers | About $50 to $80 or lower | Works when people are sent directly to a sales page | [L0413.042] |
| Facebook, cheap products | Around $25 | Needs a higher average order value because Facebook ads are expensive; add a quantity break discount | [L1107.032] |
| Amazon FBA | $19.97 to $39.97 | Avoid going above $60, because you want customers with disposable income; balances profit with margin for competitive ads and organic ranking | [L0228.011] |
| eBay | No sweet spot in these units | No knowledge unit covers eBay pricing |, |

### Low-ticket versus high-ticket trade-offs

#### Low-ticket products

- In the US market, products in the 30 to 60 USD range are easier for beginners than high-ticket products. Cost per purchase for products around 70 to 100 USD is far higher than for cheap products around 20 USD or less. [L0693.053]
- Products around $10 are hard to scale, so higher-priced books are preferred. [L1175.029]
- A heavy book costing about $20 including shipping is not viable to test. Average order value must be much higher to leave room for ad costs and margin. [L1175.028]
- Target an average unit price of about $30 to $100. Go higher only with awareness that fewer products will be available at higher price points. This band is the filter used for the selection described here. [L1175.030]
- A low margin struggles to scale. Example: a product costing $26.99 and sold at $49.99 leaves about $23 profit, and an expected CPA of $15 to $20 eats most of it. [L0995.020, L0999.014]
- To fix a low margin, raise average order value through bundles, upsells or a free gift added to a higher price, as long as the gift cost is low. [L0995.020, L0999.014]
- Very low-priced items, such as a $7 wallet, only make sense with high traffic and high sales volume, since profit comes from many small margins. Test the same product at a higher price such as $19.99 in a different market. [L1185.016]
- Example: a $6.47 competitor product with a $1 wholesale cost, $1 shipping and $2.90 Amazon fees left $1.57 before ads. That is too little to pay for paid traffic, which shows why a higher price point and a bundle make ad spending workable. [L1153.027]

#### High-ticket products

- High-ticket products start at a $90 minimum price point. They are not recommended for beginners but are viable for experienced operators with a sufficient testing budget. [L0209.032]
- Avoid products above roughly $100 to $200 as a beginner. COGS becomes high, and payouts from sales can take time to reach the bank. The supplier may have to be paid from your own pocket. [L1174.015]
- Upper middle-class pricing of $1,000 to $2,000 reduces pre-sale effort and post-sale service requirements compared to lower price points (older, 2021). [L0270.004]
- Sofa example: sell $1,500 sofas to middle-class customers with better creditworthiness and healthy margins, rather than $5,000 sofas that overextend customers on credit. Current market (2023): focus on $1,200 to $1,800. [L0274.019]
- High-priced products can sell online at $6,000 to $10,000, such as infrared saunas, when the product fits the buyer's interest and the store looks credible (older, Source B 2021-22). [L0771.071]
- Example: a competitor sold a high-ticket product at 220 EUR, with a sourcing cost of about 50 EUR checked through CJ Dropshipping, leaving a margin of more than 150 EUR per sale before ad costs. A wide spread shows room for margin but does not prove the price converts, so test the product page and ads before scaling. [L0693.061]
- A high-ticket nursing book priced around 143 to 229 USD sold only about 87 units and was judged too thin and spiky to build on. A high price alone does not signal a good product, so check units sold per day before pursuing it. [L0354.027]

### High-ticket ranges and service load

- Start product selection in the $200 to $1,000 range to reduce the number of incoming calls. Products from $2,000 to $4,000 are possible from day one if you are willing to answer and return phone calls (2023). [L0835.050]
- Price ranges typically fine for a single order without heavy pre- and post-sale service are up to about $2,000. Around $2,500 is a borderline range where customers ask more questions before and after purchase, but it is not forbidden (older, 2022). [L0854.032]
- Products above $2,000 can still work, mainly for bulk buyers such as businesses or event planners. Expect more pre-sale and post-sale customer service in that range (older, 2022). [L0854.033]
- If a niche has a very wide price range, such as $30 to $12,000, focus on a mid-tier band such as $500 to $5,000. Mid-tier products usually have the most demand without the heavy service load of the top tier (older, 2022). [L0854.034]
- Sell all products in the niche priced between $2,000 and $4,000 if they fit the niche, not just the cheapest ones. Higher-priced items earn more net profit per sale (older, 2020). [L0468.101]
- List all higher-priced products you can source, even those in the $2,500 to $8,000 range. The worst outcome is that nobody buys them. The best outcome is that you start receiving inquiries and direct orders for high-value items (older, 2020). [L0358.032]
- There is no fixed price ceiling. Do not stop at around $1,000. Higher-priced products can be sold as long as the minimum margin and the other rules still apply (older, 2019). [L0960.039]
- Premium versions of a product can be sold above the niche's average price only if there is enough demand at that price segment. Check the segment's sales volume and suppliers' expected volumes before committing (older, 2020). [L0967.072]
- When a product has many variations, consider stocking the higher tiers, because customers who want custom or premium options will pay more for them (older, 2021). [L0801.056]
- To test higher-priced products in an existing store, add only the top two or three brands in the higher tier first. Measure them in the campaigns before adding more, so the budget does not spread too thin (older, 2022). [L0854.037]

### Testing higher-priced products

- Before scaling, test the product page and the ads, because a wide margin spread does not prove the price converts. [L0693.061]
- A low-value item sold at a price buyers see as too high for its usefulness struggles to convert. An ice cube listed at about 17 to 18 USD was hard to justify because cheaper alternatives exist. [L1444.053]

### Products below the floor

#### Do not list them as core products

- Products under 200 dollars are not recommended for listing. If they are listed, set their CPC to 0.01 dollars and do not run ads to them, so the budget goes to higher-priced products (2025). [L0857.028]
- Products under $200 should usually be sold as an upsell or add-on rather than as a standalone product (older, 2020). [L0852.050]
- Cheaper items below the core threshold should be sold as add-ons or upsells, not as core products that traffic is bought for. Keeping them off the main ad targets avoids spending on traffic that yields too little profit per sale. [L1147.015]
- Example: a $150 accessory is not a core product because it is below the $200 threshold. It is added to a higher-priced main product as an add-on, so the extra sale adds profit without receiving paid traffic. [L1147.017]

#### Keep them as add-ons and upsells

- Products below the core threshold are not dropped. They still earn profit as add-ons or upsells attached to core product orders. [L1147.018]
- For products under $200 that buyers usually purchase in multiples, check competitors' bundle or package prices to see typical quantities. If buyers commonly buy sets, the niche can work (older, 2021). [L0469.073]
- Do not use the loss-leader or make-money-on-backend model. For a low-ticket product, bundle it with a high-ticket item or use it only as an upsell or remarketing offer (older, 2020). [L0268.026]
- Add a quantity break discount so customers buy more units per order. This raises average order value on cheap products. [L1107.032]

#### Raise the price of very cheap items

- Raise the prices of very cheap items so they leave room for profit. A novelty ice mold priced around 7 USD was judged too low. The lesson suggested at least 10 to 15 USD for such items. [L1325.017]

### Building price-range collections in Shopify

Procedure: create a bounded price collection.

1. Create a new collection and enter the name, for example '$1,000 to $2,000'.
2. Set Collection type to Automated.
3. Keep Products must match on 'all conditions'.
4. Add condition 1: Price, is greater than, 999.99.
5. Add condition 2: Price, is less than, 2000.01.
6. Click Save.

Procedure: create an open-ended price collection.

1. Create a new collection and enter the name, for example '$2,000 and up'.
2. Set Collection type to Automated.
3. Change the condition field to Price.
4. Set the operator to is greater than and the value to 2000.
5. Click Save.

New products above the threshold are then added automatically. [L0371.019, L0371.020]

#### Further details

- For a US nursing study guide, a price near 24.99 USD was judged to leave too little margin, while a pocket guide format around 37 USD was judged the better price point. Check whether a low price leaves room for your unit cost before choosing it. [L0354.026]

## Unit economics and margin math

### The full cost stack comes before any price

- Before you set a price, list every cost per sale and per month: the total supplier price including product and shipping, payment fees, customer acquisition cost, fixed costs, and a reserve for returns. Only then do you know the break-even point and the margin each sale needs. [L0436.001]
- A price set without payment fees, acquisition cost, fixed costs and refunds can turn a sale that looks profitable into a loss. Check the full cost stack before you trust a sticker price. [L0436.002]
- Acquisition cost matters only for paid traffic. If most customers come through word of mouth, acquisition cost has little effect on pricing. If you buy traffic with paid ads, every price must cover acquisition cost on top of product cost and fees. [L0436.006]
- Example: a product that costs $10 from the supplier and sells for $15 leaves too little room to cover acquisition, fees, returns and profit. With paid ads, a gap that narrow is very hard to make profitable. [L0436.007]
- Use a profit calculator before creating any offer to calculate profit margins, set new ROAS and CPA targets, and verify that the offer stays profitable at scale. [L0232.002]
- Reduce unit economics aggressively (product cost, operating expenses, fulfilment cost per order). Every dollar saved per unit becomes budget for advertising, which is the core lever for scaling. [L0041.008]
- Wholesale means the cost of goods sold: only the product price paid to the supplier. Shipping is a separate cost and is not included in wholesale. [L0966.041]

### Profit per sale formulas

- Net profit per sale = MAP − COGS − ad budget per sale − shipping cost. Keep COGS at or below 50% of MAP and the ad budget per sale at or below 10% of MAP. [L0359.068]
- Profit per sale = selling price (at or above MAP) − wholesale cost − shipping cost (if shipping is free) − advertising cost per sale. Keep advertising cost within about 10% of the selling price. [L0667.068]
- Profit per sale = selling price − wholesale price − shipping cost − payment processing fee − advertising cost per sale. Heavy items with high shipping costs need this check before the product is listed, because margins can be very thin. [L0468.100]
- Profit per order = selling price − supplier wholesale cost − card processing fees (about 2.9%) − advertising cost per sale − shipping cost, if you offer free shipping. *Older advice (2021).* [L0671.262]
- Net profit per sale = MAP − wholesale − average shipping cost − ad cost per sale. Example: MAP $1,000, wholesale $500, shipping $150, ad cost $100 gives $250 profit, a 25 percent net margin. [L0966.044]
- Profit before shipping per sale = MAP − wholesale − ad spend (up to 10% of MAP) − payment fee (2.9% of MAP). Example: MAP $1,000 and wholesale $500 leave about $371 before shipping, after $100 of ad spend and a $29 fee. [L0970.104]
- Profit before ads = sale price − wholesale cost − shipping cost − Amazon fees (including the referral fee). Example: $19.97 − $3.00 wholesale − $1.50 shipping − $6.40 Amazon fees = $9.07 profit before ads. The Amazon referral fee in this example is 15 percent of the sale price. [L1153.026]
- Net margin = (sale price − wholesale − shipping − ad cost per sale) ÷ sale price. Example: $1,000 − $500 wholesale − $150 shipping − $100 traffic leaves $250, which is 25%, below the 30% target the lesson sets. *Older advice (2019).* [L0663.055]
- Break-even price for a bulky product sold with free shipping: minimum selling price = COGS + average shipping cost + other per-order costs (customer acquisition, card processing fees, traffic and the seller's time). If the price only equals COGS plus shipping, net profit is $0 before those other costs, so every sale loses money once they are added. [L1180.014]

**Worked example at 30% off MAP with free shipping.** A $600 product costs $420 from the supplier (600 × 0.70), leaving a $180 margin (600 − 420). The ad budget is up to $60 (10% of MAP), leaving $120 (180 − 60). Subtract the card fee of 2.9% of the sale and other order fees from that $120. *Older advice (2020).* [L0967.069, L0967.070]

### Landed cost and shipping

- Before you list a dropship product, add the supplier product price to the shipping cost for the destination country to get the landed cost. Example: a cup washer costs $5.95 for the chosen variant and totals $18.85 with shipping to a US address (shipping $12.90). Compare this with the planned selling price of $39.99. List the product only when the gap leaves enough room for ads, fees and profit. [L0635.032]
- Test every product first against this baseline: the minimum profit margin should be at least two to three times the cost of shipping and fulfilling the item. [L0706.005]
- Heavy and bulky items need the shipping check before listing, because the freight cost depends on size and weight, not on the selling price. [L0468.100, L0855.041]
- Doubling the wholesale cost is not enough on its own. With an $80 product at a 2x markup, only $80 is available to cover shipping, ads, fees and profit. *Older advice (2023 is not flagged; this statement is from 2023).* [L0855.040]
- Example: a $180 futon with $90 wholesale and $250 freight loses $160 per order. A $1,800 leather sectional with $900 wholesale and the same $250 freight keeps $650 per order. [L0855.042]
- Example: a bulky home gym sells for $2,500 and the wholesale cost is $1,250, giving $1,250 gross. Shipping costs about $400, leaving $850. After the order processing fee, about $800 remains. Ads to acquire the customer cost about $100, leaving about $700 net. The lesson: bulky or heavy products only work when the selling price is high enough to carry the shipping costs. A $600 item that costs over half its price to ship is not worth selling. [L0551.056]
- Freight and order costs for big items: with free shipping, a $500 item costing $250 leaves $250 of margin, while a $3,000 item costing $1,500 leaves $1,500. If free shipping costs about $300 on a large item, the cheap item drops to about $50, while the expensive one keeps about $1,200. *Older advice (2021).* [L0671.263]
- Shipping cost depends on distance, so orders to far-away regions can cost much more than nearby ones. Calculate the average shipping cost per product and raise prices or charge shipping where profit is too thin. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L1201.024]
- Example of a thin-margin order: a customer paid $500 for two units of the same product, with no discount, paid via PayPal, and no sales tax. The supplier total was $475 ($250 for products, $225 for shipping). The PayPal fee was $14.80 and fraud insurance was $5. Result: gross profit $25, net profit $5.20, net margin 1.04%. Here shipping almost equals product cost, which signals that the price should go up or shipping should be charged. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L1201.011]
- Batch size must match product margin. Small batches cost more to ship per unit, which is affordable only if each unit earns enough margin. On a $400 product, small-batch shipping is a minor issue. On a $50 product, high shipping costs can wipe out profit. [L1508.031]
- Example of spread versus shipping, stand-up desks (average package 5 ft × 6 in × 24 in, average weight 100 lb, average shipping $200 per order). *Older advice (2022).*

| Supplier | MAP price | Wholesale (50%) | Spread | Left after $200 shipping |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A (standard) | $1,000 | $500 | $500 | $300 |
| B (standard) | $1,200 | $600 | $600 | $400 |
| C (solid wood, premium) | $2,000 | $1,000 | $1,000 | $800 |
| D (budget) | $700 | $350 | $350 | $150 |

[L0854.027]

- Spread = MAP price − wholesale cost. Net after shipping = spread − average shipping cost per package, because free shipping is paid from the margin. *Older advice (2022).* [L0854.026]
- A budget desk with MAP $400 and wholesale $200 leaves a $200 spread. After $200 average shipping, nothing is left, and the 2.9% transaction fee then makes the sale a loss. *Older advice (2022).* [L0854.028]
- Ad costs and merchant fees must come out of the spread after shipping. A remaining $150 can look fine but may not cover them, so check whether the product is still worth advertising before you scale it. *Older advice (2022).* [L0854.029]

### Spread between MAP and wholesale

- Margin before expenses = MAP − wholesale cost. With a MAP at 2x wholesale, a $500 wholesale cost leaves $500 to cover ads, fees and shipping. [L0942.016]
- The starting point for profit is the spread between MAP and wholesale. Net profit should aim for 30%; the other levers are customer acquisition cost and shipping cost. [L0965.039]
- Profit margin is determined by the spread between wholesale cost and MAP. After negotiating supplier pricing, the biggest remaining costs are shipping and ad spend. *Older advice (2023 course is not flagged).* [L1094.082]
- At a selling price of 2x wholesale, gross margin = (selling price − wholesale cost) ÷ selling price. Example: (1,000 − 500) ÷ 1,000 = 50% before card fees, shipping and ad costs. These remaining costs must come out of this 50% to reach a net profit of about 30%. [L0969.114]
- Margins below the 50% spread are not a standard target. A lower margin can be acceptable only when each sale still brings in large absolute profit, such as a very expensive item or a large bulk order. [L0970.105]
- A high product price alone does not justify a low margin. An item priced around $4,000 with a 10% margin is still not acceptable. [L0970.106]
- A 25% profit margin does not mean paying 75% of the sale price for the product. After cost of goods sold, the remaining money must cover shipping, ad costs, processing fees, team costs and net profit. *Older advice (2018).* [L1109.019]
- On a $1,000 sale, wholesale should be $500 or less, leaving about $500 for shipping, ads, fees and profit. The minimum target in this example is $250 net profit, which is 25% of the sale price. *Older advice (2018).* [L1109.021]
- With careful control of shipping, ads and fees, net profit margins can reach about 40%. Margins below that depend on the same cost control. *Older advice (2018).* [L1109.022]
- Doubling the wholesale cost is not enough; see the spread-and-freight case above. [L0855.040, L0855.041]

### Payment fees

- Card processing fee of about 2.9% of the sale is used in several older worked examples. [L0671.262, L0970.104, L0854.028]
- Calculator gateway rule: the standard Shopify Payments example is 5.5% of the price plus $0.30. Use your own Shopify Payments rate if it differs. [L0986.001, L0986.002]
- Worked gateway figures in the calculator: a $40 price on site gives a gateway fee of $2.50 (5.5% × 40 + 0.30), and an $80 price on site gives $4.70 (5.5% × 80 + 0.30). [L0986.008, L0986.009]
- In the PayPal example above, the fee of $14.80 on $500 is about 2.96%, and fraud insurance of $5 was also paid. [L1201.011]
- Payment fees are paid from the margin along with shipping and ads. Their total must be subtracted before any net-profit check. [L0671.262, L0962.098]

### Ad cost per sale, break-even ROAS and break-even CPA

**Break-even ROAS and CPA**

- Break-even ad spend per day = number of sales needed × average order value. Equivalently, sales needed per day = daily ad spend ÷ average order value. [L0378.032]
- Break-even cost per purchase = selling price − (product cost + shipping cost). Example: a $39.90 product with a $17 fulfilment cost yields a margin of about $22. *Check:* $39.90 − $17 = $22.90, so the source's $22 is rounded down; with $22.90 the break-even ROAS is 1.74, not 1.77. [L0440.037]
- Break-even ROAS = selling price ÷ margin before ads. Example: $39.90 ÷ $22 gives about 1.81 in the source's own $22 margin; $39.90 ÷ $22.90 gives 1.74. The source states 1.77. Ad campaigns must keep ROAS above break-even to make money; below it means a loss. [L0440.038, L0440.037]
- Break-even ROAS is the return on ad spend at which ad revenue only covers product cost and fees. Example: product cost $18 and selling price $39.99 gives a break-even ROAS of 1.85 in the Dropship Toolkit. Product cost alone gives $39.99 ÷ $21.99 = 1.82, so the 1.85 depends on the tool's fee assumptions. If actual ROAS is above break-even, the product makes money. If it is below, raise the price or choose a product with a lower cost of goods. [L0661.025]
- Break-even ROAS in the calculator = total revenue ÷ margin $. Buy 1 gives $43 ÷ $32.50 = 1.32; Buy 2 get 1 free gives $83 ÷ $60.30 = 1.38. [L0986.007, L0986.008, L0986.009]
- Target cost per acquisition (CPA) must be below break-even cost per purchase. For a product sold at $30 that costs $10, acquire each customer for under $20, because that difference is the margin available for advertising. [L0440.039]
- At the start, the margin of $20 is calculated without advertising expenses. Other costs such as apps, Shopify and hiring are considered later. Once a product has run for more than a month, reserve about 5 to 10 percent of product plus shipping cost for these. [L0440.040]
- For a store with several price points, calculate break-even ROAS from the lowest-priced product at the start. After about 100 orders, switch to the actual average order value and the average cost of product plus shipping. [L0645.044]
- Work out break-even and selling price before setting the campaign structure. For a $40 product, the maximum CPA is around $15-$18. [L1195.044]
- Ad budget ceilings: keep ad cost per sale at or below 10% of MAP. The 10% rule is a budget ceiling for net-profit planning; break-even CPA is the point where a sale stops making money. These answer different questions, so check both. [L0359.068, L0667.068, L0970.104, L1195.044]
- Margin cushion: do not run a store with margins so thin that one day of ad account neglect could lose money. Keep enough room in each product's margin to absorb mistakes and ad fluctuations. *Older advice (2021).* [L0962.099]

**ROAS targets for paid launches**

- When launching a product on paid ads, start at a medium price point rather than a very cheap one. The minimum acceptable starting point is a 3.5x return on ad spend (ROAS), with a long-term target of around 8x. [L1215.006]
- Raising average order value (AOV) can turn an unprofitable item into a profitable one, because the extra revenue covers the margin gap. Measure profitability per order after each AOV change. [L0920.019]

### Gross versus net margin

**Gross margin**

- Markup to gross margin: 43% markup on wholesale ≈ 30% gross profit; 54% markup on wholesale ≈ 35% gross profit. [L0255.004]
- Gross margin per unit = selling price − supplier cost, before transaction fees, shipping and advertising. Example: a product sold at $38.99 with a supplier cost of about $20 leaves roughly $19 per unit before those other costs. [L0372.028]
- Compare products by profit per order and by how many orders the same total profit requires, not by margin percentage alone. A $50 product with $20 profit has a 40% margin, but a $3,000 product with $900 profit has only a 30% margin and still earns 45 times more per sale. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L0771.069]
- A typical dropship net margin is about 30% of total sales revenue. [L0011.002]
- Business margins: about 23% after negotiating volume discounts with suppliers. [L0226.036]
- A margin of 25% or higher is the target for gross margin on products. Products with thinner margins often cannot carry the cost of paid ads. *Older advice (2021).* [L0853.022]
- Gross margin in the calculator: Margin $ = Total Paid − Total Cost For Us, where Total Cost For Us = COGS + Pick N Pack + Shipping + Gateway. Margin % = Margin $ ÷ Total Paid. The margin is therefore after product, fulfilment, shipping and gateway fees, and before ad spend. [L0986.007, L0986.001]
- The lesson's 70% profit margin threshold for slight price cuts below competitors does not say whether it is gross or net. Calculate it only after COGS and ad spend are subtracted, so the number reflects what is actually kept. [L1149.013]

**Net margin**

- Net profit margin after all costs must be at least 20% and aim for 30%. Costs include cost of goods, shipping, ads, Shopify fees, merchant fees and other expenses. *Older advice (2021).* [L0962.098]
- Target an average net profit margin of about 30% across the business after all costs. Do not treat the MAP-to-wholesale spread alone as the margin, because shipping and ads still need to be paid from it. [L0966.045]
- Net profit should aim for 30% of the sale. A supplier that gives only about 15% margin leaves very little room for ad spend, so reaching a 30% net profit target usually requires very low customer acquisition costs. Check this before you build a catalogue around that supplier. [L0965.039, L0965.087]
- Target profit after shipping is about 25 to 30 percent of the selling price, measured after freight. *Older advice (2023 course is not flagged).* [L0855.045]

### Minimum margin targets

**Targets at a glance**

| Measure | Threshold | Source | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Profit per sale (dollars) | At least ~$20, or a 3x markup | Source C | Start-point figure, before ads [L0298.031, L0440.035, L1219.011] |
| Profit per sale (dollars) | Target about $20 per product | Source C | Set final price after bundles and volume discounts [L1219.011] |
| Net profit per sale | Below roughly 10%: do not list | Source A (older, 2019) | [L1069.049] |
| Gross margin | 30% is thin; review before scaling | Source D 2026 | [L0351.045] |
| Gross margin | 30-40% per product; 50% possible but rare | Source B (older, 2021-22) | [L1148.019] |
| Gross margin | Below 20% is a danger zone | Source B (older, 2021-22) | [L1148.020] |
| Gross margin | 18-25% is the minimum for a winner | Source C | [L0661.024] |
| Gross margin | About 25% or higher | Source A (older, 2021) | [L0853.022] |
| Gross margin | Reject suppliers at or below ~15% | Source A (older, 2019) | [L0960.034] |
| Landed-cost margin | About 30%, not 15%, when shipping is separate | Source A (older, 2019) | [L0960.035] |
| Calculator margin | At least 70% before ads; 69%, 68%, 67% acceptable if 70% is out of reach | Source D 2026 | [L0986.010, L1182.001] |
| Net margin | At least 20%, aim for 30% | Source A (older, 2021) | [L0962.098] |
| Net margin | About 30% average across the business | Source A 2025 | [L0966.045] |
| Net margin | 25-30% of price after freight | Source A 2023 | [L0855.045] |

[L0298.031, L0440.035, L1219.011, L1069.049, L0351.045, L1148.019, L1148.020, L0661.024, L0853.022, L0960.034, L0960.035, L0986.010, L1182.001, L0962.098, L0966.045, L0855.045]

**Dollar and ratio tests**

- A typical target margin is about $20 per product. The final price should be set after you decide on bundles, bundle deals and volume discounts, since these change the margin. [L1219.011]
- Choose products with a minimum margin of about $20. Smaller margins make it harder to get sales and lead to more returns, complaints and chargebacks as order volume grows. When competitors charge close to the same price, increase average order value through bundles to improve profit. [L0440.035]
- Ratio example: a sourcing cost of about $20 and a selling price of about $40 was described as a very good ratio for a product. [L0530.025]
- The wholesale cost should be about 50 percent of the selling price, which is the typical target. Lower margins make it difficult to pay for shipping and ads. *Older advice (2020).* [L0551.054]
- A product with a 30% gross margin is thin, so its pricing should be reviewed before scaling. Thin-margin products leave little room for chargeback and refund costs. [L0351.045]
- A product is only a winner if its profit margin is at least 18 to 25 percent. A product that loses money does not count as a winner, even if it sells. [L0661.024]
- Margin below 15%: reject the supplier, because ad costs, shipping and payment processing fees must still be covered. A healthy margin is needed on top of these costs. *Older advice (2019).* [L0960.034]
- If wholesale includes shipping, or shipping is charged separately, the total landed cost still needs a margin around 30%, not 15%. Ad costs and processing fees must be paid from that margin. *Older advice (2019).* [L0960.035]
- Do not launch a store if most products have average margins of about 10 percent before ad costs. Negotiate better prices or drop low-margin products from the store. *Older advice (2021).* [L0962.097]
- Example: a store offering free shipping, a free gift and a price-match guarantee had margins too thin on about half its products. Removing the price-match guarantee first restored margins while keeping free shipping and the free gift. *Older advice (2021).* [L0962.100]
- Returns: use a return rate of about 1% to 2% at most for drop-shipped products. If a supplier has no bad reviews, returns do not need to be built into product costs. *Older advice (2021).* [L0853.041]
- Reserve for returns: list a reserve for returns among the costs per sale before setting any price. [L0436.001]
- Thin margins and low-margin suppliers are a problem on every scale: the margin rule applies to all products. [L0962.099, L0962.097]
- Margin rule for shipping: the minimum margin should be at least two to three times the shipping and fulfilment cost. [L0706.005]
- Small-margin products cannot absorb mistakes at scale; keep margin for returns, complaints and chargebacks. [L0440.035, L0351.045]

**Pricing and minimum-margin sanity checks**

- With a MAP at 2x wholesale, the margin before expenses equals the wholesale cost (see spread section). [L0942.016]
- A product should leave a clear margin before you run any offer; do not discount below the threshold. [L1148.022, L1204.069]

### Pricing from cost: setting the selling price

- Selling price = total cost per order ÷ (1 − target margin). Total cost per order includes product cost, credit card fees, merchant account fees, shipping and fulfilment cost per unit. For a 70% margin, price = total cost ÷ 0.30. [L1182.005]
- Example: for a product costing $8 with a 70% margin target, raise the price until margin reaches 70% after fees, shipping and fulfilment are added. The sheet shows the resulting selling price and margin, which you check against the 67-70% range before using the price. [L1182.006]
- Set the selling price so that gross margin reaches 70% of the price, the main pricing target for every product. If 70% is not reachable, 69%, 68% or 67% is the acceptable minimum. [L1182.001]
- Gross margin on the calculator target applies to offers; use the product-level benchmarks above when screening products. The two measure different bases, and a product with product cost at 50% of price will not reach 70% after fees. [L0986.010, L1182.001, L0551.054]
- Price the product at about three times the wholesale cost when it is not MAP-protected and still worth listing. *Older advice (2019).* [L1069.091]

**Procedure: fix a product that shows no profit at MAP with flat-rate shipping** *(older advice, 2019)*

1. Calculate the profit per sale as the selling price minus the wholesale cost minus the flat-rate shipping cost. [L1069.091]
2. If the profit is zero or negative, ask the supplier's sales rep whether a different price list applies to your account. [L1069.091]
3. If no different price list exists and competitors sell at break-even, do not list the product. [L1069.091]
4. If the product is still worth listing and is not MAP-protected, price it at about three times the wholesale cost, do not run Google ads on it, and rely on organic sales. [L1069.091]

### Offers, bundles and discounts

- Every offer must make financial sense. The free item or gift must be affordable, and the offer must still produce profit after ad spend and product costs. [L0650.035]
- An offer can never reduce the price to zero. Each discount, free item or bonus must still cover product cost, shipping and ad spend, so calculate the margin before running it. A free gift only makes sense when its cost is low compared with the order value it is tied to. [L1204.069]
- Example of a cheap novelty product: sold at $13 with a supplier cost of $2 to $3 from AliExpress, leaving roughly $10 gross margin per unit before fees. The presenter judged this healthy for a low-return, low-risk item. [L0655.018]
- Product cost examples for a bundle: a necklace costs about $4-5 to make, a jewellery box about $1, and a three-item bundle about $13, giving a margin of about $17 on a $29.90 price. [L0502.021]
- Bundles for buy-X-get-Y: enter the total supplier cost of all units in the bundle and the total price the customer pays, not per-unit figures. Mixing per-unit cost with bundle price distorts margin and break-even ROAS. [L0986.011]
- For a buy-X-get-Y offer, Price Before Discounts must be the full price of every unit in the bundle, including the free units. With a single unit at $40, Buy 2 get 1 free uses $120 here. An $80 Price On Site then shows a $40 saving and a Savings % of 33.33%. [L0986.020]
- Enter Price Before Discounts as the compare-at (strike-through) price the store displays next to the sale price, because the sheet calculates Savings and Savings % from it. The savings then match the discount shoppers see on the product page. [L0986.021]
- To compare a bundle with a single unit, divide the bundle's Price On Site and its COGS by all units in the bundle, including free ones. For Buy 2 get 1 free at $80 price on site and $18 COGS across 3 units, this gives about $26.67 revenue and $6.00 cost per unit, against $40 for a single unit. [L0986.022]
- Discount test example: Product B, with $450 supplier shipping and free customer shipping, drops to $300 gross profit (12% gross margin) and $202.20 net profit (8.09% net margin) after fees. With a 5% discount, gross profit is $175; with 10%, $50; with 15%, −$75. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L1148.022]
- New stores often do not sell at a profit at first and may only break even. The list price, supplier cost, negotiated supplier cost, and whether shipping is charged to the customer are all adjustable levers that take time to tune. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L1148.024]
- Test different price points and offer structures in the calculator before launching them, because each change moves margin, break-even ROAS and target CPA. Comparing scenarios on paper shows which offer can still pay for ads. [L0986.013]
- Bundles raise average order value, which improves profit per order. [L0440.035, L0920.019]
- Discount-level spreadsheet: the build-phase product pricing sheet (Product / Supplier Pricing) takes each product's list price, supplier cost and shipping paid by the customer. It shows gross profit and gross margin at 5%, 10% and 15% off. Check it before any discount to see how much margin each level leaves. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L1246.016]

**Procedure: setting an offer for a new store with no sales history** *(older advice, 2018)*

1. Compare the MAP price of each product with its wholesale cost. [L1085.070]
2. Ask the supplier for sample shipping costs. [L1085.070]
3. Calculate the expected profit per sale from these numbers. [L1085.070]
4. Base the offer on that profit. Do not choose a discount without these figures. [L1085.070]

### Margin calculators and sheets

**Calculator procedure (Margins & Offers sheet)**

1. Make your own copy of the shared margin calculator sheet. [L0986.001]
2. List every offer you run or plan to test, such as Buy 1, Buy 2 get 1 free or Buy 3 get 2 free; the list depends on your product. [L0986.001]
3. For each offer, enter the COGS as the total supplier cost of all units the customer receives in that offer. [L0986.001]
4. Enter the Pick N Pack cost and the Shipping cost you pay; if shipping is already included in COGS, leave Shipping at 0. [L0986.001]
5. Check that the Gateway column uses your Shopify Payments rate, the standard example being 5.5% of the price plus $0.30. [L0986.001]
6. Enter Price On Site as the total the customer pays for the offer, for example $80 for Buy 2 get 1 free. [L0986.001]
7. If you charge shipping, enter the Shipping Price the customer pays. [L0986.001]
8. Read Total Paid / Total Rev, Margin $, Margin % and BE ROAS for the offer. [L0986.001]
9. Enter Price Before Discounts to see Savings and Savings %. [L0986.001]
10. Read Target CPA for 2+ ROAS and for 2.5+ ROAS. [L0986.001]
11. Confirm that Margin % is at least 70%. [L0986.001]
12. Fill every cell for each offer you run before you use its numbers. [L0986.001]

**The sheet and its columns**

- A Google Sheets margin calculator with a Margins & Offers tab lets you test several offer structures side by side. Each offer is one row, with columns for costs, price, shipping, gateway fee, margin, break-even ROAS, savings and target CPA. Use it to compare prices and bundle structures before you launch an offer. [L0986.002]
- Table: Margins & Offers tab, one row per offer, with a Current Offers header row above them. [L0986.007]

| # | Offer | COGS | Pick N Pack | Shipping | Gateway | Total Cost For Us | Price Before Discounts | Price On Site | Shipping Price | Total Paid / Total Rev | Margin $ | Margin % | BE ROAS | Savings | Savings % | Target CPA for 2+ ROAS | Target CPA for 2.5+ ROAS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Buy 1 | 8.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 2.50 | 10.50 | 60.00 | 40.00 | 3.00 | 43.00 | 32.50 | 75.58% | 1.32 | 20.00 | 33.33% | 16.25 | 13.00 |
| | Buy 2 get 1 free | 18.00 | 0.00 | 0.00 | 4.70 | 22.70 | 120.00 | 80.00 | 3.00 | 83.00 | 60.30 | 72.65% | 1.38 | 40.00 | 33.33% | 30.15 | 24.12 |
| | Buy 3 get 2 free | blank | blank | blank | 0.30 | 0.30 | blank | blank | blank | 0.00 | -0.30 | #DIV/0! | #DIV/0! | 0.00 | #DIV/0! | -0.15 | -0.12 |

The Buy 3 get 2 free row is an unfilled example; its errors show what happens when price and cost cells are empty. [L0986.007]

- Worked example, Buy 1: COGS $8, no Pick N Pack or shipping cost, a $40 price on site and $3 shipping charged to the customer. Gateway is $2.50, total cost $10.50, total revenue $43, margin $32.50 (75.58%), break-even ROAS 1.32, and target CPA $16.25 for a 2+ ROAS target or $13 for a 2.5+ ROAS target. [L0986.008]
- Worked example, Buy 2 get 1 free: COGS $18 for all three units, no Pick N Pack or shipping cost, an $80 price on site and $3 shipping charged. Gateway is $4.70, total cost $22.70, total revenue $83, margin $60.30 (72.65%), break-even ROAS 1.38, and target CPA $30.15 for a 2+ ROAS target or $24.12 for a 2.5+ ROAS target. [L0986.009]
- The sheet's relationships, seen in both examples: Total Paid = price on site + shipping charged; Margin $ = Total Paid − Total Cost For Us; Margin % = Margin $ ÷ Total Paid; BE ROAS = Total Paid ÷ Margin $; Target CPA = Margin $ ÷ 2 for 2+ ROAS and ÷ 2.5 for 2.5+ ROAS. [L0986.007, L0986.008, L0986.009]
- Aim for a gross margin of at least 70% on every offer before you run ads for it. The 70% figure is the minimum target in this approach, and an offer below it has little room for ad costs. [L0986.010]
- Shipping charged to the customer goes on the revenue side (Total Paid / Total Rev), not into the product price. Shipping you pay for fulfilment goes in the Shipping column and adds to total cost. [L0986.012]
- Mistake: leaving a price or cost cell empty makes the sheet show #DIV/0! in Margin %, BE ROAS, Savings % and Target CPA, because the formulas divide by zero. Fill in every cell for each offer, including Pick N Pack and shipping, even when they are 0. [L0986.018]
- Profit calculator tool: Dropship Toolkit takes product cost and selling price and returns break-even ROAS. [L0661.025]

### Filtering products by net profit per sale

- Start from the spread: MAP minus wholesale, then take out shipping, fees and ads. A product that does not leave enough after these is not a candidate. [L0966.044, L0854.026, L0970.104]
- Keep a product only if its net profit per sale is above roughly 10%. Products below this are not worth listing. *Older advice (2019).* [L1069.049]
- Net profit per sale check (target 30% net, 20% floor): use the net-profit formula for each candidate, then compare against the threshold. [L0962.098, L0966.045]
- Compare candidates by profit per order and by how many orders the same total profit requires, not by margin % alone. [L0771.069]
- For a catalogue with hundreds of products, do not calculate every item. Focus on the best sellers, which are often fewer than 20 out of 500 products and give the highest leverage. For large catalogues, group products into price buckets, such as $2,000, $1,700, $1,500 and $1,300, and use averaged targets per bucket. *Older advice (2021-22 course).* [L0446.014]
- Shipping-heavy items with high shipping costs need the net check before listing, because margins can be very thin. [L0468.100]
- Amazon net check: profit before ads = sale price − wholesale − shipping − Amazon fees. [L1153.026]
- Products whose net profit is below the threshold and that show no profit at MAP with flat-rate shipping: see the procedure above. [L1069.091]
- Do not launch a store on a supplier list where most products are around 10% margin before ads. [L0962.097]

## Setting the base selling price

### Placeholder prices during the build
- Enter a fixed placeholder price such as $69 in the Price field during the build, not an empty field, so the preview shows a realistic layout. Replace it once the final margin and price are decided. [L1331.013]
- Demo product prices should be realistic but need not be exact, because they are placeholders that get updated when real supplier products are added to the store. [L0088.009]
- Price demo products in line with the niche's actual market prices. Example: selling price $799, compare-at price $999, cost per item $300. [L0036.011]

### Inputs to collect before setting a price
- Price every product for the USA market by default, because most customers are there, and use US costs and US fees when filling in the sheet. [L1182.002]
- Set the price from several inputs together: supplier cost, the margin needed after all per-order costs, competitor prices, and the value the customer perceives. Product cost only sets the minimum and does not determine the final price on its own. [L1182.019]
- Include ad spend and COGS (cost of goods sold) in the calculation before fixing the final price. Do not decide the price from product cost alone. [L860.009, L1149.002]
- Base prices on healthy margins that cover payment processing fees, shipping costs, ad spend and profit. Do not base pricing solely on competitors' prices, because low-margin competitors usually give poor service and fail within years, so long-term success needs sustainable margins. [L0274.018]
- Calculate the total dropshipping price for the chosen variant and destination country from the supplier platform, then add payment fees, the estimated customer acquisition cost, fixed costs per sale and a return allowance. [L0436.012]
- Payment fees are not in the 3x split (see Markup rules). At a $29.99 price, a 1.5% to 3% gateway fee costs about $0.45 to $0.90, which comes out of the $10 margin, so subtract it before accepting the price. [L0436.026]

### Full pricing procedure
1. Calculate the total dropshipping price for the chosen variant and destination country from the supplier platform. [L0436.012]
2. Add payment fees, an estimated customer acquisition cost, fixed costs per sale and a return allowance. [L0436.012]
3. Research competitor prices for the same or a similar product. [L0436.012]
4. Set a starting price and check it with the 3x rule. [L0436.012]
5. Adjust the price against the market and pick a discount strategy. [L0436.012]
6. Launch with ads and test prices and offers. [L0436.012]

### Markup rules
**2x: the minimum floor**
- The simplest starting rule is to sell at no less than 2x wholesale, using the supplier's MAP (minimum advertised price) as the reference. This leaves room for shipping, ads and fees. [L0854.031] (older, 2022)
- A rough check: wholesale cost should be about half the selling price (MAP). The spread must still leave money after expenses. [L0855.039]
- Minimum sale price = 2 x wholesale cost. A product with a wholesale cost of $500 should sell for at least $1,000. In a MAP market, the sale price column is set by MAP instead. [L1146.006]
- Example: a stand-up desk with a wholesale cost of $500 and an MSRP of $1,200 has a strong margin built in. If competitors sell it for $900, do not list it at $900, because the minimum is $1,000 (2x wholesale). [L1146.007]
- If competitors already sell above the 2x minimum, price at or near their level instead of at 2x, and do not undercut the market. Example: with a $500 wholesale cost and competitors at $1,200 on Google Shopping, sell above the $1,000 minimum. [L1146.008]
- The 2x calculation works in any currency, because only the numbers change. A wholesale cost of 500 in any currency gives a minimum price of 1,000 in that same currency. [L1146.017]
- Conflict with a blanket doubling: do not arbitrarily double wholesale prices. Validate the price against market rates. (older, 2022) [L0249.034]
- Resolution: the 2x figure is a floor to check against the market, not a default to apply to every product. [L0249.034, L0854.031, L1146.008]
- Never set the sale price by copying what competitors charge at the moment. Base it on wholesale cost using the 2x rule and the competitor check. [L1146.005]

**2.5x: testing and strong-brand starts**
- Start at least at 2.5x if you are new. [L0645.019]
- A markup of about 2.5x is a reasonable starting price when a product has high perceived value and several marketing angles or benefits. Lower competitor prices do not rule this out if branding is strong. [L1217.060]
- For a first test, start at 2.5x to 3x and raise prices as branding is added. [L0335.062, L0335.028]

**3x: the standard target**
- Aim for a markup of at least 3x, for example buying at $10 and selling at $30 or more. [L0335.028]
- Selling price at about three times the total dropshipping cost, then check whether the market accepts it. $10 total cost gives a $29.99 price: $10 for the product, $10 for customer acquisition and $10 as margin. [L0436.019]
- The same formula, stated as: selling price = 3 times supplier cost, covering supplier cost, customer acquisition cost and profit margin. Example: supplier costs $10, price $30. [L1172.030]
- Round the 3x result down to a.99 ending. A $10 total dropshipping cost gives 3x = $30, which becomes $29.99. [L0436.027]
- Set the selling price at a minimum of 3 times product cost, for example a $10 product sold at $30. Treat a $20 minimum selling price as the bare minimum. [L0440.036]
- Aim for at least 3x the cost of product plus shipping. If product and shipping cost $10, the price should be at least $30, otherwise the product is hard to scale profitably. [L0708.005]
- Aim for 3x the cost of product plus shipping, and start at 2.5x if you are new. Keep a minimum margin of $20 when the product price is low. [L0645.019]

**Organic versus paid traffic**
- Organic sales can usually be priced at two to three times cost, which leaves room to cover taxes and possibly shipping. Paid ad sales need a higher price than organic, because ad costs come on top of the product cost. [L0706.006]

| Landed cost | Sale price | Channel | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| $10 | $20 | Organic | Workable, leaves room for taxes and shipping |
| $10 | $30 | Organic | Better margin |
| $10 | $20 | Paid ads | Not profitable |

[L0706.007]

**4x: branded and visual products**
- Fashion products can usually carry a higher markup of around 4x once the store has custom images or videos and branding, because buyers pay for lifestyle and visuals. [L0335.062]
- In a fall sock example, one pack was sourced for about $5 and sold for $20, a 4x markup. The lesson describes this as a standard single-item markup. [L0347.015]

**10x: high perceived value**
- For products with high perceived value, such as jewelry, the selling price can be about 10 times the sourcing cost when buyers believe the item is worth it. A product bought for about $5 can be sold at roughly $50. Perceived value sets the ceiling, not the cost. [L0693.060]

**Fees, low-priced items and currency gaps**
- Build the currency difference into the price when buying from US suppliers in US dollars and selling in Canadian dollars. Otherwise the exchange rate gap can erase the margin. (older, 2021-22) [L0905.017]
- To offset eBay commission and payment processing fees on low-priced items, apply a markup rule to the product feed: any product below a set price is increased by a fixed percentage. For example, a fee of about 0.30 GBP per order can make cheap products unprofitable without a markup. [L1441.040]
- A cheap product bought at about $10 or less on AliExpress was sold at $29.99, matching competitors. The margin was about 60%, so price matching made it workable for a low-cost product. [L1444.052]

**Perceived value and premium positioning**
- Premium positioning allows higher prices when differentiation exists. Selling supplements at 50% to 100% above competitors is possible when a unique formula or packaging justifies the premium. Perceived value drives willingness to pay. [L0227.025]
- A product with high perceived value can be priced well above its supplier cost, for example a $23 to $25 supply cost with a potential selling price near $50. Check the supplier cost of the exact variant before assuming the margin. [L0372.029]
- A security camera that works like a light bulb went viral (video of about 6.7 million views, with a second video still growing). It sold for $38.99, with a supplier cost estimated at about $20. A similar product on Amazon was listed around $69. The estimated margin was considered good, and the perceived value suggested a price up to around $50, although TikTok buyers might not accept that. [L0372.027]
- A product that started at $49.99 was later sold at $89.99 without changing the product itself. The increase came from custom branding, custom packaging, custom content, good images and videos, and a branded website. [L0363.015]
- Strong product images and branding can raise perceived value enough to support a higher price in niches such as jewelry. Example: a necklace that once sold at $12.99 can be sold at $19.99 with good images, then offered as BOGO. [L1345.014]
- A case with a buy-cost of about $20 and a break-even of about $52 leaves room for a launch price of $49 or $59. [L1194.036]
- When a supplier sells versions of one product that differ in a single feature, such as a hood or open feet, the version with the extra feature can be priced higher. The price gap should match a feature the buyer can see on the product page. [L1194.057]
- When a viral product's selling price feels too high for its perceived value, look for a cheaper variation or another supplier that brings the price into the target range before running ads. Test only once buyers can see clear value at that price. [L0530.036]

**Platform differences**
- The same product can carry a different price on a different platform, because each platform's audience accepts a different price. Example: a store running ads only on Pinterest sold the same product at about $38. [L0871.092]

### Competitor and Google Shopping benchmarking
**Where to look**
- If no MSRP exists, search Google for competitor prices and match them. (older, 2022) [L0249.034]
- Compare competitor prices using the item price shown for the same product on Google Shopping. This is the price to benchmark against. (2024) [L0971.095]
- Do not base the selling price on Amazon prices, which are usually lower. Benchmark against competitors selling the same product on Facebook, since that is the platform used. [L0693.022]
- Ignore very low prices from competitor stores that look like low-effort tests, such as a product crossed out at 40 and listed at 20. These prices are not a reliable benchmark. [L0693.023]
- Compare your prices with competitors to decide whether to match, undercut or charge more. A higher price is defensible only when the customer perceives more value in your store. [L0436.013]
- A common starting benchmark is a retail price of about double the product cost. Verify this against competitor prices on Facebook before setting the final price. [L0693.024]
- Use the Pricing Research columns of the sheet: MSRP or RRP, MAP and competitor prices, plus whether competitors charge for shipping. Use them to compare your price against the market while moving the list price toward the best balance of sales and profit. (older, 2021-22) [L1148.025]

**Shipping in competitor averages**
- When estimating a competitor average price, include shipping if it appears on most Google Shopping listings for that product. Otherwise average only the product prices. (older, 2021) [L0968.076]
- Do not inflate the sale price to include shipping. Instead, set the MAP high enough that the markup covers shipping, customer acquisition and profit margin. (older, 2020) [L0268.023]

**Competitor research procedure**
1. Find several competitor listings for the same or a very similar product. [L1167.011]
2. Note the one-time purchase price and the subscription price for each listing. [L1167.011]
3. Note the spread between the lowest and highest price, and any subscription discount shown. [L1167.011]
4. Choose your price inside that range and set it in the Pricing section of the product. [L1167.011]

- Example: for a similar toothpaste tablet product, competitor listings ranged from $32 for a one-time purchase up to $48 depending on the purchase option. One listing offered a subscription at $28.80 (10% off a one-time $32 price), and another advertised Save 33% on a 4-month supply. [L1167.012]
- A demo price of $35 was chosen inside the $32 to $48 range. The demo was only an example: the final price should come from your own margin calculation, not from the competitor range alone. [L1167.013]
- A single-book example: the standard price in the Buy 1 tier is $41.95, and the lowest acceptable price for that tier is $39.99. [L1149.003]

**Positioning against competitors**
- Set the base price close to competitors' prices. If the profit margin is at least 70%, you can price slightly below them to undercut. If margins are strong but tighter, price slightly above them to protect profitability. [L0860.008]
- Price a product at roughly the same level as direct competitors, using the net margin after ad spend and COGS as the deciding factor. If that margin is at least 70%, price slightly below competitors to undercut them. If the margin is strong but tighter, price slightly above them to protect profitability. [L1149.001]
- The lesson gives no numeric boundary between "at least 70%" and "strong but tighter". Below 70%, only the direction is given (price slightly higher to protect profitability). Set the exact adjustment from your own margin calculation. [L1149.014]
- Matching a competitor's price, especially when they offer free shipping, without first factoring in ad spend and COGS skips the profit check. Price decisions need the full cost picture. [L0860.010]
- Premium positioning of 50% to 100% above competitors is possible with differentiation. [L0227.025]
- Check the price after rounding: the final price must still fall within the target margin range (see Psychological price endings). [L1182.011]

### Avoiding price wars
- Do not start a price war with competitors, because it rarely works in this kind of business. Check competitor pricing for similar products before setting your own price. [L1215.004]
- Do not undercut the market. Undercutting starts a price race to the bottom. [L1146.008]
- Matching a competitor's price can leave only about 5% to 7% profit. That is a red flag for paid traffic and a reason to negotiate supplier pricing before advertising. [L0966.047]

### Psychological price endings
- Psychological pricing means ending prices just below a round number, such as.99 or.95, which buyers perceive as lower. This makes the same product look more attractive than a whole-dollar price. [L0436.015]
- The 3x rounding step uses a.99 ending: round the result down, so $30 becomes $29.99. [L0436.027]
- Final prices are rounded to a.95 ending, such as $45.95 or $64.95. After rounding, check that the margin still falls within the 67% to 70% range. [L1182.011]
- Both endings are in use. The Source C rule uses.99 and the Source D rule uses.95. The sources do not say which one to pick for a given product, so test per market. [L0436.027, L1182.011, L1316.004]
- Price-ending tests matter: a price like 49.90 instead of 50 in the local currency can lift conversion. Test this per market rather than assuming one rounding rule works everywhere. [L1316.004]
- In a new market such as the UK, do not use an automatic currency conversion price. Use a price ending like 24.90 or 26.90 pounds instead of 27 pounds, which the mentor described as unattractive. [L1161.031]

### Anchoring
**Reference points before the price**
