- Anchoring: set a reference point before the reader judges a price. For example, compare the product with hours of cleaning each month or with the much higher cost of an alternative, so the real price looks small. Stacking bonuses with individual values and a total value, then showing a much lower price, uses the same effect. [L0516.029]
- Example copy: compared to the hours spent scrubbing each month, this brush is a small one-time cost. [L0516.029]

**Price anchors for thin margins**
- When profit margins are tight and large discounts would erode profitability, create a price anchor: duplicate the product, raise its listed price by 15% to 30%, then discount from that higher price. Customers cannot easily calculate the true savings because they lack a reference point for the actual product cost, which creates perceived value. [L0291.005]
- Example: original product cost $100. Duplicate the product and raise the price 20% to $120. Offer 30% off this higher price: $120 × 0.70 = $84 final price. The customer perceives $36 in savings, but the actual discount from original cost is only $16 (16%), which preserves margin. [L0291.006]
- Apply the price anchor strategy only when margins are low. With strong margins, offer straight discounts without the duplicate-and-inflate technique. [L0291.007]

**Buy-one-get-two-free anchor**
- Set a deliberately high price for one unit, then offer buy one get two free. Buyers compare the offer with the inflated single price, feel they receive the extra units free, and can picture giving them to relatives or friends. [L1219.010]
- Example 1: a $25 phone stand shown at $75 with buy one get two free. Example 2: an $18 pet brush shown at $54 under the same offer. [L1219.010]
- Raise the product price, then offer buy-one-get-one-free on top, so the offer still looks large while the margin stays intact. [L1436.046]

### Compare-at and strikethrough pricing
**Shopify fields**
- In Shopify, the Price and Compare at price fields use the store's currency, shown as a prefix in the field (for example A$ in an Australian store). Enter all prices in that currency. The compare-at price must be higher than the price to show a sale. (older, 2021-22) [L0452.031]
- Enter the selling price in the Price field and keep "Charge tax on this product" ticked by default. Cost per item is visible only to you and is used for the margin and profit display. (older, 2021-22) [L1166.014]

**Core rules**
- Do not use a compare-at price as the sale price. The actual selling price must match the product feed. Compare-at is only an additional display. (older, 2020) [L0468.103]
- Do not show every product as a discount. Use a strikethrough price only when the original price is legitimately higher. This maintains credibility. (older, 2022) [L0249.040]
- Only display a compare-at price that the product has really sold at. An inflated reference price is a deceptive pricing claim. Competitor pages show a sale price next to a struck-through original and a percentage saving, for example 59.99 against 119.99 with 50% saved. [L0600.038]
- Display a sale price next to a higher compare-at price with a percentage savings label, for example $19.99 next to a struck-through $39.99 with a SAVE 50% badge. This makes the saving visible on the product page. [L0347.019]
- Show a higher regular price crossed out next to a lower sale price, so the discount is visible at a glance. The higher reference price makes the sale price look like a real deal. [L1325.018]
- Examples in the same pattern: a regular price of $30 crossed out next to a sale price of $19.99, and a regular price of $12 crossed out next to a sale price of $7.49. The lesson's example was a regular price of $4.10 reduced to $1.49. [L1325.018]

**MSRP and MAP as strikethrough and sale price**
- List the MSRP as the strikethrough price and sell the product at MAP. Example: wholesale $500, MAP $1,000 and MSRP $1,250 gives $1,250 struck through and a sale at $1,000. Competitor sites can be checked for the strikethrough price, and it is the MSRP about 95% of the time. [L0941.050]
- If a supplier does not send an MSRP, create an everyday price to use as the strikethrough price. A missing MSRP happens roughly 10% to 20% of the time and is not unusual. [L0941.051]
- Show MAP as the main price on the product page and MSRP as the crossed-out compare-at price, which matches typical retail presentation. (older, 2021) [L0968.079]
- List products on the store at MAP, the minimum advertised price set by the supplier. Use MSRP as the compare-at price with a strikethrough so the discount is visible. (2024) [L1115.065]
- When a supplier also publishes an MSRP, set the MAP as the sale price and the MSRP as the compare-at price shown with a strikethrough. Example: MAP 1000 and MSRP 1200 gives a 1200 compare-at price. (2023) [L1094.076]
- When MSRP equals MAP, either set the compare-at price about 20% above MAP or leave it blank. Match the approach used by the top competitors. (older, 2020) [L1111.088]

**Volume discounts**
- For a 50% off volume discount, set the compare-at price to about double the sale price. Example: compare-at $79.99 and sale price $39.99. [L1345.016, L1260.019]
- For volume discounts: set the compare-at price to about double the sale price (for example, compare-at 79.99 and sale price 39.99), show the sale price in a larger font, add 50% off, and optionally a time-limited or event-based scarcity message. Use a theme or page builder such as PageFly or GemPages to highlight the compare-at price. [L1260.019, L1345.016]

**Multi-offer display**
- Example structure of a Posture Pro Corrector offer: regular price $49.99, a 20% off offer (implied price $39.99), a risk-free trial also at $39.99, and a free gift (Posture Fix Guide) shown as a $19.99 value. [L0313.019]

### Multi-currency and multi-market pricing
- Each extra market needs its own prices in its currency. With many markets, prices must be converted by hand, which is easy to forget and can leave products underpriced. Reducing to a few free markets was seen as simpler, while showing local currency at checkout is worth doing for the UK and similar markets. [L0694.026]
- A store serving a specific country should display prices in that country's currency, such as CHF for Switzerland, rather than USD. Local currency pricing makes the offer easier to understand and fits local payment methods. [L0492.068]
- Convert price ranges to local currency and keep lower-priced niches around $200 from dropping too low, such as to 150 pounds in the UK. Higher-priced niches can be less strict. (older, 2021) [L0469.075]
- Set prices per country based on local landed cost and shipping cost, not one global price. Example: dropshipping cost was $16 for Australia and $19 for the UK. The UK price was set higher (54.90 AUD) to protect margin, while Australia stayed lower because its shipping was cheaper. [L1316.005]
- Test price endings per market (see Psychological price endings). [L1316.004]
- In new markets, avoid automatic conversion prices (see Psychological price endings). [L1161.031]
- The 2x wholesale minimum applies in any currency (see Markup rules). [L1146.017]

<!-- Gap: no source unit covers "price per day framing", so this section is omitted rather than filled with unsourced content. -->

#### Further details

- Before you fix the final price, factor in ad spend and COGS (cost of goods sold). Do not decide the price from the product cost alone. [L0860.009]

## MAP, MSRP and wholesale: reading supplier pricing

### Supplier price lists: wholesale, MAP and MSRP

- A supplier price list gives three prices: wholesale (your cost), MAP (minimum advertised price, your selling price) and MSRP (manufacturer's suggested retail price, your strikethrough compare-at price). [L0255.001, L0939.011]
- Example: wholesale $500, MAP $1,000, MSRP $1,200. [L0255.002]
- Example of a supplier's price sheet after approval, usually one spreadsheet with three rows:

| Price | Role for your store | Example (USD) |
|---|---|---|
| Wholesale | cost you pay the supplier | 1,000 |
| MAP | the price you sell at | 2,000 |
| MSRP | the crossed-out compare-at price | 3,000 |

  *Older (2019).* [L1110.008]
- Definitions:
  - MSRP is the manufacturer's suggested retail price.
  - MAP is the minimum advertised price you may display.
  - Wholesale is what you pay the supplier. *Older (2019).* [L0663.026]
- For decisions, wholesale and MAP matter most; MSRP matters less. *Older (2019).* [L0960.033]
- MSRP is a reference point in the price list, not the price to aim for, because most retailers sell below it. [L1146.013]
- MSRP is always higher than MAP. [L1115.066]
- MSRP and MAP example: MSRP 400, MAP 350, wholesale 175. MAP is shown as the sale price; MSRP is shown struck through. *Older (2022).* [L0665.064]
- Example: a studio microphone with MSRP $300 and MAP $250. If the brand lets resellers advertise at MAP, sell it for $250. *Older (2019).* [L0663.025]
- Use the supplier's price list. Upload each product at the prices the supplier specifies instead of choosing your own. [L1147.014]
- Use the supplier's MAP as the listed price on the product page, and calculate margin from that same price. [L0964.162]
- Use MAP controls: pick suppliers that set a minimum price so competitors cannot undercut each other to zero margin, and set your prices from the supplier's list. [L1147.013]
- Price lists may show net price, MAP or IMAP, and wholesale. MAP is the sell price and wholesale is your cost. To confirm MAP, search the product name on Google and check competitor listings. [L0667.066]
- If a supplier gives neither MAP nor MSRP, you set your own selling price and check it against competitor prices. *Older (2022).* [L0969.113]
- Record MSRP/RRP and MAP together with competitor prices, and whether competitors charge for shipping, to see where your list price sits. *Older (2021-22, Source B).* [L1148.028]

#### MAP policy in three forms

- A supplier's MAP policy can appear as:
  1. a MAP column in the price list,
  2. a multiple of wholesale, such as 2x,
  3. a maximum discount below max retail price.

  Treat each form as the lowest price you may advertise. *Older (2018).* [L1109.023]
- Formula: MAP = wholesale × 2 when the supplier gives a multiplier. Example: $500 wholesale gives MAP $1,000. *Older (2018).* [L1109.020]
- Example: max retail price $148 with a 20% discount cap sets the lowest advertised price at $112. *Older (2018).* [L1109.024]
  - Check: 20% below $148 is $118.40, not $112. Confirm the exact figure with the supplier before using either number.
- Some lower-tier suppliers do not enforce MAP but still cap discounts at 20% below max retail price. Treat that cap as a MAP rule. *Older (2018).* [L1109.025]

### What MAP allows and forbids

#### Definition and minimum

- MAP is the lowest price a retailer may advertise or sell a product for, set by the supplier. Example: a $1,000 MAP on a standing desk means no retailer may list it below $1,000, which removes price competition between sellers. [L0486.004]
- MAP is the lowest price you may show in your store and in your ads. It is the default price on the product page and the price people see in an ad. *Older? No (2025).* [L0966.039]
- MAP sets only a minimum. A product can be listed above MAP but not below it. *Older (2020).* [L1111.087]
- MAP sets the lowest listed price. If MAP is $1,000, your listed price must not go below $1,000, the same as every other seller. *Older (2020).* [L0961.001]
- *Older (2022).* MAP is the lowest price a product may be advertised at, and the product page price must be at least MAP. [L1113.072]
- The price shown on a product page cannot be below MAP, because the "A" in MAP stands for advertised. [L0857.019]
- MAP is the lowest price a supplier lets retailers advertise. [L0969.113]

#### Allowed

- MAP has no maximum. You may list above MAP, but never advertise or display below MAP on the site or in ads. [L0667.063]
- Selling above MAP is permitted. Example: MAP $2,000, sold at $5,000, is allowed under the MAP policy. *Older (2020).* [L0358.016]
- Pricing above MAP is rare. It works only where few retailers sell the brand and competitors also price above MAP, which you can see on Google Shopping. *Older (2020).* [L1277.081]
- Prices should not be undercut by competitors, because suppliers enforce pricing policies. Work with brands where the margin is locked in. *Older (2020).* [L1277.083]
- "Add to Cart to See Price" may replace the price, as long as the lower price is revealed only after the buyer adds the item to the cart. *Older (2020).* [L0961.004]
- Lower prices with discount codes at checkout are a practical option unless the supplier agreement says otherwise. [L1285.037]
- A price-match guarantee matters only where the products come from MAP-enforcing suppliers. If the suppliers are not MAP suppliers, remove the price-match page from the store menu so shoppers do not see it. *Older (2022).* [L0429.007]

#### Forbidden

- Never list a product below MAP on the store, because it is advertised below the minimum advertised price. [L1285.037]
- Coupon codes do not change the rule: the advertised price is what draws clicks. *Older (2018).* [L1109.026]
- Bonuses count as a price. If the bonus is advertised below MAP, it breaks the policy. Example: a $100 MAP paddle with a bonus paddle advertised at $50 breaks MAP. *Older (2021).* [L0469.070]
- A MAP agreement, signed during supplier approval, means you may not advertise or sell below MAP. Every seller of that product must show it at or above MAP. *Older (2019).* [L1110.011]
- Once a supplier approves you, do not undercut other sellers. If a competitor's best price is $1,500, sell at $1,500, because price cutting eventually leaves no one with a profit. *Older (2020).* [L0961.005]

#### Why MAP exists

- MAP enforcement stops competitors from undercutting your price. Without it, competitors selling below your cost can drain your margin and sales. *Older (2019).* [L1275.071]
- Suppliers without MAP let competitors undercut, so avoid their products unless the margin is good. [L0855.052]
- Silver suppliers enforce MAP, so you compete only on service and brand, not price. Check that a supplier enforces MAP before you commit to its products. [L0486.009]
- MAP creates a level playing field among retailers. *Older (2021-22, Source B).* [L0879.037]

### The 2x spread rule

#### Core rule

- MAP must be at least 2 times wholesale. Example: $500 wholesale needs a MAP of at least $1,000. Wholesale can be no more than 50% of MAP. *Older (2019-2021).* [L0247.039, L0468.098, L1086.005]
- The 2x is an approximation for typical items: MAP should be about 2x wholesale to maintain healthy margins. *Older? No (2025).* [L0255.003]
- For products that will receive ad spend, MAP should be at least 2x wholesale. Example: $500 wholesale implies $1,000 MAP. [L0970.101]
- List at 2x wholesale as long as the price covers all expenses. Example: $1,500 wholesale listed at $3,000. [L0970.102]
- Minimum threshold: MAP must be at least 2x wholesale. If margins are insufficient, negotiate wholesale down rather than selling above MAP, because customers will buy elsewhere at lower prices. [L0251.009]
- Minimum rule example: MAP $575 on a $500 product is a 15% markup, which is not worth pursuing. *Older (2019).* [L0960.032]
- Example: if MAP is 500, wholesale should be no more than 250. If wholesale is 400, MAP should be at least 800. [L0934.032]
- Minimum spread used to decide whether a product is worth carrying: wholesale no more than 50% of MAP. [L0934.031]
- Target a MAP about twice the wholesale price, a 100% gap between wholesale and MAP. *Older (2019).* [L0663.054]
- Wholesale should be no more than half of MAP. Suppliers above that leave too little margin. *Older (2022).* [L0854.025]

#### Spread target and formula

- Spread = (MAP − wholesale) ÷ MAP. The target is about 50%. [L0835.048]
- For advertised products, aim for a minimum 50% spread between wholesale and MAP. This leaves room for up to 10% of the price in ad spend, a 2.9% transaction fee, and shipping. [L0970.103]
- Example: wholesale $161, MAP $230 gives a spread of about 30%, which is too low. [L0835.048]
- Example: wholesale $1,200, MAP $2,000 gives a spread of 40%. It leaves $800 profit per sale, which can be acceptable. [L0835.048, L0835.049]
- The 50% target has one accepted exception: a 40% spread on a $2,000 MAP product with a $1,200 wholesale price, because the $800 dollar profit still makes the sale worthwhile. [L0835.049]
- Minimum listed price formula: minimum listed price = 2 × wholesale cost (the MAP). Example: 500 wholesale must be listed at no less than 1,000. *Older (2021).* [L1279.050]
- The 2x rule is calculated on product cost only, excluding shipping. Example: wholesale $500, MAP $1,000. [L0966.042]
- A wholesale of $700 on a $1,000 MAP product is too thin for ads. Rely on free organic and free Google Shopping traffic instead. [L0966.043]
- First step when reviewing a product's margin: check the wholesale-to-MAP multiplier. *Older (2021).* [L0671.267]
- The ratio can narrow somewhat at higher prices, but a solid spread is still needed to cover shipping, ads and card fees. *Older (2019).* [L1110.009]

#### Multiplier below 2x

- A multiplier of about 1.8 can occasionally be acceptable. *Older (2021).* [L0671.129, L0671.264]

#### Thresholds at a glance

| Case | Figures | Verdict | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Target | MAP 2x wholesale, spread 50% | Standard for products with ad spend | [L0970.101, L0970.103] |
| Near-minimum | MAP about 1.8x wholesale (spread about 44%) | Occasionally acceptable | [L0671.129, L0671.264] |
| Exception | Spread 40%, wholesale $1,200, MAP $2,000 ($800 profit) | Can be acceptable | [L0835.048, L0835.049] |
| Too low | Wholesale $161, MAP $230 (spread about 30%) | Too low | [L0835.048] |
| Markup | MAP $575 on $500 product (15%) | Not worth pursuing | [L0960.032] |
| Commission | 20 percent commission | Bad deal | [L1110.009] |

## Exclusivity, direct sales and channels

- With true exclusivity, you set the selling price yourself, so MAP does not limit it. Confirm exclusivity in writing before relying on it. *Older (2018).* [L1109.027]
- A brand that sells directly to the public is fine for a reseller, as long as the brand lets you advertise at its MAP. *Older (2019).* [L0663.024]
- Suppliers may sell direct to the public at MSRP while retailers advertise at MAP. Check the supplier's MSRP and MAP before deciding whether the brand's margin works. *Older (2019).* [L1086.006]
- A supplier's own website may sell at MSRP while your store sells at MAP. That is acceptable because you set your own price. Check the supplier's retail price before listing so your store is not underpriced by mistake. *Older (2020).* [L0468.191]
- A direct-to-consumer supplier can be carried only if it sells at MSRP and lets retailers sell at MAP, or if its direct sales effort is weak, for example no ads and an outdated website. [L0857.023]
- A supplier that lists a product below MAP on its own website while others sell at MAP:
  - If your MAP is lower than their website price, the product may still be worth selling.
  - If they sell below the MAP you intend to use, skip the product entirely. [L0251.004]
- Some brands sell directly to consumers and to online retailers at different prices. Brands may give online retailers incentives, so verify pricing with the supplier. [L0940.030]
- Online retailers often receive better wholesale prices than large box retail chains, which makes online sellers a preferred channel when comparing supplier pricing. [L0940.031]
- A brand selling on Amazon does not disqualify it for your store, provided your listed prices follow the supplier's price sheet and pricing policy. *Older (2021).* [L0834.121]

## MAP outside the United States

- MAP policies cannot be legally enforced in the EU, Australia or New Zealand. Brands there often still require retailers to sign an agreement not to list below a set price, and enforce it through the account relationship. *Older (2021).* [L0834.110]

### Supplier pricing with little or no margin

#### Warning signs

- Avoid suppliers offering only 15% off MAP. After transaction fees (2.9%), shipping and ad spend, these are nearly impossible to sell profitably. *Older (2022).* [L0249.041]
- A 20% supplier discount on a $1,000 product makes MAP $1,000 with insufficient margin. *Older (2020).* [L0268.081]
- A supplier offering 50% off MSRP while MAP is 25% off MSRP leaves too little margin. The deal works only if competitors sell at MSRP. *Older (2021).* [L0671.130]
- A supplier whose wholesale is above half of MAP leaves too little room for margin. *Older (2022).* [L0854.025]
- A 20 percent commission is a bad deal. *Older (2019).* [L1110.009]

#### Options when margin is thin

1. Check the multiplier and spread against the thresholds above. [L0671.267, L0970.103]
2. If the spread is 40% and the dollar profit is strong, the exception may apply. [L0835.049]
3. If margin is insufficient, negotiate wholesale down. Do not sell above MAP to compensate, because customers will buy elsewhere at lower prices. [L0251.009]
4. If the product is listed above MAP, it must go without paid traffic: list at a higher price and use organic or remarketing only. Example: a $1,000 product with only 20% discount is listed at $1,600 with no paid traffic. *Older (2020).* [L0268.081]
5. Above-MAP pricing works where few retailers sell the brand and competitors also price above MAP. Check Google Shopping before you rely on it. *Older (2020).* [L1277.081]
6. Without MAP, the retailer sets its own price and must check it against competitor prices. [L0969.113]
7. Products without MAP enforcement are avoided unless the margin is good. [L0855.052]

#### Negotiation email

Use this template when competitors are undercutting you and the supplier's terms leave too little margin:

```
We noticed that [competitor name] is selling [product name] at [competitor price]. At that price, there is not enough margin for us to drive traffic profitably. Do you offer different pricing tiers, incentives or wholesale discounts for a retailer in our position?
```

[L0966.048]

### Competitors selling below MAP

#### Monitoring

- Monitor competitors for MAP compliance:
  1. Check their current discounts for MAP violations.
  2. Report any violation as soon as you find it. [L0920.012]
- Monitor for prices below your MAP, including product-specific discounts that can push a competitor under MAP. Report violations quickly. A competitor who undercuts for a long time can build a war chest and outbid you in paid advertising. [L1357.014]
- Minimum advertised price policies protect your margin. Monitor competitor prices for your products, and report violations to the manufacturer to enforce the policy. [L1472.037]

#### Checking whether the supplier enforces MAP

1. Pick a specific product from the supplier.
2. Search its name on Google and open the Google Shopping results.
3. Compare prices across sellers and confirm the listings are the identical product.
4. If the same product sells much cheaper at some sellers, MAP is likely not enforced.

*Older (2021).* [L0968.073]

#### Do not guess: ask the supplier

- When a competitor sells a supplier's products below the MAP price sheet, ask the supplier's sales rep rather than guessing. Send a screenshot of the competitor's listing and the supplier's price list, and ask whether the price list is wrong. *Older (2021).* [L0801.048]
- Email to a supplier sales rep:

```
Email to a supplier sales rep about a competitor selling below MAP:

Hi [sales rep name], I am seeing [competitor store name] selling [product name] for [competitor price], which is below the MAP price on your current price list. I have attached a screenshot of their listing and a screenshot of your price list. Do I have an incorrect price list, and could you confirm the correct pricing for this product?
```

*Older (2021).* [L0801.049]

#### Reporting a violation

- Report a MAP violation to the supplier immediately by emailing your contact with a screenshot of the violating price. *Older (2021).* [L0968.074]
- Full sequence when a supplier breaks MAP:
  1. Take a screenshot of the Google Shopping result showing the lower price.
  2. Take a screenshot of their website showing the price.
  3. Email the supplier with the proof.
  4. Ask them to reach out to the retailer to adjust price per policy.
  5. Give the retailer a few days to a week to fix it.
  6. If they don't comply, the supplier should remove them from the approval list.

  *Older (2022).* [L0272.026]
- Shorter sequence for reporting a competitor who violates MAP:
  1. Email your sales rep or supplier contact right away.
  2. Include a screenshot of the violation and the link to the page showing the lower price.
  3. Ask the supplier to act on the violation.

  *Older (2019).* [L0357.042]
- Another sequence:
  1. Take a screenshot of the competitor listing that sells below MAP.
  2. Send it to your supplier contact, naming the competitor's domain and stating it violates MAP policy.
  3. Ask the supplier to have it resolved.

  *Older (2021).* [L1279.051]

#### Google Shopping and price gaps

- Do not raise bids to fight a large price gap. MAP exists to level the field, and lowering your bids cannot close a price gap in Google Shopping, a price comparison engine. *Older (2021-22, Source B).* [L0879.037]
- Competing for top positions with a large gap wastes money. Shoppers still see the cheaper price and buy elsewhere. Clicks come from people checking the full price, but few convert. *Older (2021-22, Source B).* [L0879.038]

## Pricing products from suppliers without MAP

### Core floor: 2x wholesale for non-MAP products

- Without a MAP, the selling price should be at least 2x wholesale. Competitors should also sell at a minimum of 2x wholesale. For example, a 250 wholesale price implies a competitor price of at least 500. [L0247.038]
- The rule for non-MAP pricing is to set prices at least 2x wholesale. Research competitor prices. If competitors sell above 2x, match or exceed that level rather than undercutting. [L0270.013]
- Without a MAP or MSRP, the formula is: selling price = wholesale × 2. Example: wholesale 500 gives a selling price of 1,000. [L969.002]
- Minimum advertised price for suppliers without a stated MAP: MAP = 2 × wholesale cost. Example: wholesale $500 means the selling price should be at least $1,000, which leaves roughly 20 to 30% after expenses. [L1281.030]
- MAP means Minimum Advertised Price. [L0270.013]
- The 2x test: the average competitor selling price should be at least double the wholesale cost. If it is below double, treat it as a red flag and do not upload the supplier's product, since margins will not be sustainable. [L1051.006]
- Example with $500 wholesale: an average competitor price of $700 fails the 2x test, so the product should not be listed. An average competitor price of $2,000 passes, leaving about $1,500 per sale before fees. [L1051.007]

### Alternative markup and where the rule is recorded

- Where a manufacturer sets a minimum advertised price, follow the MAP price list. Where none exists, apply a fixed markup such as 20% to each product and compare it with competitor prices. [L1396.038] *(Older advice, 2021-22. This 20% fixed markup differs from the 2x floor above. Use the MAP list where one exists, and use the 2x floor for non-MAP products as described above. [L1396.038, L1051.006])*
- Put the pricing rule on the upload card so the person uploading does not have to guess. [L1396.038]

| Multiple of wholesale | Source | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| 2x | L247.038, L270.013, L969.002, L1281.030, L1051.006 | Default floor for non-MAP products |
| 3x | L1069.045 (2019, older) | No usable market price exists, and shipping does not wipe out the margin |
| 20% fixed markup | L1396.038 (2021-22, older) | No manufacturer MAP list exists; compare each product with competitor prices |

### Setting up price fields

- The compare-at price is the supplier's MSRP, and the sale price is the MAP. [L1281.031]
- Example: wholesale $500, MAP $1,000, MSRP $1,200. Set the compare-at price to $1,200 and the sale price to $1,000. [L1281.031]

### Pricing against the market

**Procedure: price a non-MAP product by market (2025)**

1. Search Google for the product from the supplier's brand to see the prices competitors list. [L966.049]
2. If competitors list it at a price that gives enough margin, list your product at that higher price. [L966.049]
3. If competitors list it too low for a sufficient margin, keep the product on your store at a markup you accept. [L966.049]
4. Do not include that product in your paid ad spend. Sales from free traffic still earn money without wasting ad budget. [L966.049]

**Procedure: start at 2x and adjust to the market (2020, older)**

1. Set the starting selling price at about double the wholesale cost. [L967.065]
2. Search for the same brand's products online to see what other retailers charge. [L967.065]
3. If other retailers sell at a higher multiple of wholesale, match that multiple instead of doubling automatically. [L967.065]

**Procedure: check the market before doubling (2021, older)**

1. Search for the product online. [L853.035]
2. Note what other sellers charge. [L853.035]
3. If most sellers charge about 2x or more, the higher price can work. [L853.035]
4. If sellers charge only about 1.2x, the markup is not worth pursuing. [L853.035]

**Procedure: price a non-MAP product, 2019 version (older)**

1. Search Google for the supplier's product names and note the lowest prices from competitor listings on Google Shopping. [L1069.044]
2. If competitors sell at a much higher price (for example $1,000 against a $200 wholesale cost), price the product at the market level and run Google Shopping ads for it. [L1069.044]
3. If competitors sell close to the wholesale cost (for example $300 against a $200 wholesale cost), do not run Google ads for that product, because no one will buy at a loss. List it only if needed for catalog completeness. [L1069.044]
4. If the market gives no usable price, set the price at three times the wholesale cost, provided shipping does not consume the margin. [L1069.044, L1069.045]
5. Exclude non-MAP brands from the price match policy. [L1069.044]

**Three-times formula (2019, older)**

- Selling price = wholesale cost × 3, as long as shipping cost does not wipe out the margin. Examples: $200 wholesale gives $600, and $500 wholesale gives $1,500. [L1069.045]
- This 3x rule sits alongside the 2x floor. The 2x floor is the newer and more common guidance. Use 3x only when there is no usable market price. [L1069.045, L969.002, L1051.006]

### What to do when competitors sell below 2x

The sources disagree on this case. Each applies to a different situation.

| Source | Action when competitors are below 2x |
|---|---|
| L969.002 (2022, older) | Still list at a minimum of 2x. Rely on free traffic instead of paid ads. |
| L1051.006 (undated) | Treat it as a red flag and do not upload the product. |
| L1069.044 step 3 (2019, older) | Do not run Google ads for the product. List it only if needed for catalog completeness. |
| L855.046 (2023) | List it at about double wholesale without ads. This keeps the store looking established. |
| L966.049 step 3 and step 4 (2025) | Keep it at a markup you accept and exclude it from paid ads. |

- Choose the option that fits your margin. If the product cannot reach an acceptable margin at the market price, the sources mainly differ on whether to list it at all. [L1051.006, L969.002, L855.046, L966.049]

### Avoiding price wars

- Do not win non-MAP sales by undercutting competitors. Price cuts start a race to the bottom, where competitors undercut again until no margin is left. Match the average market price when margins allow it, and win customers through conversion optimization instead. [L1051.008]
- Sell at MAP and do not manipulate feed prices. For non-MAP suppliers, match a low competitor price only when profit remains, because price wars leave everyone with less profit. [L469.071]
- Pricing far below the market, for example selling a $1,000 market product at $800, starts a price war and gives away profit. Price with the market instead. [L1069.046]
- Using the three-times rule without checking the market can leave money on the table, because the actual market price may be much higher than three times wholesale. Always check competitor prices first. [L1069.047]

### Selling above MAP with trust

- With a strong review history, a store can sell above MAP and still win sales. A small discount, such as $50 off a multi-thousand-dollar order, can close the sale when buyers want peace of mind. [L806.055]

### Price-match protection for non-MAP suppliers

**Procedure: protect prices when selling a supplier with no MAP policy (2019, older)**

1. Exclude that supplier's brands from your price match policy by adding a note with an asterisk and an exclusions list naming the brands on the price match page. [L357.039]
2. Hide or remove the price match icon or guarantee from that supplier's product pages. [L357.039]
3. Check the supplier's products on Google Shopping before contacting them. [L357.039]

**Rules**

- Exclude suppliers without a MAP shown on the product page from the price match guarantee. Their products should not be covered by that promise. [L247.043]
- If a supplier has no MAP policy, exclude its brand from the store's price match policy and check competitor prices before running ads on those products. [L1069.043]
- Do not offer a price-match policy for suppliers with no MAP. Check Google Shopping before setting your price. [L468.099]
- In markets without MAP policies, such as Australia or the UK, do not use price match guarantees. Set the sale price at least at double the wholesale cost. [L967.067]
- Price-match guarantees must never cover items you sell at a loss because of a supplier with no MAP policy. Otherwise, a customer can later find the product cheaper and demand a refund of the difference. [L357.041]

### Deciding whether to sell a product

- Sell no-MAP products only when few sellers offer them. If many companies sell the same product, the margin will be hard to achieve. [L357.040]
- Before contacting a no-MAP supplier, check how many companies sell its popular products on Google Shopping. [L357.040]

### Deciding whether to advertise a product

- If a supplier has no MAP and no margin, do not run ads to those products. List them at about double wholesale without ads, which keeps the store looking established. [L855.046]
- Run paid ads only on products where the market price gives enough margin. Otherwise, list the product without ads. [L1069.044, L966.049]

### Escaping restrictions with your own brand

- Creating your own brand from a no-name supplier product lets you avoid pricing and channel restrictions. Once you have a private brand with no restrictions, you can consider other sales channels, but only after the main store is consistently strong. [L357.043] *(Older advice, 2019.)*

### Escaping restrictions with bundles

- If a supplier does not enforce MAP, combine multiple units into bundles and advertise them as a combo product. Build a separate product page for each bundle. [L852.054] *(Older advice, 2020.)*

**Pattern: bundle differentiation (2021-22, older)**

- Combine the best-rated components from several suppliers, each already sold separately by its maker, into one starter kit under your own brand. Offer a recurring consumables subscription on top. [L1155.011]
- Pattern: take the top-liked part from brand A, the kit from brand B and the consumable from brand C. White-label each, then sell them as one bundle plus a subscription. [L1155.011]
- Example 1: a home brewing store white-labels a fermenter from one maker, a kit from another and a yeast refill from a third. It sells a starter set with monthly yeast delivery. [L1155.011]
- Example 2: a coffee gear store white-labels a grinder, a brewing dripper and a filter paper pack into one starter bundle, with a monthly filter subscription. [L1155.011]

**Case: 3D printer store (2021-22, older)**

- A store selling 3D printers ran for about one year. Customer comments showed that buyers liked the extruder from one maker, the kit from another and the filament from a third. [L1155.017]
- The owner planned to ask each maker to white-label its part, combine the parts into one starter bundle under the store's own brand, and add a filament subscription. [L1155.017]
- The owner sold the store before this plan was carried out. [L1155.017]

#### Further details

- Exclude suppliers without a MAP shown on the product page from the price match guarantee. Their products should not be covered by that promise. [L0247.043]

Protecting prices when selling a supplier with no MAP policy:
1. Exclude that supplier's brands from your price match policy by adding a note with an asterisk and an exclusions list naming the brands on the price match page.
2. Hide or remove the price match icon or guarantee from that supplier's product pages.
3. Check the supplier's products on Google Shopping before contacting them.
[L0357.039]

- Sell no-MAP products only when few sellers offer them; if many companies sell the same product, the margin will be hard to achieve. Before contacting a no-MAP supplier, check how many companies sell its popular products on Google Shopping. [L0357.040]

- Price-match guarantees must never cover items that you sell at a loss because of a supplier with no MAP policy. Otherwise a customer can later find the product cheaper and demand a refund of the difference. [L0357.041]

- Creating your own brand from a no-name supplier product lets you avoid pricing and channel restrictions. Once you have a private brand with no restrictions, you can consider other sales channels, but only after the main store is consistently strong. [L0357.043]

- Some suppliers have no MAP policy. Do not offer a price-match policy for those suppliers, and check Google Shopping before setting your price. [L0468.099]

- Sell at MAP and do not manipulate feed prices. For non-MAP suppliers, match a low competitor price only when profit remains, and avoid undercutting because price wars leave everyone with less profit. [L0469.071]

- With a strong review history, a store can sell above MAP and still win sales. A small discount such as 50 dollars off a multi-thousand dollar order can close the sale when buyers want peace of mind. [L0806.055]

- If a supplier does not enforce MAP, combine multiple units into bundles and advertise them as a combo product. Build a separate product page for each bundle. [L0852.054]

Before doubling prices on products from a supplier that does not enforce MAP, check other sellers' prices.
1. Search for the product online.
2. Note what other sellers charge.
3. If most sellers charge about 2x or more, the higher price can work.
4. If sellers charge only about 1.2x, the markup is not worth pursuing.
[L0853.035]

- If a supplier has no MAP and no margin, the store should not run ads to those products. Listing them at about double wholesale, without ads, keeps the store looking established. [L0855.046]

1. Search Google for the product from the supplier's brand to see the price competitors list.
2. If competitors list it at a price that gives enough margin, list your product at that higher price.
3. If competitors list it too low for a sufficient margin, keep the product on your store at a markup you accept.
4. Do not include that product in your paid ad spend; sales that come from free traffic still earn money without wasting ad budget.
[L0966.049]

Use this procedure to set a selling price when a supplier has no MAP:
1. Set the starting selling price at about double the wholesale cost.
2. Search for the same brand's products online to see what other retailers charge.
3. If other retailers sell at a higher multiple of wholesale, match that multiple instead of doubling automatically.
[L0967.065]

- In markets without MAP policies, such as Australia or the UK, price match guarantees should not be used. Set the sale price at least at double the wholesale cost. [L0967.067]

- Without a MAP or MSRP, set the selling price at 2x wholesale (selling price = wholesale × 2). Before listing, check competitor prices: if competitors sell above 2x, do not undercut them; if they sell below 2x, still list at minimum 2x and rely on free traffic instead of paid ads. Example: wholesale 500 gives a selling price of 1,000. [L0969.002]

## Deciding which products get ad spend

### Margin gate before any ad spend

- Paid traffic goes only to products where enough money remains after supplier cost. The core test is that cost of goods must be no more than half of MAP, so at least 50 percent of MAP is left after cost. [L0359.064]
- MAP must be at least 2X wholesale before a product is advertised. Older advice (2022): below that, sell the product only through free traffic, and give ads only to products with enough margin to cover ad spend. [L0963.065, L0429.045]
- Older advice (2021): if the gap between MAP and cost of goods is only about 30 percent, the product is usually not worth listing. Ask the supplier for a higher margin first, and expect to pass if the supplier refuses. [L0247.040]
- Older advice (2019): if the gap between wholesale and MAP is about 20 percent or less, keep the product on the site but run no paid traffic, because paid traffic makes profit almost impossible at that margin. [L0663.056]
- Older advice (2019): if margins after shipping are only about 10 to 15 percent, keep the product listed but stop advertising it. If competitors seem to advertise it profitably, ask your supplier for a different price sheet. [L0663.057]
- Products with margins under 10 percent, or below your goal (for example under 25 percent), do not need to stay on the store. Remove them, apply a higher markup (knowing they will likely not sell), or keep only the high-margin variants from that supplier. [L0253.039]
- Healthy margins are 25 to 30 percent or higher. [L0255.048]
- Check the spread before adding a product. If the gap between wholesale cost and MAP is too narrow to cover heavy-item freight and advertising, do not list it. [L0667.069]
- If the spread between MAP and wholesale is too small to cover shipping and ads, run no paid ads on that product. Rely on free traffic for it, or negotiate better wholesale prices first. [L0966.046]
- Older advice (2022): use the after-shipping spread of each product to decide where ad money goes. Push products with the most money left after shipping, and reduce spend on thin-margin products. [L0854.030]

**Quick reference**

| Signal | Action | Source ids |
|---|---|---|
| MAP at least 2X wholesale (cost at or below 50% of MAP) | Eligible for paid ads. Below this, free traffic only. | L0359.064, L0963.065, L0429.045 |
| Gap of about 30% between MAP and cost | Usually not worth listing. Ask for a higher margin; pass if refused. | L0247.040 |
| Gap of about 20% or less | Keep on site, no paid traffic | L0663.056 |
| 10 to 15% after shipping | Keep listed, stop advertising | L0663.057 |
| Under 10%, or below a 25% goal | Remove, raise price, or keep only high-margin variants | L0253.039 |
| About 9% after product cost and merchant fees | Remove or raise price; no room for ads | L1281.034, L1281.035 |
| 25 to 30% or higher | Healthy; prioritise ad budget by gross margin dollars | L0255.048 |

- The sources disagree on the 30 percent and 20 percent gaps. The 2021 advice would pass on a 30 percent gap when deciding whether to list a new product. The 2019 advice keeps a 20 percent gap product on the site. Both agree that the product gets no paid traffic. Read the 2021 rule as the test for new listings and the 2019 rule as the treatment for products already on the store. [L0247.040, L0663.056]
- Both the 30 percent gap and the 20 percent gap sit below the 50 percent line in the core test, so neither qualifies for ads. [L0359.064, L0247.040, L0663.056]

### Price and profit floors for paid traffic

- Older advice (2020): do not list products below a minimum profit of $200, even from brands from suppliers you already have approved. Focus selling only on the products from those suppliers that meet the profit threshold. [L0245.069]
- Do not spend on ads for products priced under $200. Exclude them from ad campaigns and use them only as upsells or cross-sells. [L0359.066]
- Filter products by net profit per sale, not by revenue or percentage margin alone. Products with a low dollar profit should be excluded from ad campaigns even if they sell well. [L0856.041]
- Choosing a minimum net profit threshold is a business decision. Use a fixed dollar amount of net profit per sale (for example $200 or $300) rather than a percentage, and base it on supplier margins and historical sales. The threshold is not universal. [L0856.040]
- A low-priced product (for example $100) with a moderate percentage margin still yields little profit per sale. Consider excluding products under a set price point from paid campaigns, unless they are add-ons. [L0856.042]
- Products priced below a set point (for example under $1,000) can be excluded from alpha and beta campaigns, so the budget goes to higher-value items. [L0856.043]
- Products priced between $3,000 and $5,000 are still eligible for paid ads when their margins meet the criteria. Measure conversion rates for these products on their own. [L0970.109]
- Older advice (2019): do not pay for traffic to low-price products that are too small to earn back ad costs. Keep them on a high-priced store as free bonuses instead. [L1002.035]
- Facebook scaling depends on profit per sale. Tests of offers, price, upsells and average order value raise profit. The product must earn enough margin that return on ad spend stays profitable even when it drops. [L0995.014]
- How to read the dollar figures: $200 is the floor in the sources, both as minimum profit for products from approved brands (older) and as the minimum price for ads. $300 and $1,000 are examples of stricter cut-offs for a store that has its own sales history. Set your own figure from supplier margins and past sales. [L0245.069, L0359.066, L0856.040, L0856.043]

### Ad spend cap as a share of MAP

- Spend up to 10 percent of MAP on paid ads per sale. For a $1,000 MAP, the maximum is $100 in ads per sale. [L0359.065]
- Older advice (2020): ad spend per sale should be capped at about 10 percent of MAP. For example, the spend limit on a $600 sale is $60. Anything higher leaves too little margin for operating costs. [L0967.071]
- Check the required return before scaling. With a 30 to 35 percent supplier discount and shipping still paid, reaching 20 percent profit required a ROAS of about 12 to 14, which is unrealistic for most stores. Fix margins in pricing or supplier terms before scaling paid traffic. [L0807.016]

### Organic-only products

- Products priced around $100 to $150, with a net profit of about $50, can go on an organic-only collection. Promote them through email and social promotions for events such as Black Friday or the 4th of July, instead of paid traffic. [L0359.067]
- Older advice (2020): a supplier promotion of 30 percent off is tricky for dropshipping, because little profit remains after shipping and ad costs. Sell such products through organic traffic, not paid ads. [L0551.055]
- Selling above MAP is reasonable only for products that receive organic traffic only. Never put paid ads behind products sold above MAP. (Older advice, 2021; this statement merges two identical units.) [L0671.050, L0671.189]
- For discounts on products that cannot be matched on price, do not advertise them. Keep them on the site only if they support the store, and spend the budget on products you can compete on. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0968.084]
- Exclude low-margin products from discounts, coupons and free-shipping promotions, and do not spend paid traffic on them. Rely on free traffic for those sales, and put the paid budget toward products with more profit. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0968.077]
- For low-margin products that are not profitable to advertise, follow this procedure:
  1. Still list the product on the store.
  2. Do not spend money on paid advertising for them.
  3. Rely on organic and free traffic.
  4. If someone finds and purchases organically, margin is still captured. [L0255.049]

### Google Shopping rules for low-ticket and add-on items

- Older advice (2022): for low-ticket items, do not spend money on Google Shopping campaigns. Include them in Google free product listings to get organic visibility without ad spend. [L0249.037]
- Older advice (2022): accessories sold individually should be removed from Google Shopping ad campaigns but kept in Google Merchant Center for free product listings. Charge shipping for items under a certain threshold (for example $100), rather than offering free shipping. [L0249.038]
- Older advice (2022): accessories priced above $200 can be tested in Google Shopping campaigns. Monitor them so they do not consume the budget meant for the more expensive core products, and lower cost-per-click for them if needed. [L0665.061]
- Read the two 2022 accessory rules together. Individually sold accessories come out of Shopping (L0249.038). The exception is accessories above $200, which may be tested under monitoring (L0665.061). [L0249.038, L0665.061]
- Older advice (2022): if a product's margins become too thin after paid ad costs, remove it from your Google Shopping product feed to avoid wasting budget. Keep it in your catalog for free organic traffic (Google organic search and non-paid Google Product Listings). [L0272.025]
- If competitor listings are priced differently across product tiers, first check whether the higher tier gets impressions, clicks and sales. If it does not, raise the CPC for that tier, or give each tier its own alpha and beta campaign with its own feed. [L0470.091]
- For the lowest-margin supplier's products, raise prices by 20 percent. Exclude those products from Shopping ads, so ad spend does not compete against sellers charging 20 percent less. Keep them on the store, where they add credibility and still sell if customers find them. [L1097.030]

### Campaign budgets and bids

- Older advice (2022): start new Google ad campaigns at $10 to $30 per day, not $50 to $100. Raise the budget step by step (for example $30 to $50, then $80 or $100) only when return on ad spend stays well above the target of at least 10x. [L0665.058]
- Older advice (2022): per-day Google ad budgets should follow profitability. A store on $70 per day should first prove profitability at $30, then at $50, then at $70. [L0665.059]
- If high-price products get no impressions while low-price products get heavy traffic, test lowering CPC on the cheap products (for example from 50 cents to 35 cents). Budget then reallocates naturally to the higher-price products. Alternatively, increase CPC on the high-price products. [L0274.020]
- Each product in a Google Shopping campaign should start with the same bid, for example $0.50 CPC. Change bids for each product individually once data shows which products have the best margins and sales. Do not change bids for all products at once. [L1114.050]
- Low-margin products that take heavy ad spend should get a lower CPC, such as $0.30. High-margin products that get clicks but not enough traffic should get a higher CPC, such as $0.75. This is an ongoing process of adjusting bids to find the most profitable level. [L1114.051]
- With healthy margins of 25 to 30 percent or higher, prioritise the paid advertising budget on products with the highest gross margin in dollar amounts, not just percentages, to maximise return on ad spend. [L0255.048]

### Excluding thin-margin products

- Upload only the products you want to sell from a supplier catalog. Skip those with too little margin, even if the supplier lists them. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0962.107]
- Do not list products that competitors sell at a loss, and do not list products whose margin is negative at MSRP. They are not worth the time. (Older advice, 2021; this statement merges two identical units.) [L0671.044, L0671.183]
- If a supplier requires an inquire-only button and no cart, do not list its products. Google Shopping ads need products that can be bought directly. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0671.196]
- Products whose competitor price is only slightly above wholesale cost, leaving no real profit, should not receive Google ad spend. (Older advice, 2019.) [L1069.048]
- Do not price-match a supplier's MAP products when the margin will not cover it. If ads cannot be afforded at that margin, rely on free traffic sources instead of cutting prices to break-even. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0853.034]
- Older advice (2021-22, Source B): when an order's net margin is very thin, you can raise the product price, start charging for shipping, slow down ad spend on that product, or remove the product from the store. Shipping costs can sometimes almost equal the product cost, so this situation is expected to appear from time to time. [L1201.012]

### MAP enforcement and non-MAP suppliers

- MAP enforcement by suppliers matters more than the margin figure alone. Products from MAP suppliers can be given ad budget. Non-MAP products should rely on organic traffic. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0429.079]
- Some brands set different MAP prices for stocking and non-stocking dealers. If you cannot match the required price, keep those products on the site without paid ads, and do not spend budget trying to compete on price. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0968.111]
- For suppliers without MAP enforcement, follow this procedure (older advice, 2022):
  1. Remove products that show only about 9 percent margin after product cost and fees, or raise their price until the margin is acceptable.
  2. Products with about 20 percent or more margin that you match against competitors can stay listed for organic and free Google traffic, but remove them from paid Shopping campaigns.
  3. Include in paid Shopping campaigns only the products with strong margins. [L1281.034]
- After product cost and merchant fees, the margin must still leave room for an ad budget. A product with a margin of about 9 percent has no room for ads at all. (Older advice, 2022.) [L1281.035]

### Negotiating supplier terms to improve margins

- Negotiating cost of goods with a supplier is a direct way to increase margins when a product's numbers do not work at the current price. [L0855.048]
- Wholesale pricing often has tiered structures based on sales volume. When contacting suppliers, ask whether they have different pricing tiers where you save on wholesale cost based on units sold or annual volume. This opens the negotiation without requiring you to commit or prove yourself first. [L0251.018]
- When you need a better price, ask for the exact price you want. Suppliers often do not expect the request and will frequently comply. Phrase any cancellation threat in a friendly, non-threatening way, since most suppliers also want to keep a profit. [L1472.025]
- Before offering discounts on products with thin margins, renegotiate the supplier pricing first. Discounting a product with a small margin does not fix the margin problem. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0960.036]
- Skip a supplier that offers only about a 10 percent discount after a minimum purchase, because that leaves too little margin for drop-shipping. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0469.074]
- For low-margin products that cannot carry an ad budget, follow this procedure:
  1. Ask the supplier for tiered or incentive wholesale pricing, framed as a request for better pricing on specific products rather than a complaint.
  2. Keep products with some margin in the store and list them in free Google product listings.
  3. Run ads only for products whose margin supports the ad cost. [L0470.075]

## Price testing and price changes

### Starting prices and launch

- Launch at a lower price, such as $49 or $59, to collect pixel data, and raise the price once the data gives a clear answer. [L1194.037]
- A low launch price can make a product look cheap, so the effect of price on the offer depends on the product. [L0871.040]
- Test a higher price before deciding that a product does not work. [L1428.018]
- Price that is not uncomfortable is probably too low. Raise it and measure the response before deciding otherwise. [L0378.017]
- A price that feels uncomfortably low can mean the offer is far below the value it delivers. A deliberately low entry price can be a good choice when the value is clearly higher. [L0378.018]
- Review prices at least once a year and raise them as results improve. Keeping the same price for a year is already too long. [L0378.019]
- Start with a small discount and raise prices step by step as reviews grow. This protects margin while building trust. [L0806.056]
- When competitors are much cheaper and you cannot match them, start with a competitive price to get sales. After you confirm profitable sales, invest in custom videos, images and testimonials, then raise the price. [L1217.062]
- If competitors list a product above your price, you may raise your price to match. Test the change for about a week and watch how conversions respond before keeping it. [L0470.076]

### Price validation phase

Procedure: pricing for a product validation test. [L0661.023]
1. Set the selling price close to the competitor price, for example 29.99 dollars, even if the margin is only about 10 dollars.
2. Use this low-margin phase only to check whether the product sells at all.
3. Once the product works, raise the price or lower costs until the margin after ad spend, taxes and transaction fees is about 20 dollars per sale.

### Testing prices down when nothing sells

Example: a product with fulfillment cost of about 20 USD was first sold at 39.99 USD without sales. The price was lowered to 34.99 USD to find out whether price was the blocker. [L0773.012, L0774.011]

Procedure: price testing sequence. [L0773.013, L0774.012]
1. Lower the price and check whether sales start. This tests whether price is the blocker.
2. If sales start at the lower price, lower the fulfillment cost so the product still earns a good margin at that price.
3. If there are still no sales at the lower price, the problem is more likely the landing page, the theme or another factor, not price.
4. If sales start, raise the price step by step (for example 34.99, 39.99, 44.99) and monitor the conversion rate and margin.
5. Keep the price that gives a good margin and the highest conversion rate.

Price tests should show whether price makes a difference. The best price is the one with a good margin and the highest conversion rate. [L0773.014]

### Testing prices up

- Test prices in both directions, including prices well above the current one. Price is one of the first things to test on any offer, even when changing it looks inconvenient. [L0378.020]
- Example: a product sold at $47 was changed to $97 with the same sales page and copy. Conversion stayed the same, so revenue doubled and profit rose sharply. [L0378.021]
- When conversion rate is good during ad runs, test higher prices, because upsells and bundles are where most profit is made. [L0440.042]
- Raising the price can increase profit without losing sales, and the perceived value of a product can rise with the price. [L0995.013]
- In one price test, a $15 increase produced a large improvement in return on ad spend and profit, while a $5 step had little effect. Larger increments worked where small ones did not, but results differ by product and audience. [L0995.013, L0999.017]
- Raising the price by a larger amount may be needed to see a difference. A small increase of about $5 may show no difference, so about $15 can be tested. [L0995.013, L0999.017]
- Raising prices can increase profit even if some customers leave, because the extra margin on the remaining sales can be larger than the lost volume. Test with an acceptable volume loss in mind rather than assuming the market will not accept the increase. [L1046.038] (Older advice, 2021 to 2022, Source B.)
- Raising the selling price while leaving ad spend unchanged usually fails, because the ads keep spending while sales drop, especially when many competitors sell the same product. Find other ways to add revenue, such as cross-sells, upgrades or geo-targeting, before trying a blanket price increase. [L0942.020]
- When inventory runs low and demand is high, raise the price to slow sales and preserve stock until the reorder arrives. This maintains momentum and profit without stockouts. [L0228.037]
- Price each design by its demand. Test prices upward until sales slow, then keep the highest price that still sells well for each design. [L0492.034]
- Example: a blanket design was tested from 50 to 60 to 70 and settled at about 70 to 80. Designs that sold well stayed at the higher price, while slower designs were priced lower and not reordered. [L0492.033]
- Pricing should be tested per product rather than always lowered. [L0388.007]
- Example: a home decor product sold at $19.90 had weak sales and was judged too cheap by people who would have bought it. After the price was raised to at least $30, sales started to come in. [L0388.007]
- Example: a home decor product at 19.90 USD drew visits but almost no sales. After the price was raised to 31.90 USD, sales started with a conversion rate of about 3 to 4 percent. [L1428.018]
- Value through branding, support and a guarantee can justify a higher price. Price testing matters because a low price can make a product look cheap or less valuable to buyers. [L0871.040]
- Example: a product started at $49 and was later sold at $90 by adding better branding and custom content, using the same underlying item. [L0788.049]
- Example: a product started at $49.95 and was later sold at $89.95 with no drop in sales pace after more investment in custom content, lifestyle images and the landing page. Raising price this way depends on perceived value rather than on undercutting competitors. [L0871.038]
- Example: a supplement launched at $22.95 and was increased to $28.95 after reviews had built up. The first increase, made when review count was low, caused a conversion drop. The same increase later succeeded once social proof was higher. Higher prices work when customer trust, shown by reviews, supports them. [L0227.026]

### Step sizes and increment rules

Procedure: price optimization on a performing offer. [L0233.020]
1. Confirm the offer is performing: high ad CTR and high store conversion rate with acceptable profit margin.
2. Increase the product price by €2 every 2 days.
3. Track conversion rate and profit margin closely after each price increase.
4. Continue daily price increases (€2 every 2 days) until conversion rate drops noticeably (typically 10-20% decline).
5. Stop increasing immediately when the conversion decline appears.
6. Return to the previous price point that achieved both the highest conversion rate and the highest profit margin.
7. Lock in that price as the baseline until offer components or market conditions change.

Procedure: stepwise increase on a working offer. [L0502.026]
1. Once the offer, the ads and the traffic all perform, raise the price in steps of 2 currency units (for example from 29.90 to 31.90).
2. Watch the conversion rate at each step.
3. Keep raising the price until buyers stop buying.
4. Return to the last price that gave the best combination of conversion rate, CTR and margin.
5. Price increases do not work for every product. For products where a higher price signals higher quality, it can raise conversion.

Case: a shoe product was tested last year with a starting price of 29.90. The price was increased in steps of about 2 until buyers stopped buying at 49.90, then lowered back. 44.90 gave the best conversion rate, the best CTR and the highest margin, about 15 more per sale than the starting price. [L0502.027]

Procedure: price increase testing method. [L0788.050]
1. Raise the price by $5 each time you add value, such as custom videos or better product images.
2. Make price increases about every two weeks.
3. Test each new price for about five days.
4. If sales stay consistent, keep the higher price.
5. If sales dip consistently after about one week, revert to the previous price and work on branding before raising prices again.

Procedure: test an increase on a product that is already selling. [L0871.039]
1. Test a price increase of about $5 to $10.
2. Wait about 3 to 4 days and check whether sales continue at a consistent rate.
3. If sales hold, keep the new price. If they drop, test again at a smaller step or improve the value first.
4. Repeat price testing as the product's branding and proof improve.

| Source | Step | Interval | Observation window | Stop or return rule | Context | IDs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Source C | €2 | Every 2 days | Track conversion and margin after each step | Stop at a noticeable conversion drop (typically 10-20%); return to the best point | Offer already performing (high CTR, high store conversion, acceptable margin) | L0233.020 |
| Source C | 2 currency units | Not stated | Conversion rate at each step | Keep raising until buyers stop; return to best combined conversion, CTR and margin | Offer, ads and traffic all perform; some products gain from higher price | L0502.026 |
| Source C | $5 | About every 2 weeks | About 5 days per price | Revert if sales dip consistently after about 1 week | Each time value is added | L0788.050 |
| Source C | $5 to $10 | Not stated | 3 to 4 days | If sales drop, test a smaller step or improve value first | Product already selling | L0871.039 |
| Source C | $5 may show nothing; test about $15 | Not stated | Not stated | Keep the larger step if it pays off | Price test, profit and return on ad spend | L0995.013, L0999.017 |
| Source C | $2 (AutoDS rule) | Per week (sales threshold of 20) | Weekly sales count | Mirror rule lowers price below 20 sales per week | Automated dynamic pricing; starting test values | L0477.007, L0477.008, L1297.006 |
| Source C | 34.99, 39.99, 44.99 | Not stated | Conversion rate and margin | Keep the price with a good margin and highest conversion | Stepping up after a lower price started sales | L0773.013, L0774.012 |

### Price and offer A/B tests

- Price tests should show whether price makes a difference. Test with a clear reading of conversion rate and margin for each price. [L0773.014]
- Price is one of the first elements to test on any offer. [L0378.020]
- Value changes such as custom content or better images can come before a price rise, so each change is tested against the price it supports. [L0788.050, L0871.039]

### Per-market price tests

- The price A/B test is run separately for each market: UK, Australia and US. It is not run as one global test. [L1288.012]
- Example: a product auto-converted to 50 AUD converted poorly. After the Australian price was set to 49.90 AUD, the conversion rate went up. Set a separate price on every market, because automatic conversion often produces a round number that does not convert well. [L1316.003]
- Lomo (also written Lo Meo) is a paid tool for dynamic price testing that changes prices per country and currency. It offers a seven-day free trial, which allows a seven-day price test across markets. [L0995.012, L0999.019]
- Google Optimize probably cannot change prices dynamically, so a fixed price test would be needed for each country. [L0995.012]
- Google Optimize cannot change prices dynamically per currency, so it is better for testing headlines or static elements. [L0999.019]

### Pricing automation

Procedure: setting an automatic pricing rule in AutoDS. [L1297.006, L0477.007]
1. Open Automations at the top and click Add automation.
2. Name it automatic pricing and click Next.
3. Keep 'raise price by sales performance' on.
4. Set the condition: number of sales higher than 20 per week, then increase the price by $2.
5. Add the mirror rule: number of sales lower than 20 per week, then decrease the price by $2.
6. Click Next, select the product, and click Finish.

The aim is to find the highest price where sales start to fall, since that price gives the best margin. [L0477.007, L1297.006]

Dynamic pricing through AutoDS is meant to find the price point where sales start to fall, so the highest price that still sells can be identified. The 20-orders-per-week threshold and the $2 step are the starting test values shown in the lesson, not universal rules. [L0477.008]

### Price changes visible to customers

- A rich product pin pulls the price dynamically from the store. Lowering the price, for example during a discount, sends a price-change notification to every user who pinned that product. The store owner does not need to do anything extra to trigger it. [L1136.011]
- Prices are updated only when a supplier changes its MAP policy. Store prices are not reviewed continuously against the market, and discounts do not change this rule. [L1090.077] (Older advice, 2021, Source A.)
- Small price changes, such as $19.97 to $21.97, may not change buyer behavior but still improve profit. Check the margin effect of each price step before changing a price. [L1153.028]

### Announcing price rises

- Announce a price increase with a fixed cut-off date. State that orders placed before the date get the current price and later orders pay the new price. Give both figures in the letter so the reader can compare them. [L1369.013]
- Announce a known future cost increase with its reason and date, such as paper, printing and binding costs that had more than doubled. Buyers respond to a believable cost reason more readily than to an unexplained price rise. [L1369.014]
- When announcing a price rise to existing customers, hold one or two units at the old price for them and state the exact percentage increase. Explain the reason for the rise so the exception looks believable. A specific limited hold works better than a general loyalty claim. [L1369.113]
- Before a known supplier price increase, announce a dated last-chance promotion. Sync it with an email campaign, social posts and a countdown on product pages until the new prices take effect. [L1112.069] (Older advice, 2021, Source A.)

### Protecting buyers after a price drop

- Avoid the situation where customers feel that a product they just bought is now cheaper. After a large order, if the product goes on sale within one or two weeks, send the buyer a free product instead of a discount, to protect trust and repeat purchases. [L0334.022]

### Raising prices after validation

- Raising the product price by $5 (from $44 to $49) during the scaling phase lifted the margin of the product. [L1436.049]
- A product price was raised by $5, from $44 to $49, and the store's margin improved while sales continued. Price changes combined with post-purchase extras were one of the steps used to improve profitability. [L1382.020]
- Check the margin of each price step before and after the change, not only the sales count. [L1153.028, L0502.026]
- Review prices at least once a year and raise them as results improve. [L0378.019]

### Relaunching after a failed increase

Procedure: if raising the price causes sales to drop sharply. [L1215.007]
1. Relaunch the product with new campaigns, a new ad account or a new pixel before deciding.
2. If it still does not sell profitably at the higher price, stop selling it.
3. Do not keep a product running at a loss in the hope that the price can be raised later.

The first increase in the supplement example failed at low review count and the same increase later succeeded once social proof was higher, so timing the increase against trust matters. [L0227.026]

### Sanity-checking a price with outsiders

- Ask a few people outside your team whether a price feels credible for the product. Their reaction is a cheap first signal before running paid tests. [L0388.008]

## Discounts and coupon codes

### Discount types

- Percentage off is the default offer type for most stores. [L1404.016]
- Percentage and dollar-off are close to equivalent in effect; the Source E test found little difference. A common rule of thumb is dollar-off when the discount is over $100 and percentage-off below that, and mixing both is acceptable. [L1404.014]
- Percentage is the usual choice in Shopify. A code such as save 10 would be set to 10%. [L1322.005]
- Fixed-dollar discounts are easier for shoppers to picture than percentages. Use a dollar amount for pop-up and email codes where possible, and compare it against a percentage offer if conversion stalls. [L0476.004]
- Dollar-off offers such as "Save $20," "Save $30 on first orders," or "Save up to $80 sitewide" work best for higher-AOV products or when bundling multiple items. [L0232.003]
- Discount scopes to choose from:
  - Site-wide: one percentage on all products. [L1404.015]
  - Specific SKUs: a set percentage or dollar amount on selected products. [L1404.015]
  - Tiered: for example, spend $100, then get 20% off the amount after that. [L1404.015]
- Spend-threshold discount: spend 1,000 USD and save 100 USD on any order over 1,000 USD. This is simpler to understand than a percentage discount with a dollar cap. [L1090.081]
- Spend-and-save example for a homebrew equipment store in January: during the first week, orders over $500 save $50, and orders over $1,000 save $75. Spend thresholds can be varied freely to match the store's average order size. [L1205.006]
- Tiered discounts, such as 5% on orders of $250 or more and 10% on orders of $500 or more, are built by creating separate Shopify discount codes and setting a minimum order amount on each. *Older (2021).* [L0671.054, L0671.193]
- Tiered spend discount used for Black Friday promotions *(older, 2021)*:

| Order spend | Discount |
|---|---|
| 400 dollars or more | 15% off |
| 800 dollars or more | 20% off |
| 1,200 dollars or more | 25% off |

  [L0801.051]
- Low-value tiered coupons reward larger orders, for example $10 off at $500 spend and $20 off at $1,000 spend. Choose tier values based on what competitors offer in your niche. [L1357.008]
- Use a discount code rather than an automatic discount when the offer should be targeted. A code requires the shopper to enter it at checkout, while an automatic discount applies to every cart. [L1322.003]
- An automatic site-wide discount of 10% was active on the example product page. Compare an automatic discount against a coupon code with a deadline when setting up your own offer. [L0655.017]
- A monetary percentage promotion is a fixed percent off (for example 5% or 10%) for a set time, applied when the customer enters a coupon code. It can be shown store-wide, but it should be sent specifically to the audience that has engaged but not bought. [L1205.005]
- Time-limited discount codes create urgency. They can be offered as a reward for joining the email list, shown in an exit-intent popup when a visitor is about to leave, or displayed with a countdown timer for new customers. [L1400.005]
- Limited-time discount or coupon codes use urgency to push buyers to purchase sooner. They can be given to new email subscribers or shown in an exit-intent popup as the visitor is about to leave. [L1399.007]

### Discount sizes

- Starting point for testing: a 5% order discount. You can create as many discount codes with different values as you need. [L1192.007]
- Coupon codes: offer 5-10%. 10% is competitive because most competitors either do not promote discounts or offer less than 10%. [L0137.002]
- Expiring coupon percentages: the typical levels used are 5%, 7% or 10% off, and this depends on the store. [L0531.005]
- Competitor first-order discount: about 10% in exchange for an email signup. *Competitor example, not dated.* [L0600.020]
- Welcome kit for newsletter sign-ups: usually 5-10%, which customers see as the usual reward. [L0605.029]
- Typical sign-up incentives are a 5% or 10% discount, or a fixed $5 discount. A 10% first-order discount is the standard used in this setup. [L0607.007]
- Volume offers: typically 20 to 50 percent. Discounts of 10 percent or 5 percent are seen as weak by the audience. [L1259.029]
- Bundles: 15% is a reasonable starting point, but it must fit the margin. [L1259.033]
- Ceiling for strong offers: a maximum discount of 70 percent off, with 50 percent off as the usual standard. [L1436.042, L1382.017]
- Launch discount: 50% off to generate rapid sales. [L0227.027]

### Sizing discounts to margin

- Calculate margin after coupons, not before them. If net margin after discounts is only 12 to 15 percent, the current discount structure should be changed. [L1097.027]
- Only offer discounts on your site if you can afford them. [L0966.053]
- Before offering a first-order discount such as 5% or 10% off, check on the pricing sheet that gross profit stays positive at that discount. A small percentage can turn a product with thin margin into a loss. *Older (2021-22).* [L1148.026]
- Effect of discounts on Product A (list price $1,000, supplier cost $600, supplier shipping $150, free customer shipping; gross figures before processor and insurance fees):

| Discount on list price | Price charged | Gross profit | Gross margin |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0% | $1,000 | $250 | 25.00% |
| 5% | $950 | $200 | 21.05% |
| 10% | $900 | $150 | 16.67% |
| 15% | $850 | $100 | 11.76% |

  *Older (2021-22).* [L1148.018]
- A 5% discount on a product with $1,000 net profit (for example, a $50 discount) remains profitable and is worth offering if it increases conversion rate. [L0253.011]
- A coupon such as 100 USD off orders over 1,000 USD can be offset by raising prices slightly, but if competitors undercut you, a smaller incentive such as 50 USD off orders over 1,000 USD is the safer option. [L0966.052]
- When margins are too thin, a sitewide discount of 10 percent off all orders can be lowered to 5 percent off all orders. This keeps a discount on the site while protecting margin on each order. [L1097.028]
- Replace a percentage-off code with a minimum-spend code, such as $50 off orders over $1,000, or $100 off orders over $2,000 for higher-priced stores. Create the rule in Shopify's discount codes so it applies automatically. This raises average order value while giving less discount per order. [L1097.029]
- Set eligibility by profit, not by a blanket rule. Example: 20 percent off orders over 250 dollars requires any starter kit, while free expedited shipping requires a premium kit. [L1204.041]
- Bundle headline math: a bundle is advertised as access to its listed tools at one tenth of their list price, and its title frames this as 95% off. If the extra 10% code applies to the bundle price, the total discount is about 91% off list, not 95%. Compare the final checkout total with the list prices of the tools you would actually buy, not with the headline percentage. [L1387.013]
- Discounts go straight to lost profit. Limit discounts or stop offering them entirely. Some stores have kept sales volume while refusing negotiated discounts, so the effect on orders can be tested. *Older (2021-22).* [L1046.039]
- Do not hand out irresistible deals without a reason. Offering a discount too early gives away margin to people who would have bought anyway, and it can also weaken the urgency of future deals. *Older (2021-22).* [L0799.020]
- Do not run a standing discount on product pages as a permanent sales tactic, especially when margins are thin. A permanent 5% discount erodes margin without making the offer more convincing. *Older (2021-22).* [L0409.028]
