- The ebook should read as a thoughtful gift, not a sales pitch. Use a warm, direct voice, short paragraphs, subheadings, and bullet points where they help readability. [L0860.014]
- Filler chapters and placeholder text make the bonus look cheap and weaken the offer. Every chapter should be useful on its own. [L0860.015]
- Ebook bonus titles should match the product's buyer. For a beauty product, a skincare guide such as "Five-Step Skincare Routine For Dry Skin" fits. For a posture product, a habits guide such as "6 Daily Habits For Sitting Taller At Work" fits. [L0860.030]
- For a book seller, a good free gift is a short e-book that teaches the reader how to create an e-book, offered as a bonus on the Buy 2 and Buy 3 bundles. [L1149.009]
- Make the ebook about how to properly use or optimise the main product. This justifies higher bundle prices and increases the perceived value of the tier. [L0232.027]
- In a free-gift ebook, place a short tracker or overview of the whole challenge, such as a 7-day reset map, before the day-by-day breakdown. The reader knows the full path before the detailed steps begin. [L0862.039]
- Example of a challenge ebook step: before bed, take a small flashlight and a small mirror. Spend about 30 seconds on three checks: the gumline where teeth meet gums, the back of the tongue for any white or yellow coating, and the gaps between the back teeth. Note one simple observation only, without changing your routine. [L0862.040]
- A short usage guide for a product that buyers may ask about can be made as follows. [L1432.043]
  1. Find a store that sells the same product and has a strong product description.
  2. Use that description to understand the product's features and how it is used.
  3. Write and design a PDF of three to four pages that explains how to use the product.
  4. Deliver the PDF to buyers automatically, with a delivery app or a download link in the order confirmation.
  5. Prefer creating the guide yourself over buying Amazon seller material to reuse, since your own version is safer and more specific. [L1432.043]
- Adding a small, unannounced free gift to the parcel costs almost nothing if it is printed cheaply, for example three items per A4 sheet in black and white with a simple recipe. Customers who receive such a surprise return more often, refer others, and leave better feedback. [L1437.005]

#### Sourcing and rebranding ebooks

- Ebook topics that buyers can use: a free ebook such as a resistance band exercise guide, an ab workout guide, or a 15, 30, or 60 minute workout guide. A resistance band seller added a mesh bag to solve an odour complaint and included three free ebooks. [L1153.031]
- Buy a PLR (private label rights) ebook on a health-related topic from a site such as plr.me, typically around $5 to $10 or less. [L0871.047]
- A free-ebook sourcing procedure is set out below. [L0650.068]
  1. Choose a topic related to your product or your customer's needs, such as wellness or a product-use subject.
  2. Search that topic on a free-ebook website and download a matching ebook.
  3. Hire a freelancer on Fiverr to change the cover and branding to match your store.
  4. Offer the rebranded ebook as a free bonus with a purchase, and do not sell it directly. [L0650.068]
- A free ebook rebranded with a new cover and logo can be offered as a bonus. It should not be sold directly because of copyright concerns. The lesson describes this as a gray area and recommends the rebranding. [L0650.041]
- Disagreement on selling rebranded PLR ebooks: one lesson says the ebook can be edited in Canva and kept as delivered, since the rights allow reuse, and then either added as a bonus or sold as a product on the site. [L0871.047] Another says rebranded ebooks should not be sold directly because of copyright concerns and should be offered only as a free bonus. [L0650.041, L0650.068] Use it as a free bonus in every case. Sell it only if the PLR licence you bought explicitly allows resale.
- A cheap-upsell strategy with a free ebook as the add-on follows these steps. [L0577.011]
  1. Search the supplier marketplace (AliExpress example) for a product related to your main product. Filter by reviews and find an add-on that costs between 10 cents and 1 dollar.
  2. Offer the add-on for free on the store, either beside the main product or as a free bonus when customers buy.
  3. Find a PLR ebook related to the product's benefit (for example, skin care for sensitive skin for a shower-filter product) on a PLR site.
  4. Buy the PLR ebook for about 2 dollars 70 cents, rebrand it with your own logo, and give it to customers as a downloadable bonus.
  5. State the ebook's value as 20 dollars in the offer, for example "Receive a 20 dollar ebook free with your order."
  6. Make the offer very visible on the site (banner, dropdown, or product gallery image as second image).
  7. Set up the add-on as a variant of the main product's Shopify listing, and use an automation app (Auto DS was named) so the supplier receives orders for both items together.
  8. Tell customers in the order confirmation email that the items may arrive on different days and not together. [L0577.011]
- A custom add-on packaging made specifically for a product can be expensive: if it must be manufactured separately, suppliers often require a minimum of 500 to 1,000 units. Only use custom add-ons if the supplier already has the item in stock. [L0577.017]

#### Ebook prompts

Prompt for a finished ebook with a product and avatar placeholder (used by Source D):

```
You are a seasoned ebook author and content planner. Your job is to produce a finished, top-tier ebook of 15 to 25 pages meant as a complimentary gift for [YOUR PRODUCT AND AVATAR HERE]. Swap the bracketed placeholder for a specific product and buyer, such as: a mouthpiece against snoring for grown-ups; a training collar for people who just got their first dog; a posture brace for people who sit at a desk all day. Pick a subject that almost everyone can use and that appeals widely (productivity, mindset, health routines, money fundamentals, or another timeless theme) and that ties into the product and buyer named above. What counts most is how well it is carried out rather than the subject; the book should show what a polished complimentary ebook looks like in real use. Write the entire ebook from beginning to end, covering: an engaging title with a subtitle; a short welcome note or introduction from the author; four to six chapters containing meaningful, truly helpful material, with no filler placeholders; practical takeaways or exercises where they fit; and a final section that points to one clear next step or call to action. Benchmarks: a warm, straightforward, engaging tone that is never stiff or boilerplate; brief paragraphs, subheadings, and bullet lists wherever they make reading easier; each chapter gives genuine value rather than padding; the whole piece reads as a considerate present, not a sales pitch. Mentees will study this ebook as a model, so the execution has to be refined enough to demonstrate professional standards in structure, tone, rhythm, and how the material leads up to the call to action. Deliver the complete ebook text, formatted so it can be pasted directly into a design program or document.
```
[L0860.016]

```
You are a professional ebook author and content strategist. Your assignment is to write a full, high-grade ebook of 15 to 25 pages intended as a free gift for [YOUR PRODUCT AND AVATAR HERE]. Pick a broadly useful, widely relevant subject (such as productivity, mindset, health habits, financial basics, or another timeless topic). Quality of execution matters more than the subject: the ebook should model what a well-made free gift ebook looks like in practice. Link the chapters to the problem that [YOUR PRODUCT AND AVATAR HERE] solves, so the ebook backs up the product without directly promoting it. Write the complete ebook from start to finish, including: an engaging title with a subtitle; a brief welcome message or author introduction; four to six chapters with substantive, truly useful content and no placeholder text; actionable takeaways or exercises where suitable; and a closing section with one clear next step or call to action. Tone should be friendly, straightforward, and lively, never rigid or bland. Keep paragraphs short and use subheadings and bullet points where they help readers. Each chapter needs to provide actual usefulness, not padding. Overall, it should come across as a considerate present rather than a pitch. Because mentees will use this ebook as a benchmark, the craftsmanship must be strong enough to show professional-level structure, voice, pacing, and the way the content leads to the call to action. Output the complete ebook text in a form that can be pasted straight into a design application or document.
```
[L0860.029]

```
Act as a seasoned ebook author and content strategist. Produce a finished, high-calibre ebook of 15 to 25 pages that works as a free gift for [TARGET CUSTOMER, who they are] who [PROBLEM THE PRODUCT SOLVES, e.g. wants a healthier oral microbiome with simple daily habits]. Pick a broadly useful topic that speaks to this problem, such as productivity, mindset, health routines, money basics, or another timeless subject. The subject matters less than how well it is executed: the result should show what a strong free-gift ebook looks like in practice. Deliver the entire ebook from start to finish, containing: an attention-grabbing title and subtitle; a short welcome note or author introduction; 4 to 6 chapters with meaningful, practical material (no placeholder filler); actionable takeaways or exercises where they fit; and a closing section with one clear next step or call to action. Quality bar: a warm, direct, engaging voice that never sounds stiff or generic; short paragraphs, subheadings and bullet lists where they improve readability; each chapter gives real value rather than padding; the whole thing reads like a thoughtful gift, not a sales pitch. Name [PRODUCT NAME] only in the closing section, presented as support for the habits rather than a fix or cure. Use [CALL TO ACTION, e.g. Take 15% off your first order or Learn how it fits into your routine] as the call-to-action placeholder and invent no link. Include a brief closing note stating that the guide is for educational purposes and is no substitute for advice from a qualified professional. Output the full ebook text, not a summary.
```
[L0861.029]

Ebook-to-HTML prompt (used after the text is generated):

```
Turn this into a complete e-book in HTML. Make it look polished and stylish. The product name is [PRODUCT NAME]. Disregard the CTA link and keep every piece of text exactly as it is; only modify the layout and styling. Apply the brand colours [BRAND COLORS] to headings, highlights and dividers, use a refined serif for headings and a sans-serif for body copy, and ensure the layout is fully responsive down to mobile screens. Format each section to fit A4 pages, inserting page breaks between sections. Deliver one self-contained HTML file.
```
[L0861.030]

PDF prompt (used after the HTML version):

```
Make this a PDF now, please. Use A4 page size, keep the page breaks between sections and keep the layout from the HTML version unchanged. If the output is cut off or incomplete, ask the assistant to regenerate the full PDF instead of accepting the partial file.
```
[L0861.031]

Free-gift ebook prompt for beginner mentees (the source says to paste it into claude.ai and add any missing piece from the original lesson text):

Ask Claude (claude.ai) to write a complete free-gift ebook of 15 to 25 pages. Paste the prompt in, and if a piece is missing, add it from the original lesson text. The ebook is for beginner mentees who are learning to make this kind of ebook. It should work as a working model: readers can study it, take it apart, and copy its structure for their own projects. Pick a practical, broadly appealing, evergreen topic such as productivity, mindset, health habits, or basic personal finance. The topic matters less than how well it is executed, because the point is to show what a strong free-gift ebook looks like in practice. The output must contain everything from start to finish: a catchy title and subtitle, a short welcome or author introduction, 4 to 6 chapters of substantial, truly useful material with no placeholder text, practical takeaways or exercises where they fit, and a closing section that gives one clear next step or call to action. Quality standard: the tone is friendly, direct, and engaging, never stiff or generic. The source excerpt stops after that standard, so the remaining quality standards come from the original lesson text and should be carried over with the same meaning.

Suggest 10 free ebook ideas that help the buyer get the most out of this product, including how to use it, how to style or wear it, how to store it, how to clean it and which situations to use it in. For each idea, give a title and a short description of the content. Then outline the chapters of the best idea so I can write the ebook and add product photos.

[L0502.017]

- Turn the ebook into a delivered product with this procedure. [L0861.032]
  1. Copy the ebook prompt and replace the audience sentence with your target customer and the problem your product solves.
  2. Replace the CTA placeholder with your own call to action or leave it as a placeholder.
  3. Generate the full ebook text in an AI chat.
  4. Send the HTML prompt with your product name and brand colors, and keep the text unchanged.
  5. Send the PDF prompt and check that the output is complete.
  6. Download the PDF to your computer.
  7. Install the Digital Downloads app and open the ebook product.
  8. Click Add asset, then File, and upload the PDF.
  9. Tick Not a physical product in the product's Shipping section and save.
  10. Attach the ebook product as the free gift in the bundle and confirm it is available.
  11. Read the full ebook text before publishing, since AI output can contain errors. [L0861.032]

### Tiered and selectable gifts

- Tiered gifts make the buyer add more items. Each extra item unlocks an additional free gift, up to a set maximum. Removing an item removes the gift, which uses loss aversion to encourage larger orders. [L0919.021]
- Example: a tiered gift offer in the cart unlocked three gifts as the buyer added bottles: a recipe book, a mystery gift, and a milk frother. Removing a bottle removed the matching gift. Gift tiers were tied to quantity and capped at a set number. [L0919.046]
- Example: a necklace offer and selectable gifts with a choice of design, as above. [L0661.027]
- Include a card that new customers return with their order to claim a gift, and give existing customers a different reason to receive it. The gift helped the catalogue beat competing catalogues and paid for itself several times through larger orders. [L1261.159]
- Free gift price guidance: a base product at $45 with a free gift gave a total perceived price of around $60, which was a good sweet spot. A total of $80 to $100 was harder to sell because buyers did not see enough value. [L1315.030]
- Example: a free gift that costs about $2 was added to a product sold at $59.99 instead of $60, which skews the perceived value upward. This was the most profitable offer for that store. The gift must still be relevant to the customer and must not eat too much of the margin. [L0995.021, L0999.016]
- Bundle-stacking with value display, where the bonus is shown at its dollar value: offer the main product plus a free bonus item, show the bonus's original retail price in parentheses (for example "$19.99 value"), and add urgency language. This works because the free item increases perceived bundle value at minimal cost, and the specific dollar value makes the gift tangible. [L0313.004]
  - Example one: "FREE meal planning guide ($24.99 value) with every 90-day nutrition package."
  - Example two: "FREE stretching routine video ($14.99 value) with every recovery device-today only." [L0313.004]
- Example: a posture corrector bundle with a free gift. The free "Posture Fix Guide" was displayed at a $19.99 value and included with the corrector purchase. The ad used "limited time only" and "today only" language. The free guide was a low-cost value-add that increased perceived bundle worth without significant cost to the seller. [L0313.014]

### Presenting gifts on pages

- Show the free gift clearly on the product page and in the cart, with a headline, an image, and a claim message after adding to cart. Customers who do not know about the gift may not add the product. [L1315.032]
- Add free bonus items, such as a digital cheat sheet or e-book, as a visible line near Add to Cart and in the bundle block. State the bonus's stated value when it has one. Only promise a bonus that is actually delivered automatically after checkout. [L1188.018]
- Place an image labelled with the gift and its value under the product description on the product page. Use wording such as "free gift with your order, 39 USD value." [L0963.068]
- Bonuses should be shown on the product page as free gifts, not hidden behind extra steps. Do not make customers call or take extra steps to receive an offer, such as a gift voucher for large orders, because extra steps cause distractions and lost sales. [L1110.030] *(Source A, 2019: older advice.)*
- Show the bonus on the landing page as part of the offer, next to the main product. [L0502.011]
- Make the offer very visible on the site with a banner, a dropdown, or a product gallery image as the second image. [L0577.011]
- Present a bonus-offer pattern on an expensive product page: "If you buy [expensive product], you get [free item] included." The customer sees the bonus as added value to a purchase they already want. Two examples: "Buy the 4-seat sauna and get a free thermometer kit included." and "Order the commercial espresso machine and receive a free descaling set with it." [L1002.034] *(Source A, 2019: older advice.)*
- Threshold gift pattern: "spend over [threshold] before [date] and get a bonus [gift] valued around [amount]." It works because buyers already plan to spend the amount, and the gift feels like extra value even when it has nothing to do with the store. [L1113.078] *(Source A, 2022: older advice.)*
  - Example one: "Order over [threshold] this [holiday] and receive a free [gift] shipped with your order."
  - Example two: "Shop before [date] with over [threshold] in your cart and we add a bonus [gift] at no cost." [L1113.078]
- Gift-with-purchase promotion: offer a free trending product with any order placed before a deadline, such as 11:59 pm on Cyber Monday. It works even in niches that are not gift products, because buyers shopping for the main product also consider the bonus item as a present for someone else. [L1111.090] *(Source A, 2020: older advice.)*
- Offer the gift as a bundle choice in the bundle block: the main product with the free bonus and the stated value shown at the bundle price. [L0313.004]
- Bundle presentation: list the offer as separate components stacked on the main product, not as one item. [L0683.007]
- Bonus stacking for a 59.99 USD product: show the old price 119.99 USD next to the new price 59.99 USD, list a free ebook worth 19.99 USD, free shipping instead of 9.99 USD, and 24/7 customer service. Add a second unit for 39.99 USD instead of 59.99 USD to raise average order value. [L0683.008]
- Each bonus should be given a stated value, such as the price it would cost on its own. Customers then see the total package as worth more than the price. [L0683.009]
- Lead magnet pattern: keep the same product and the same price, and add a small free gift that matches the buyer's interest to raise conversion rate. Rotate gifts every two days so each gift gets a fair test. Example: a sold-out-style fitness band at the same price with a free water bottle; a kitchen knife with a free cutting board. [L0773.015]
- Bonus items can be presented as the "premium" in a sales letter, with the premium's price or worth named in the letter. [L1369.016]
- Free gifts should be placed where the buyer sees them before deciding; the same bonus that is hidden in the footer does not increase conversion. [L1315.032]
- Add a free ebook with the shape of the offer: "Receive a 20 dollar ebook free with your order." [L0577.011]
- Show the gift image in the offer image, so the buyer knows exactly what they get. [L1363.059]
- Bonus delivered after purchase must be listed truthfully on the offer, so the buyer knows the bonus will arrive. [L1188.018]

### Fulfilment of gifts

#### Same-warehouse and separate shipments

- Fulfilment depends on where the gift ships from. [L0358.033]
  1. If the free gift ships from the same warehouse as the main product, bundle the gift into the product on your store so the supplier ships both together.
  2. If the free gift ships separately or from another warehouse, set up an automation in Zapier.
  3. Set the Zapier trigger to fire when a new order is placed with an order value above the threshold, for example above $2,000.
  4. Set the action to send an email to the fulfilment contact with the bonus gift and the customer's shipping address. [L0358.033] *(Source A, 2020: older advice.)*
- Gift shipped separately from the main order: a store bought the gift directly from the brand and shipped it separately, with the business address as return address. The email list and ads promoted it. [L1113.079] *(Source A, 2022: older advice.)*
- Gift-with-purchase fulfilment when a low-cost gift is added to a high-ticket order: [L1223.032]
  1. Pick a gift the buyer will value (a popular consumer electronic item unrelated to the store's products) and check that its cost fits the margin on the main sale.
  2. When a customer completes a qualifying order, take the customer's shipping address from the order.
  3. Order the gift from the retailer and enter the customer's address as the delivery address, so the gift ships straight to the buyer.
  4. Let the buyer receive the gift as extra value on top of the main purchase. [L1223.032]
- Gift-with-purchase value: a low-cost gift with purchase can drive pre-Christmas orders. The lesson's example was a roughly $80 iPod shipped to the buyer's address on top of a high-ticket purchase. The buyer sees the gift as extra value, and the seller pays only the gift and shipping cost. The gift should be desirable to the buyer and worth it to the seller at the chosen spend level. [L1223.016]
- Check regularly that every order includes the free gift. A free gift that is not added to some orders creates refund and support problems. [L1173.023]
- Thresholds such as $2,000 apply to the trigger for the Zapier automation step. [L0358.033]

#### Dropship SKUs and automation

- Setting up a free-gift or buy-one-get-one offer with a dedicated supplier SKU: [L1428.020]
  1. Ask the supplier, for example on CJ, to create a variant that combines two items under one SKU.
  2. On the supplier's product page, choose the two-item variant. The total dropshipping price is shown at the bottom.
  3. Copy the SKU number of that variant.
  4. Add the variant to your store as the offer.
  5. Check that your margin covers the cost of two items before setting the price.
  With this setup the supplier ships both items on each order without any app. [L1428.020]
- A temporary free-gift offer can use an automatic website discount instead, for example one free item when two are added to the cart. The supplier then ships each item separately, so the supplier must know the two items to ship. A permanent offer is better served by a dedicated SKU, because the supplier ships it automatically. [L1428.021]
- Bonus setup steps: [L1293.033]
  1. List your top three bestsellers and the high-margin products you want to sell.
  2. For each, identify the accessory the buyer would normally need.
  3. Check the supplier's price list for cheap items you have not added to your store.
  4. Add the bonus to the supplier order confirmation, or to the SKU, so it ships with each order.
  5. Offer the bonus as a free gift once the purchase reaches the threshold. [L1293.033]

#### Shipping and compliance

- Customers may receive the add-on and the main product on different days, so tell them in the order confirmation email. [L0577.011]
- Free packaging, shipping, or extras that are not in the supplier's standard shipment need to be planned before launch so the order can be fulfilled. [L1173.023]

### Digital versus physical performance

- Physical product bonuses have historically delivered stronger conversion lift than digital bonuses. [L0314.001] *(Source A; this is the source's general view.)*
- Digital bonuses underperform physical bonuses in conversion lift, based on practical experience. [L0314.009]
- In one store, a digital bonus was tested as extra value but did not beat a relevant physical free gift. Prefer a physical gift that is closely related to the main product and its customer problem. [L1315.062]
- Physical bonuses can change with the purchase month. Each monthly window gives a different item that fits the season, such as a summer ingredient pack in one month and an autumn pack in the next. Buyers get a new reason to act, and the bonus is something they would probably buy anyway. [L1204.071]
- Physical bonus case: a merch store ran a five-day promotion in which every order over $50 received a free physical book. The offer raised average order value and turned old leads into customers. The bonus was given without any upsell and without extra charge. [L1205.014]
- Test different bonus types and combinations with different products over time. Do not expect to find the perfect bonus strategy immediately. Continuously measure which bonuses increase conversion and refine based on sales data. [L0314.017]
- Test whether the gift matches what the target buyer wants, and compare a physical gift with a digital gift on the same offer. [L1173.024, L0314.011]
- In one store owner's experience, adding a bonus gift worth about $20 to the buyer tripled the conversion rate for the specific products it was attached to. The effect was tested on products the owner was already selling, not on new listings. [L1293.035]
- The $20 bonus strategy: give a low-cost item with each purchase to convert buyers who are comparing with Amazon and competitors. The formula is a product-specific bonus that the buyer would normally need anyway, worth about $20 to the buyer but cheap to the seller. A $50 to $100 margin product still earns up to $80 after the bonus. The reason: it removes the need to compete on price and makes the offer more valuable than the competitor's. [L1293.025]
- Scuba mask example: the bonus is a snorkel (wholesale about $5 to $10), offered free with any mask over $99. A podcast microphone can be bundled similarly with a stand, pop filter, and carrier case. A coffee maker example: a standard AeroPress versus a bundle that includes a reusable metal filter costing well under $1 to make, which the speaker chose over the competitor. [L1293.028]
- Free gift value comparisons: a free gift with high perceived value often moves more customers than a slightly larger money-off discount. [L1112.068] *(Source A, 2021: older advice.)*
- Premium and bonus guidance from Source A 2022: free gifts add perceived value without proportional cost. A guide valued at $19.99 costs the seller almost nothing to create, but the stated dollar value anchors customer perception of bundle worth. This is more effective than reducing the main product price by the same amount. [L0313.024]
- Free bonuses in product bundles work because they increase perceived value without proportional cost. This is more effective than reducing the main product price by the same amount. [L0313.024]
- A resistance band seller paired a $35.97 product with a $50 gift card for a partner weight-loss product, used to offer perceived value. The bonus was paired with an affiliate code so the seller could earn $50 to $150 when the buyer redeemed it. [L1153.031]
- Physical bonuses should match the buyer's need; a bonus that only looks valuable can be a waste. [L0314.002]
- Bonus items in a physical sense work when they directly address a real customer need related to the main product and cost much less than their apparent value to the buyer. Identify what customers need to use the main product effectively, then source an inexpensive version as a bonus. [L0314.002]
- Home-brewing starter kit: include a free ingredient pack (enough for one small batch) with purchases. Wholesale cost is minimal, customers see it as essential to getting started, and they are more likely to buy from you than from competitors not offering it. [L0314.004]
- Paddle boards with leashes: leashes cost $10-$15 each wholesale. Offering free leashes with board purchases provides genuine customer value while protecting margins. [L0314.005]
- Chandeliers priced at $1,000: offer a free pack of light bulbs with a wholesale cost of $20. Customers need bulbs and perceive high retail value. If competitors do not offer this, you gain a clear advantage. [L0314.006]
- Leather sofas: offer a free leather protectant to help customers maintain their purchase. This addresses post-purchase care concerns and adds perceived value without harming margins. [L0314.007]
- Treadmills: offer physical bonuses such as a free water bottle, towels, or apparel, or digital bonuses such as a free meal plan or an e-book on running technique. Test which types customers value most. [L0314.011]
- Free gift value: a bonus gift can also be a physical gift paired with a larger order. [L0999.064]
- Digital bonus versus physical gift: test both on the same product before choosing. [L0314.017]
- Airplane-style or large product purchases: the buyer may prefer a physical gift they need. [L0314.002]
- A physical gift that is cheap to print and delivered in the parcel can increase return visits and referrals. [L1437.005]
- Digital bonuses can be used when they are partnered with an external provider, so the store does not need to create the content. [L0314.009]
- Free ebook as a loss-prevention bonus: a free ebook on a related topic is a low-cost bonus that can be delivered automatically after purchase. [L1184.048]
- A physical bonus would not be justified if the margin is too thin. [L0941.108]
- White-glove delivery: offer it free as a bonus for high-ticket items ($2,000 or above), where margins are strong and most competitors offer only curbside delivery. White-glove delivery costs an extra $100-$150 per order. [L0314.012]
- Do not offer expensive bonuses ($100-$150 upgrades) across your entire site. Target them to your highest-margin products or those priced $2,000 or above to protect overall profitability. [L0314.013]

### Common mistakes

- Do not choose bonus items that carry high wholesale costs or threaten your profit margins. Bonuses are incentives, not revenue-builders. [L0314.015]
- Offering a free ebook that is unrelated to the product is weak. The bonus should be directly linked to the product's use or benefit. [L0577.016]
- Filler chapters and placeholder text make the bonus look cheap and weaken the offer. [L0860.015]
- Free gifts that are not added to some orders create refund and support problems. Check regularly that every order includes the free gift. [L1173.023]
- Customers who do not know about the gift may not add the product. Show the gift clearly on the product page and in the cart. [L1315.032]
- Do not promise a bonus that is not delivered automatically after checkout. [L1188.018]
- Bonus delivery must not be left to customers to claim through extra steps. [L1110.030] *(Source A, 2019: older advice.)*
- Custom packaging made for one product is expensive. Only use custom add-ons if the supplier already has the item in stock. [L0577.017]

#### Further details

- Free gifts like 'Free mystery gift when you spend $X' or complementary items (travel bag with hair dryer bundle) increase perceived value at zero cost and make higher-price tiers more attractive. [L0232.018]

- A low-cost bonus closely tied to the main product, such as a recipe book included with an air fryer, can raise perceived value even when it costs little to give. The bonus works best when the buyer would naturally want it alongside that product. [L0411.036]

- Lead magnet test: 1) Keep product and price unchanged. 2) Add a free gift to the order. 3) If conversion does not rise, switch to a different gift every second day (e.g., Day 1 ring, Day 2 necklace, Day 3 ebook). 4) Keep the gift that produces the most sales; the winning gifts can suggest future bundles. [L0773.016]

Test a free gift as a lead magnet while keeping the same product and price:
1) Add a free gift to the product, starting with a low-cost item that fits the product, such as a yoga mat with a 29.99 USD stretching band, which sharply raised conversion in one case.
2) If conversion does not rise, change the gift every two days.
3) Rotate options in order, for example a ring first, then a necklace, then an e-book.
4) Keep the gift that produces the most sales; the winning gift also shows which bundle idea to test next.
[L0774.013]

## Designing bundles and quantity-break ladders

### Bundle types and definitions

- A bundle combines more than one product into one offer, such as "buy this and this item and get an extra 20% off." A bundle that contains several units of the same product is a quantity break, such as "buy one and get 20% off, buy two and get 30% off." [L0434.010, L0435.012]
- Bundle pricing sells two or more units for less than the sum of the single prices, so the customer gets an automatic saving and average order value (AOV) rises. The bundle price must still leave a margin that covers all costs. [L0436.016]
- Quantity-break offers raise AOV because customers who buy more get a bigger discount, and the store earns more per order. [L0635.027]
- Automatic quantity discounts raise both AOV and conversion rate because the total looks lower for multiple units. A short urgency line such as "only 5 left in stock" can strengthen the effect. [L0773.018]
- Bulk pricing or a discounted second unit raises AOV. Buyers who want more of the same product are easier to convert than new buyers. [L0378.030]
- Bundles raise AOV because a customer who buys two items plus a free third spends more per order than someone buying one item. Use bundles to make the store more profitable per visitor, not only to win the first sale. [L0562.008]
- Bundles and volume discounts increase the number of items per order. A free-gift option can reduce the perceived price without a full discount. Test which offer type fits the margin of your products. [L0476.031]

### Bundle types in use

#### Quantity breaks and buy-X-get-Y

- **Buy two, get a percentage off the second unit or the whole order:** buy two and get an extra 30% off raises AOV and margin per order, and suits customers who want more units of the same item. [L0434.012, L0435.011]
- **Buy one get one free (BOGO):** strong with customers, because people respond to a perceived free item. [L0440.045]
- **Buy two, get the third free:** a typical bundle offer; choose the version by testing which sells best while still leaving a good margin. [L0562.007]
- **Buy two, get the third free, for products customers already buy in stores:** buy two get a third free raises order size and margin; buy two with free shipping also raises both. [L1176.027]
- **Buy any two, get the third 50% off:** encourages multiple items per order while keeping per-unit profitability. [L0232.006]
- **Buy-X-get-Y offers:** customers buy more items to qualify for the free item. They work best when the product is logically bought in multiples, such as home decor. [L0650.037]
- **Tiered BOGO-style ladders:** Buy 1 Get 1 Free, Buy 2 Get 1 Free, Buy 3 Get 2 Free, with the savings amount shown under each tier. Keep the visual layout simple so customers are not overwhelmed. [L0232.004]
- **Common offers for low-priced products:** buy two get one free and buy three get two free. A store running several of these is usually testing which quantity customers accept. [L1172.024]
- **Buy two, buy three or buy four and get one free:** works well for small, cheap products. Test several and keep the one that suits the margin. [L1171.025]
- **Buy-two offer with a second item at a percentage off:** a gifting variant, used with free US shipping. [L1225.019]
- **Buy one at 20% off, with a second item at an extra 15% off:** one of the offer structures that work on TikTok. [L1183.018]
- **Buy one and get the second at 50% off, with free shipping above a threshold:** a bundle structure, with free shipping above a threshold such as 55 dollars. [L0789.028]
- **Buy two get 50% off or 30% off:** tested structures among several others (see the list below). [L0650.034]

#### Complementary bundles

- A complementary bundle pairs two different products that solve a related problem. Replace a second identical item with a complementary product, such as a sleep-related item with pyjamas or an organiser with a wallet, because a second identical item has little value to a buyer who needs only one. [L1131.013]
- Complementary items work better than pushing customers to buy more of the same product to reach a free-shipping threshold. [L1172.026]
- Complementary bundles work well when the add-on solves a related need and is cheap to ship. Shipping both items in one parcel is cheaper than two separate orders to the same address. [L1345.022]
- Complementary-product bundles can convert better than standard discounts. A bundle with a complementary item made with the Bundle Bear app roughly doubled conversion rate compared with buy 2 get 20% off, with a 40 to 50 percent bundle take rate and about $40 extra profit per order. [L0650.038]
- Complementary items are added when customers rarely buy more than one of the same product. [L0440.045]
- Offer a complementary item when the offer complements the solution to the customer's problem or makes the product more attractive. For example, a candle seller can give two free when a customer buys four. [L0549.020]
- For a cheap product that complements a main product, test buy two get two free instead of a small percentage discount, since this can raise AOV. [L0549.022]
- Pair a main product with a part it needs from the same list, such as Fish Tanks with Aquarium Stands, Koi Ponds & Filtration Systems, and Above Ground Pools with Pool Filtration Systems and Pool Vacuums. Selling them together raises order value and saves the buyer a separate search. [L0546.020]
- Bundle a product with a complementary product the same buyer needs. Example: a neck pillow for someone with neck pain who has trouble sleeping. [L0871.043]

#### Build-your-own, sets, avatar and family bundles

- **Build-your-own:** up to 15% off, free shipping, and any combination of products the customer chooses. Show it as a pop-up offer that splits the screen so the product choices stay visible. [L0524.025]
- **Sets (sold together, older advice, Source A 2021):** matching sofas and love seats should be sold together with the recliners they match, because customers buy complete sets. Keep unrelated products out of the store, with set-based exceptions. [L0801.057]
- **Sets, rule (Source A 2023):** sell bundles only when the product naturally comes in sets, such as dining chairs sold in packs of four at about $200. Do not turn single items priced around $50 into bundles just to reach a higher price. [L0804.088]
- **Avatar bundles:** packages designed around a specific type of buyer, such as entrepreneur, traveler, biohacker, essential and longevity. Use them in promotions to match the offer to each customer type. [L0334.021]
- **Family packs:** bundles such as a family pack of 3 to 4 products work well and can be promoted on a specific collection page. [L0605.033]
- **Two-family bundle:** instead of $70 for one unit, offer two units for $60 total. This appeals to families buying gifts for multiple households. [L0209.033]
- **Multi-pair bundles for shoes:** buyers often purchase several pairs in different colors or variants at the same time, so offer multi-pair bundles next to the single-pair option. [L1219.009]
- **Subscriptions as a bundle layer:** offers can scale with quantity and subscription, with bigger discounts for more units, extra savings for a monthly subscription, and a "frequently bought together" add-on at a discount. [L0919.022]
- **Buy More Save More:** suits direct-to-consumer consumable brands such as food, beverage and makeup. It can also lead first-time buyers into a subscription, because they try an offer before deciding to subscribe. [L1231.043]

#### Bundling with an upgrade

- Bundling combines a main product with an add-on you already sell as an upgrade and gives it one bundle price. Example: a $400 golf bag and a $100 trolley sold together for $450, promoted as a single package for a few days. [L1204.035]
- A bundle works as a re-engagement tool because it is a new offer the customer has not seen before. Its price should be close to the cost of the main product plus the upgrade, with low-cost accessories added, so the margin stays acceptable. [L1205.013]
- Case: a golf bag sold for $400 and a carrier upgrade for $100. For one month, the store offered the bag, the carrier, 24 golf balls, a glove and a cleaning kit together for $500. The extra cost to the store was only slightly higher than the bag and carrier alone, and the package gave a reason to contact old leads who had viewed the bag but not bought. [L1205.012]

### Choosing bundles by stage and starting point

- Start with one product. Add a bundle or a second product once the first one shows demand. Selling several products can also build trust, depending on the niche. [L0774.020]
- When starting out, focus on one product at a time rather than a bundle. Bundles are harder to sell and harder to test for a beginner. [L0772.013]
- Start with simple bundles that make sense for the product. Do not add complex bundle structures until the store is scaling. [L1299.002]
- Keep one clear offer per store rather than several different offers for different categories, to avoid confusing customers, unless the store is large enough to manage it. The overall goal is to raise AOV. [L0650.036]
- Replace standard time-limited percentage sales with bundle offers, such as buy two get 20% off plus a free gift, or buy one and get a complementary product free. In one store this increased conversions and AOV. [L0549.019]
- Changing an offer from 50% off plus free shipping to buy one get one free or buy two get two free made a major difference in one seller's sales compared with the earlier offer. [L0549.021]
- Bundles at the first step of the shop raise AOV. A product priced at $39 can be sold as two or three units to bring the order value to $70 to $80. A bundle raises margin per order without raising the ad cost per click. [L0645.024]
- Bundles plus post-purchase upsells are the way to increase margin when a single product is priced too low. Reported results: about 70 percent of customers chose a bundle, and AOV rose from $39 to $110 across the store. [L0645.025]
- Bundle at the first step, then upsell: a single-product store that averaged $40 per order introduced buy two get extra 20% off, so two items cost about $70. Shipping per unit fell with two units shipped together, and AOV rose from $40 to $70 with higher profit per order. [L0434.013]
- Expanding a single-product store into a multi-product store with bundles and extra items raises AOV. Multi-buy offers encouraged customers to buy more. [L0519.033]
- Bundles raise AOV for the store, so a store with higher AOV can justify a higher cost per acquisition when scaling. Increasing AOV through bundles, upsells, higher-priced offers or a higher price raises profit per order. [L0999.015]
- Quantity bundles aim to raise average order value, revenue per customer and profit per order at the same time. They work as a conversion lever because each order contains more units. [L0860.002]
- Bundle structure matters more than the single-unit price: a visible quantity ladder with a clear saving per tier pushes multi-unit orders. Keep the ladder short so a buyer can compare the tiers in a few seconds. [L1188.019]
- Bundles raise AOV and also take up more space in Google Shopping ads, because each bundle is a different product listing. Bundles can be given their own UPC codes, which allows listing them on eBay. [L0920.021]
- A bundle is made by building one product page that includes several products, with the bundle price above the individual sum. No app is needed for a basic bundle. [L0855.047]

#### Bundle strategy by price point

- **Low-priced products (about $5 to $10) in paid ads:** a bundle can raise AOV to a level that covers ad costs. The bundle must still leave a margin of about $20 to $25 per customer. [L1174.016]
- **Low-priced products in general:** bundle offers such as buy two, buy three or buy four and get one free work well for small, cheap products. Test several and keep the one that makes sense for the margin. [L1171.025]
- **Low-priced kitchen products:** quantity discounts can encourage customers who want multiples to buy more. Test quantity offers with the product's natural use cases. [L1172.027]
- **Competitor example at low price:** a competitor selling a cheap item priced one unit at $24.99, two units at $29.99 and a larger pack at a higher price used bundling to raise margin per order. The product was judged a potential winner because the competitor ran 280 active ads for it. [L1174.017]
- **Cheap add-on check:** if a cheap add-on of about 2 to 3 dollars shows a good take rate of around 40 to 50 percent, a bundle with a total price up to about 40 dollars can make sense. The extra margin from the add-on can justify the bundle. [L0708.008]
- **Mid-priced products:** a bundle offer for two items is typically priced about 20% below the single price. The second item can be a different variant of the same product, for example another size or color. [L1173.021]
- **Higher-priced products:** a seller of a $79 device... see the bundle example below. Price the bundles so the best-selling option is the obvious deal. [L0789.026]
- **Thin-margin products (cheap items):** see the pricing procedure below. [L0298.032]

### Designing the tier ladder

#### Tier structures

- Create tiered offers with a simple layout. Buy 1, Buy 2 and Buy 3 quantity tiers with progressive discounts are used for a single-product store. [L0860.005, L1149.006]
- Multi-tier discounts such as "buy any 2 pieces, get the 3rd one 50% off" encourage multiple items per cart order while maintaining per-unit profitability. [L0232.006]
- Pre-selected starter kits: present the six most popular combinations with the items included, rather than making the customer pick from a long list. Customers who face around 70 stone options and 11 setting options cannot judge which choice is best. [L1225.020]
- Replace a custom bundle builder with ready-made bundles and an extra 10% off the pre-created bundle. Fewer decisions and clicks can help lift AOV. [L1225.017]
- Keep the single-unit price low for entry, then make the larger quantity or the subscription the default that drives value. Visitors are more likely to accept a bigger option when it is preselected. [L0524.028]
- Price a single unit intentionally higher than the cost structure suggests (for example $25) so the bundle offer (for example 2 units for $30) looks like an exceptional value. This raises bundle attachment rate even for low-margin products. [L0298.058]

Necklace tier ladder (case from the lesson; prices as stated, store prices in EUR, costs in USD):

| Tier | Offer | Notes from the lesson |
|---|---|---|
| 1 Basic | One necklace at 19.90 EUR | Simplest form, already an offer |
| 2 Gift | One necklace at 29.90 EUR plus a free complementary second necklace (valued at 29.90 EUR) | Raised conversion rate; the price increase was needed to fund the gift |
| 3 Buy one, get two | Buy one at 29.90 EUR and get two additional, different necklaces free; all items ship in one package | Cost of goods about 13 USD for the bundle, margin about 17 |
| 4 Big Boss | 29.90 EUR for one necklace, plus a second necklace, a jewelry box (about 1 USD cost), a free ebook or lookbook, and free shipping | Maximum perceived value; the highest-value offer in the lesson |

[L0502.014]

Case behind the ladder: an African-map necklace was sold in a Shopify store at 19.90 EUR, aimed at people with an African background who want to show their origin. The dream outcome was to look good in it and show love for one's roots, and the basic single item was offered first. The price was raised to 29.90 EUR and a complementary second necklace valued at 29.90 EUR was added as a free gift, which raised conversion. The next tested offer was buy one for 29.90 EUR and get two more different necklaces free, shipped in one package, with a cost of goods of about 13 USD. The final Big Boss offer added a jewelry box (about 1 USD), a free ebook or lookbook for styling and care, and free shipping. Each stage raised conversion, and the bundle kept a margin of about 17 at 29.90 EUR. When cost rises, the price must rise too. [L0502.028]

Necklace variant: the necklace sold for 29.99 with a free choice of country, and bundles were offered in which customers who bought four items received two for free. [L0519.032]

#### Tier design by example

- Quantity ladder for a $39.99 product on the product page: Buy 1 is 1 product at $39.99 with no bonus. Buy 2 saves 15% at $67.99 and includes a free ebook. Buy 3 saves 25% at $89.99 and includes a free ebook and free shipping. The lesson's example product price ($39.99) differs from its book price ($41.95), so calculate with your own product price. [L0860.003]
- Bundle tiers for a single book product (standard price 41.95 USD):

| Tier | Quantity | Discount | Bonus and extras |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buy 1 | 1 book | None, standard price 41.95 USD | None |
| Buy 2 | 2 books | Small discount, e.g. 10-15% | Free ebook included |
| Buy 3 | 3 books | Bigger discount, e.g. 20-25% | Free ebook and free shipping included |

[L0860.004]

- Quantity bundle discounts for a single book: Buy 2 gets a small discount of 10 to 15 percent, and Buy 3 gets a larger discount of 20 to 25 percent. The spoken explanation in the source mentions 10 to 25 percent for Buy 3, but the written material says 20 to 25 percent. Use the written range and check it against your own margin. [L1149.004]

- Quantity bundle structure for a single-book product:

| Tier | Quantity | Discount |
|---|---|---|
| Buy 1 | 1 book | Standard price - 41.95 USD |
| Buy 2 | 2 books | Small discount (e.g. 10-15%) |
| Buy 3 | 3 books | Bigger discount (e.g. 20-25%) |

[L1149.005]

- Quantity bundle ladder on a single product page: Buy 1 with Save 35%, Buy 2 with Save 50% marked Most Popular, and Buy 3 with Save 55% marked Best Offer. A free digital bonus worth $25, such as a cheat sheet or e-book, can be added to the bundle. [L1013.015]

- Case: in a Shopify digital e-book store, the product page combined a single-copy price about 35 percent below list with a quantity ladder: buying 2 copies gave 50 percent off and buying 3 gave 55 percent off. A "buy 1, give 1" gift option and a free bonus cheat sheet valued at $25 were added to the digital edition, and the top bundle was labelled "Best Offer" at $89.99. [L0560.034]

- Product page benchmark (lesson example):

| Tier | Saving | Gift wording | Bonus |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buy 1 | none | single unit | none |
| Buy 2 | 50% | Buy 1, give 1 to someone you love | free ebook worth 25 USD |
| Buy 3 | 55% | Buy 1 and give 2 to someone you love | free ebook bonus listed |

[L1141.010]

- Three-pack of a single item sold as a set: a seller that offered a three-pack of adult coloring books was the first to sell that set on Amazon and used it to stand out from single-book competitors. [L1153.030]

- Three-tier pack layout: 1 bottle for a 30-day supply, 2 bottles for a 60-day supply, and 3 bottles for a 90-day supply. Show a Most Popular badge on the 60-day tier and a Best Deal badge on the 90-day tier, with a crossed-out compare-at price (for example $129.90 beside $58.46, labelled Save 54%). The source's 54% label does not match this pair of prices, which works out to about 55%; check the figure against your own prices before publishing. [L1164.040]

- Fall sock ladder (US pricing):

| Offer | Contents | Sale price | Struck-through price |
|---|---|---|---|
| One pack | 5 socks | $19.99 | $39.99 (SAVE 50%) |
| Two pack | 10 socks | $39.98 | $79.98 (marked Best Seller) |
| Four pack | 20 socks | $79.96 | $159.96 |

The structure is buy one, buy two, buy four, with the price per pack stepping up in a consistent way. [L0347.017]

- In the same sock example, a two-pack of 10 items cost about $8 to source and sold for $39.98, so its margin per order was higher than the single-item 4x markup. Larger bundles should be designed to increase margin per bundle. [L0347.016]
- Bundle offers increase profit per customer without extra work, because they raise AOV and improve ad efficiency. Use bundles in several sizes so each order carries a bigger margin. [L0347.018]
- Each pack in a multi-pack bundle ladder for a one-size seasonal product can contain five different colors. Variety within a single pack size gives the buyer a reason to choose a larger bundle. [L0347.042]

- Bleem three-tier bundle ladder (case): the single pack showed a small saving of about $8. The two-pack cost $45 with free shipping and was labelled the most popular option. The three-pack cost $59 and included an extra crystal hair eraser for about $10 more. The store's average order value rose from about $39 to about $49, an increase of around $10, because most customers chose the two-pack or three-pack. [L0434.014, L0435.014]

- Three-tier ladder numbers: the single-unit pack offered only a small saving of about 8 USD. The two-pack was priced at 45 USD and included free shipping, which made it the stronger offer. [L0435.020]

- Lemonade-stand example: a lemonade stand charged $1 for the first cup and 50 cents for a second. Cookies were sold as 1 for $1 or 3 for $2, and golf balls at 50 cents for regular and $2.50 for premium. Bundles and bulk offers pushed the average order well above a typical single-cup sale. [L0378.029]

- Bundle example for a single device: a single device at 79 euros plus 10 euros shipping; a device with two additional accessories at a free-shipping price above 85 euros; and a device with four or more accessories at a higher tier. The middle bundle is the best seller. [L0789.026]

- Premium bundle example: a main product with a complementary item at 49.99 dollars with free shipping, and a second pack priced at 50 percent off. [L0789.028]

- Tested structures on a planters collection: buy 2 get 3rd free is one structure among several. Other tested structures include buy 1 get 1 free, buy 1 get the second item at 20% off, buy 1 get 1 free plus a small free gift for the first 50 orders, buy 4 get 4 free for a home decor product with about $30 margin per order, and buy 2 get 30% off. [L0650.034]

- Home decor item priced at 19.90 dollars, offered as buy four get four free. Average order value rose from about 19.90 to around 70 to 80 dollars because customers bought multiple units of the same item. [L0440.043]

- Multi-unit bundle pattern: where customers want several of the same item, a bundle with a clear free-unit or discount deal raises AOV without adding much cost. Example A: a skincare product at 24 dollars sold as buy three for 60 dollars. Example B: a kitchen sponge pack at 9 dollars sold as buy two get one free. [L0440.044]

- Car accessory with strong grip and two color options sold for $29.90, with offers of buy one or buy three and get one free. The price was judged fair because the product had clear images and short demonstration GIFs. [L1172.056]

- Shoe bundles: see the multi-pair bundles above. [L1219.009]

- Seasonal bundles: volume discounts of 2 items for 10% off and 3 items for 15% off are especially useful in Q4 to raise order value. [L1220.027]

- Volume discount example: 10% off for 2 items, 15% off for 3 items, 20% off for 4 items. The discount should be calculated only after checking fulfillment costs with the supplier. A two-item bundle cost about $201 instead of $223, so one item fell from about $112 to about $100. [L0477.029]

- Practical example: a bundle with an extra item, given as "buy two, get one free" plus a clear black or multi-pack option. A product with no bundles left money on the table in the example. [L0655.015]

- Jewelry case: a new jewelry store with one product and a landing page that showed only that item. The page offered a bundle option, and buyers chose to purchase more than one unit. Facebook ads ran on this single product, and an issue on the ad account side limited spend. After about two weeks the store had made roughly $1,000 in sales (about $1k in revenue in the first two weeks). [L0995.053, L0999.082]

- Quantity ladder with a family of products: a store that offered quantity options such as 2, 4 or 10 on the same page kept its listing aligned with what buyers already search for (see Google Shopping section below). [L0934.050]

### Badges and labels

- Competitor stores mark the best-value bundle with a Most Popular badge. Use the badge to guide buyers toward the bundle with the highest margin or volume, once you know which one it is. [L0600.023]
- Label the 2-pack as "Most Popular" and the 3-pack as "Best Value". Add small icons such as a gift and a truck to make the tiers easier to scan. [L0860.006]
- Label one bundle as "most popular" and highlight it visually to steer shoppers toward the higher-value option. A clearly marked default reduces choice effort and lifts AOV more than an unmarked list of equal options. [L0435.021]
- Highlight the middle bundle tier with a most popular label and add free shipping to it. This steers most buyers toward the bundle with the higher order value and better margin for the store. [L0434.015]
- Badge sets seen in the sources:
  - Most Popular on the 2-pack, Best Value on the 3-pack. [L0860.006]
  - Most Popular on Buy 2, Best Offer on Buy 3, with Buy 2 at Save 50% and Buy 3 at Save 55%. [L1013.015]
  - Best Offer on the top bundle at $89.99. [L0560.034]
  - Best Seller on the two pack in the sock ladder. [L0347.017]
  - Most Popular on the 60-day tier and Best Deal on the 90-day tier. [L1164.040]
- A badge sits on the bundle that tests as best (margin or volume), not on the bundle you want to push by default. Competitors' badge placement is a signal, not a rule. [L0600.023]
- Show the regular price next to the bundle price to make the saving clear. [L0871.044]
- Show the saving on each tier so the customer can compare them in a few seconds. [L1188.019, L0232.004]

### Naming tiers and bundles

- Creative names create psychological appeal and justify premium pricing. Examples: "Elite Growth Kit," "Stylist Bundle," "The Complete Acne Killer," "Twin Pack." Generic names such as "Bundle 2" leave perceived value on the table. [L0232.014]
- Example: a luxury hair laser at $1,999 alone can be repackaged as an "Elite Growth Kit" with a 5-month supply and extended warranty, justifying approximately $300 additional cost through distinctive naming. [L0232.015]
- Generic bundle names fail to create perceived value or justify premium pricing compared with names that suggest benefits, status or use cases. [L0232.022]
- Name bundle tiers to reflect shared use cases or paired scenarios, such as "Twin Pack," "Room for Two," "Apartment Bundle," "Partner Kit." This appeals to customers buying for multiple users and increases AOV through contextual appeal. [L0232.025]
- Give each bundle a name that describes the outcome, such as a "sleep bundle" or "back and neck bundle," and show the regular price next to the bundle price. Combined bundles with a shared problem (for example neck and back pain) sell well. Sleep bundle example: priced at $79.95 against a regular combined price of $126, shown clearly on the landing page. [L0871.044, L0871.043]
- Label each bundle by the length of supply it covers, such as 10-day, 30-day, 60-day, 120-day or one-year supply, not only by quantity. This lets buyers compare the cost per day across tiers. [L0600.039]
- Tier labels for a three-tier pack: 30-day, 60-day, 90-day supply. [L1164.040]

### Pricing each tier from its own cost

#### Rules

- Before launching a bundled offer, calculate the per-unit costs for all items combined and verify that the bundled price still allows healthy margins. Bundling power collapses if unit economics do not support profitability. [L0233.024]
- Set bundle pricing from cost of goods and margin: first calculate cost and margin for each option, then ensure the best bundle still gives a healthy margin even when a second unit is sold at 50 percent off. [L0789.027]
- Each bundle quantity must be priced on its own supplier cost, not as a simple multiple of the single-unit price. A two-piece bundle is priced from the two-piece cost, which is why $45.95 for two is not double the single price. [L1182.010]
- Use a fixed bundle total instead of a percentage discount when setting bundle prices, so the customer sees a clear per-unit price and the seller avoids discount math. Pick a total where the per-unit price drops clearly from the single price to make the saving obvious. [L0861.005]
- Check the bundle saving against the sum of the single prices. Saving percentage = (combined value minus bundle price) divided by bundle price. Example: a bundle priced at 500 with a combined value of 680 gives (680 minus 500) divided by 500 = 36 percent. [L1088.052] (older advice, Source A 2020)
- A bundle at 29.99 for two items against 59.99 full price gives a $10 saving on the pair, about 16.7% off the full price. [L0436.025]

Example: a single item at $29.99 and two items for $49.99 instead of $59.99. Before launching a bundle like this, check that the bundle price still covers product cost, acquisition cost and fees. [L0436.025]

- A bundle with a total price up to about 40 dollars can make sense if a cheap add-on has a 40 to 50 percent take rate (see the add-on check above). [L0708.008]
- Profit check: a buy-4-get-4-free offer on a home decor product left about $30 margin per order for one student. [L0650.043]
- If the profit is too thin at the current price, improve the offer rather than only changing ads, for example by giving two items at a discounted total price while keeping a larger margin. The offer change must still leave enough margin after the cost of the extra item. [L0661.028]

#### Pricing examples

- Thin-margin procedure: set the single unit at 25 dollars (or 21, or 19.99), and pre-select a 2-unit bundle at 30 dollars. Mark the bundle with labels such as "lowest price" or "most popular" and add value indicators. Customers often purchase bundles because the offer feels compelling; test with bundle apps such as Catching Bundle, Bundle Bear or White Bundle. [L0298.032]
- Two-item bundle priced about 20% below the single price (see mid-priced section). [L1173.021]
- A single bottle at $19.95 paired with a Buy Two fixed total of $29.99, which works out to $14.98 per bottle and saves about $10 against two singles. A Buy Three fixed total of $38.95 brings it to $12.98 per bottle, and the saving against a $60.00 compare-at price shows as $21.05. [L0861.004]
- Book pricing formulas (standard price 41.95 USD):
  - Bundle price per tier = unit price × quantity × (1 − discount rate). [L0860.027]
  - Worked example: with a 39.99 unit price, a 15% discount on two units gives 67.98 and a 25% discount on three gives 89.98. The displayed prices are rounded to 67.99 and 89.99 so they end in.99. Divide the bundle total by the quantity to see the effective price per unit (about 33.99 for the 2-pack and 30.00 for the 3-pack). [L0860.027]
  - For a book priced at 41.95 USD: a 2-pack at 10% off totals 75.51 and at 15% off totals 71.32; a 3-pack at 20% off totals 100.68 and at 25% off totals 94.39. Before choosing the exact percentage, check that the bundle total still covers COGS, shipping and ad spend per order. [L0860.028]

#### Pricing procedure: Buy-One-Get-One-Free bundle

1. Ask the supplier for the cost of two pieces. In the example, one piece costs $8 and the second unit adds $4, so the total for two pieces is $12.
2. In the bundle row of the sheet, check that the market label reads US and not UK.
3. Enter the total product cost for two pieces ($12) and set the quantity to 2.
4. Raise the selling price until the margin reaches 70%; in the example this gives $45.95 for two pieces.
5. Check that the margin is at least 67% before using the price.

[L1182.007]

#### Pricing procedure: Buy-Two-Get-One-Free bundle

1. Ask the supplier for the cost of three pieces, or add one more unit to the two-piece cost. In the example, two pieces cost $12 and the third unit adds $4, so the total for three pieces is $16.
2. In the sheet, change the quantity to 3 in the bundle row and enter the total product cost of $16.
3. Check that the market label reads US.
4. Raise the selling price until the margin reaches 70%; in the example the final price is $64.95, after first trying a higher price of about $69.95.
5. Check that the margin is at least 67% before using the price.

[L1182.008]

Example: three pieces with a total product cost of $16 are priced at $64.95 to hold a 70% margin after fees and shipping. A price near $69.95 was tested first and then lowered to $64.95. [L1182.009]

#### Pricing procedure: full sequence for a single product with bundles

1. Price the product at roughly the same level as competitors.
2. If the margin is at least 70%, price slightly lower to undercut them; if the margin is strong but tighter, price slightly higher to protect profitability.
3. Replace any Buy-one-get-one offer with Buy 1, Buy 2 and Buy 3 bundles that carry progressive discounts.
4. Add a free gift to the Buy 2 and Buy 3 bundles to raise perceived value.

[L1149.011]

- Bundle pricing with a margin reference: "Bundle pricing sells two or more units for less than the sum of single prices, so the customer gets an automatic saving... Calculate that the bundle price still leaves a margin that covers all costs." [L0436.016]
- Price of a low-cost add-on: if a cheap product (about $5 to $10) is competing in paid ads, a bundle can raise AOV to a level that covers ad costs. The bundle must still leave a margin of about $20 to $25 per customer. [L1174.016]

### Price-raising before a BOGO offer

- BOGO works when margins allow it. If margins are thin, raise the product price by $5 to $10 first and then test BOGO, provided branding is strong enough to justify the higher price. [L1260.017, L1345.013]
- Procedure: set up a buy-one-get-one-free offer when margins are thin:
  1. Raise the product price by 5 to 10 USD.
  2. Strengthen branding so the higher price is credible.
  3. Offer buy-one-get-one-free on the higher price.
  
  In niches such as jewelry, good product images can justify a higher perceived value (example: a necklace going from 12.99 USD to 19.99 USD with BOGO). [L1259.026]
- In niches such as jewelry, strong images can raise perceived value, so a necklace listed at 12.99 could be shown at 19.99 and then offered as buy-one-get-one-free. Results depend on niche and margin, so test. [L1260.018]
- Raise the price before the bundle is made: a single product priced too low can be lifted by bundles and upsells (see the first-step bundle section above). [L0645.025]
- The price should also be set so the single-unit price is raised before the offer and not tested for margin only: "Test which offer type fits the margin of your products." [L0476.031]
- Test offers for thin margins: see the offer change above. [L0661.028]

### Bundle offers and where to put free shipping

Sources disagree on where free shipping belongs. Both values are given below with the condition under which each applies.

| Position of free shipping | Source | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| Highest tier only; the lowest tier pays shipping (for example $5 on the lowest bundle tier) | [L0232.005, L0232.012] | Protects margins on budget purchases and uses free shipping as an incentive to upgrade |
| Never on the lowest tier (this is listed as a mistake) | [L0232.021] | Removing free shipping from the lowest tier keeps the upgrade incentive and avoids giving margin away to price-sensitive buyers |
| Middle tier, the two-pack labelled most popular | [L0434.014, L0434.015, L0435.014, L0435.020] | When the middle bundle is the one you want most buyers to pick because it carries the higher order value and better margin |
| Highest tier, the 3-pack, with free shipping as one of its extras | [L0860.007] | When the larger bundle should carry the most visible extras |
| Above a threshold, the free-shipping price (e.g. above 85 euros or above 55 dollars) | [L0789.025, L0789.026, L0789.028] | When the product price is high enough that a threshold steers buyers to a bundle |
| Free shipping on a buy-two offer | [L1176.027] | Raises both order size and margin |
| Free shipping on a gift variant of a buy-two offer, with free US shipping | [L1225.019] | Gift-season offers |
| Free shipping as a feature of the build-your-own bundle | [L0524.025] | Build-your-own offers |
| Free shipping on the top bundle tier of the necklace ladder | [L0502.014] | The highest-value offer in the lesson |

- Free shipping on the lowest tier removes any incentive for customers to upgrade to higher tiers and wastes margin on price-sensitive buyers who would purchase regardless. [L0232.021]
- Charge $5 shipping on the lowest bundle tier and offer free shipping on the highest tier to protect low-end margins while using free shipping to incentivise upgrades. [L0232.012]
- Place free shipping on the highest-tier bundle, never the lowest. This protects margins on budget purchases while using free shipping as an upsell incentive. [L0232.005]
- Bundles with complementary items work better than pushing customers to buy more of the same product to reach a free-shipping threshold. [L1172.026]
- Bundles reduce shipping cost per unit, since one parcel carries several items. This improves profit per order while the bundle price still feels like a saving to the customer. [L0435.013]
- Bundle shipping saves money because two items shipped to one address cost less than two separate orders, so bundles can improve margin while appearing to be a deal. [L1259.032]
- When an offer includes several items, ask the supplier to ship them in one package. Otherwise shipping costs multiply and the margin disappears; the extra cost should only be the product cost of the extra item. [L0502.018]
- For a bundle with a complementary item, the bundle is shipped as one order to the same address to save on shipping costs. [L1259.030, L1260.022]
- Complementary-product bundles work well when the add-on solves a related need and is cheap to ship. [L1345.022]

#### Gifts placement

- Free gifts go on the bundles, not the single-unit tier. Adding a free gift to the Buy 2 and Buy 3 tiers raises perceived value and conversion rate. [L1149.008]
- Offering free items or strong discounts such as buy one get one free or buy two get two free attracts customers, because people respond strongly to a perceived free item. [L0440.045]
- The offer should either complement the solution to the customer's problem or make the product more attractive. For example, if you sell candles, give two free when a customer buys four. [L0549.020]
- Place the free ebook on the 2-pack and the 3-pack, and place free shipping on the 3-pack. This gives the larger bundle the most visible extras. [L0860.007]
- Free gift on a bundle with a gift-friendly message: a single tier with "buy 1 and give 1 to someone you love" wording. [L1141.010]
- Free gifts raise perceived value, and the free-gift option can reduce the perceived price without a full discount. [L0476.031]
- Gift-season offers should sound like a gift, not a bargain. [L1225.019]
- Gift-friendly niches can benefit from buy-one-get-one or buy-2-get-items offers, because customers can give the items to friends or partners. [L0650.039]

### Bundle strategy by brand and niche

- Buy-one-get-one-free can downgrade a brand that sells a premium or identity-based product, because it cheapens the perceived value. Use a different offer for such brands. [L1131.012]
- Buy-one-get-one-free can be a high barrier when many customers only want one gift, as seen with a previous Mother's Day mask offer that required buying two. Test percentage-off or a second-item discount, which gives more flexibility to single-gift buyers. [L1225.015]
- The best offer type depends on the product and niche. Gift-friendly niches can benefit from buy-one or buy-2-get-items offers. [L0650.039]
- BOGO and similar offers are niche- and margin-specific and must be tested. Products that seem unlikely to sell in multiples often do sell that way when the offer is tested. [L1345.015]
- Bundle offers such as "buy two, get one free" plus a clear black or multi-pack option (see the example above). [L0655.015]
- Start a new store with one product and add a second product once the first one shows demand. [L0774.020]
- Products bought in stores: a bundle offer such as buy two get a third free works well for products customers already buy in stores. [L1176.027]
- Bundle the products that buyers would purchase together. Bundles work best when the products are related and buyers would purchase them together. Use a bundle app such as Bold Product Bundles for these combinations, though any bundle app works. (older advice, Source A 2021) [L1112.070]

### Sourcing and supplier bundles

- Create product bundles from the same supplier, such as kits made of components that come together, rather than making customers assemble cheap separate components to reach a price point. (older advice, Source A 2021) [L0671.055, L0671.194]
- Before building a bundle from related products, test a tiered quantity discount on the same product:
  1. One item at the normal price.
  2. Two items at a special discount price.
  3. Three items at a better discount price.

  This raises AOV with no extra product research, and the bundle idea can be tested later if it still needs a boost. [L0774.053]
- Test a second product as a bundle with the supplier before running ads on it:
  1. Choose the main product and a second product that is selling well for other stores.
  2. Ask the supplier agent to create a bundle SKU with the main product and the second product.
  3. Measure which bundle buyers pick most, then run ads for the second product on its own.

  [L0774.018]
- Discuss tiered pricing options with your supplier before launching a bundle. [L0240.011]
- Bundles and quantity levels with the supplier: ask the supplier for the cost of two or three pieces before pricing the bundle (see the pricing procedures). [L1182.007, L1182.008]

### Bundles and Google Shopping

- Bundles with a single item's page: create the product page for the single item that Google Shopping shows most, and offer quantity options such as 2, 4 or 10 on the same page. This keeps your listing aligned with what buyers already search for. (Source A 2023) [L0934.050]
- Do not repackage a product into a higher-priced bundle SKU, such as an $800 product sold as a $1,200 bundle. Google Shopping shows the higher price next to competitors at $800, which lowers click-through rate and sales. (older advice, Source A 2018) [L1109.032]
- Unique bundles raise AOV and also take up more space in Google Shopping ads, because each bundle is a different product listing (see the channel section above). [L0920.021]

### Bundles on TikTok and other channels

- Offer structures that work well on TikTok include buy one get one free, buy two get one free, buy one at 20% off with a second item at an extra 15% off, and bundles. Building a strong offer is the most important investment when creating a unique selling angle. [L1183.018]

### Unbundling and debundling

#### Principles

- Unbundle high-priced products into separate components to lower the entry price. This improves click-through rates on Google Shopping ads while generating add-on revenue. [L0240.011]
- A debundled listing with a lower price can raise Google Ads click-through rate because it is cheaper than competitors' bundles. It is not false advertising when the listing photo and title show only the single item. [L1076.029]
- Unbundling sells the core product without an accessory at a lower price, and the accessory is offered as an optional add-on. Confirm the MAP limit with the supplier before listing it. [L1204.037]

#### Examples

- Product bundle: two separately priced items, one at $400 and one at $100, can be sold together as a single $500 bundle. The bundle packages the products into one purchase with a combined price. [L0757.020]
- Product un-bundle: a single higher-priced package is split into parts that can be sold separately. The lesson's example pairs a $1,000 price with an $800 price for the un-bundled version. [L0757.021]
- Paddle board example: a paddle board listed at 800 dollars without a paddle, which is 200 dollars below the 1,000 dollar competitors' price, with the paddle added for plus 200 dollars. Confirm the MAP limit with the supplier before listing it. [L1204.037]

#### Procedure: debundling a product for a cross-sell

1. Check the supplier price list for a single-item price, or ask the supplier for the separate MAP, wholesale and retail prices of the item sold in the bundle.
2. If the single item can be bought separately, list it at its lower price.
3. Use a photo of the single item only.
4. Let the customer add the accessory on the product page through a cross-sell offer.

[L1076.028]

### Procedures for complementary bundles from a low-cost accessory

1. On AliExpress, search for accessories related to the main product (for example fitness, health, jewelry, hair or nail accessories) and set a price filter from 0.01 USD to about 4 USD (for example min 0.01 and max 4 dollars).
2. Pick a low-cost complementary item (example cost around 2 USD) that solves a related problem.
3. Bundle it with the main product and ship as one order to the same address, which saves on shipping costs because two items ship to one address.
4. List the complementary item at a higher value (example 20 USD) while its cost is about 2 USD.
5. Offer the bundle with a visible saving, about 15% or a dollar-equivalent saving (example: main product 39.99 USD plus complementary product at 20 USD with a 15 USD discount, so buyers see the second item as almost free). Use wording such as "save big with our bundle."

[L1259.030, L1260.022]

### Testing and rollout

- Offers should be tested, and the one that makes the most sense for the margin should be kept. [L1171.025, L0562.007]
- Test BOGO and similar offers before committing, because the best structure depends on niche and margin. [L1345.015]
- A quantity-break offer and a buy-two offer both raise AOV, so test which one lifts AOV more. [L0635.027, L0434.012]
- Use bundles to make the store more profitable per visitor, not only to win the first sale. [L0562.008]
- Multiple bundle structures: several tested offers exist on the same product; keep the one that makes the most sense for the margin. [L0650.034, L1171.025]

### Mistakes to avoid

- Offering free shipping on the lowest tier removes the incentive to upgrade and wastes margin on buyers who would buy anyway. [L0232.021]
