- If you have no idea which product to upsell, use Shopify product recommendations or a recommendation app. The lesson prefers choosing a specific product in advance based on what each buyer purchased. [L1412.034]
- Find upsell ideas by identifying high-quality, high-margin products, the offline services competitors provide, and non-tangible offerings customers would value. [L0240.015]
- Look at what top offline competitors offer as upsells and bring similar services to the online store. [L1076.053]
- A small ebook on a topic related to the niche, such as the best products for a target group, can be sold as a cross-sell for about 5 to 10 dollars alongside the main product. [L1381.043]
- Upsells and cross-sells in the same niche: a coaster or metal straw for a coffee product can work well as a bundle item. [L1383.052]

### Cross-sell setup in the theme (without a third-party app)

**Setting up cross-selling in theme settings (Source A, older advice where applicable)**

1. Enable the cross-sell option. [L0973.008]
2. Enter the heading text shown above the suggested products, for example "you may also be interested in". [L0973.008]
3. Select the collection that holds the main products, for example a longboards collection. [L0973.008]
4. Add the tags "cross-sell one" and "cross-sell two" to the products that should be suggested for those main products. [L0973.008]
5. Test by adding a main product to the cart, confirming the heading and the tagged suggestions appear, and checking the cart shows the added items. [L0973.008]

- Cross-selling example: a seller of stand-up paddleboards suggests a paddle to buyers of a board, so the customer can add both in one order. The suggested item should logically match the main product. [L0973.009]
- In the Manhattan theme, cross-sell suggestions appear right after a customer clicks add to cart, before the cart page opens. The suggestion heading and the tagged products show at that step, so the customer can add them without leaving the flow. [L0973.017]
- For native cross-selling in the Manhattan theme, the main products are defined by the chosen collection, and the suggested products are the ones carrying the cross-sell tags. Tags therefore go on the add-on products, not on the main products. [L0973.018]
- Cross-selling is a feature to add as the store grows, not something to set up from the first day. Suggested products should make sense together so that customers add them to the cart. [L0973.010]

### Testing and measuring

- Test different upsell offers, because conversion varies widely between offers (see Pricing, margin and price limits). [L0923.041]
- Sources differ on when to measure results. The Visually cart test is set to track AOV as a goal. [L0389.010]
- Results vary from no gain to a large lift. A 30 percent rise in AOV for the top products has been reported, but do not expect this as a default. [L1113.077]
- Check the cart preview before publishing a cart upsell to confirm no free item appears. [L0389.014]

#### Further details

- Cross-selling succeeds when you educate customers about how the related product adds value or solves a secondary need, rather than simply listing options without context. [L0240.009]

- Collection pages can raise average order value because a customer who adds the winning product can also add other items from the same page to the cart. This is one reason to send ads to a collection page instead of a single product page once a product wins. [L0642.057]

## Post-purchase and checkout offers

### Core principles of post-purchase offers

- A post-purchase upsell is shown after checkout is complete, so the extra offer adds no friction to the original purchase. ReBuy presents it as a risk-free way to lift revenue from orders that are already placed. [L0593.007]
- Post-purchase upsells raise average order value. This matters most when margin per order is low, for example around 25 USD, because even a 5 USD increase per order adds up over many orders. [L0693.026]
- Customer acquisition cost is already paid on the first purchase, so it can be removed from the margin calculation for upsells and cross-sells. [L1404.026]
- Lead Value Optimization (LVO) raises the value of every visitor by offering related products and services right after a purchase. These offers can come from your own catalog or from other companies promoted as affiliate products. [L0676.009]
- Configure LVO offers before building the post-purchase funnel, because the upsell and related-product sequences depend on them. Treat LVO as the prerequisite for the post-purchase layer, not as an optional add-on. [L0676.018]
- A modern sales funnel keeps selling to a buyer while they are still in the cart, before they leave the site. Each accepted upsell can be followed by another upsell, so every purchase builds on the previous one. [L0676.010]
- One-time offers and limited-time buy-now offers are a supporting tactic, not the main focus of a store's funnel strategy. [L0676.011]
- Always have an upsell ready. Show one or two upsells immediately after a purchase, on the same page before the buyer leaves; this is the simplest way to capture extra revenue from buyers who have already decided to pay. [L0523.019]
- Upsell and downsell products must relate to the main product or belong to the same niche, so customers perceive them as natural complements to what they just bought. [L0291.002]
- Older (2021-2022): For a low-priced product, plan a pipeline on your own website where customers are upsold to higher-priced items. Plan the upsell path before traffic arrives. [L0768.021]

### Offer count and structure

- Each product should have at least one upsell. Two to three upsells are standard for typical conversion; up to four can be added for more aggressive offers. [L0291.001]
- Structure the first upsell as the same product the customer bought, offered at a lower price point. [L0291.003]
- Show the first upsell immediately after checkout as a one-click addition. Buyers accept congruent add-ons more readily while they are still deciding to buy. [L0378.027]
- Shopify allows at most two levels of post-purchase upsells on checkout. [L1410.022]
- Conflict on the number of levels: the general structure allows up to four upsells [L0291.001], but the Shopify checkout limit is two levels [L1410.022]. Use the two-level pattern where the Shopify checkout is the delivery point; longer chains apply where the setup allows them. [L0291.001, L1410.022]

### Choosing the upsell product

- Make the first upsell your highest-value product that fits the buyer. Judge upsells by the dollars they bring per offer shown, not by conversion rate alone. [L0378.024]
- Compare two upsells by revenue per offer shown:

```
Revenue per offer shown = price × conversion rate
```

A $100 upsell must convert about five times as often as a $500 upsell to bring in the same revenue per offer shown. [L0378.025]

- Offer upsells that are highly congruent with the first purchase. They should enhance the product, deliver the result faster, or provide a done-for-you or automated version of it. [L0378.026]
- The best first upsell is often more of the item the buyer already bought, especially a consumable they cannot use without. Buyers who need that item to continue an activity are the most likely to accept. [L0378.028]
- Miniature versions of the existing product, or re-offering the same product, work well because customers already know it. [L1404.026]
- Keep upsell price at or below 14 dollars. The seller reports about 40 percent of buyers accept the upsell when it is combined with SMS, email and retargeting. [L0789.031]
- Conflict on price level: the 14-dollar ceiling applies to the reported setup with SMS, email and retargeting [L0789.031]. Higher-ticket upsells that fit the buyer closely also convert, as the case below shows, so judge price by revenue per offer shown [L0378.025, L0378.033].
- Upsell price check: before publishing an upsell, compare the total the customer would pay after accepting with the bundle price on the product page for the same quantity. [L1141.005]

### Upsell and downsell sequences

#### Standard sequence

1. Present the first upsell.
2. If declined, present the first downsell (a lower-priced product from the same category).
3. If the first downsell is declined, either present the second downsell or direct the customer to the Thank You page.
4. If the customer accepts the first upsell, present the second upsell.
5. If the second upsell is purchased, present the third upsell or direct the customer to the Thank You page.
6. If the second upsell is declined, present the first downsell again, then direct the customer to the Thank You page.

[L0291.004]

- A downsell is a cheaper alternative offered after a customer declines an upsell. It keeps part of the sale and adds revenue at a lower price point, and is used together with upsells to raise average order value. Older (2021-2022). [L0511.024]

#### Two-level pattern (Monster funnel)

- Pattern: on accept, the first upsell goes to the second upsell; on decline, the flow goes to the first downsell, then to the thank-you page, so every path ends at the thank-you page. [L1410.022]
- Build sequence in ReconvertUpsell:
  1. Set a trigger condition, for example product purchase data: a specific product (at least one item, or the entire order) with unit/item count equal to 1.
  2. First upsell: the same product at 15% off with a timer. On decline: the same product at 30% off (last chance).
  3. Second upsell on accept: a different product in the same niche (chosen via Shopify product recommendation or a selected item), with a discount.
  4. Thank-you page with recommended products, a share button and a collection section.
  5. Enable the funnel under Settings > Checkout > Post-purchase page > select ReconvertUpsell.

  [L1410.023]

#### Chained flow

1. Initial offer: BOGO 25% off.
2. If accepted, upsell a gift card worth 10% off.
3. If declined, downsell to BOGO 50% off.
4. If the gift card upsell is declined, show the same gift card offer at a higher discount.
5. Each accepted or declined step can start a new upsell or downsell, so the flow keeps going as long as the customer accepts or declines.

[L1413.011]

- Upsell after an accepted 25% BOGO offer: show a gift card worth 10% off. Gift cards raise average order value and also encourage a second purchase, because the buyer has to spend the credit. [L1413.009]
- Downsell pattern: after a shopper declines the first offer, show the same type of deal at a deeper discount, framed as a final chance. Examples: after a declined 15% bundle offer, show the same bundle at 30% off with the line *Final offer, this price ends at checkout.*; after a declined free-shipping offer, show a 20% off code with the line *One more chance before you leave.* [L1413.008]
- Test sequence for a same-item offer:
  1. Make the first post-purchase offer the same product at a strong discount.
  2. If the customer accepts, offer the same product again at an even better price, or a different product, and split test those two options.
  3. If the customer declines, offer the same product again at a bigger discount.
  4. Add a small app sell on the thank-you page.

  [L0693.025]

### Discounts and price checks

- Highest conversion among post-purchase discounts came from offering the same product at 15-30% off. The speaker started with 15% for the first offer and 30% for the final decline offer. [L1410.021]
- Setting range: after checkout, show a post-purchase upsell with a discount on the same or a complementary product, for example 20 to 40 percent off. Add scarcity and use an upsell app to implement it. [L0645.026]
- Conflict on depth: the 15-30% range [L1410.021] is the measured best in one store; the 20-40% range [L0645.026] is the general setting. Start at 15% and go to 30% for the final decline, as measured; widen toward 40% only with a tested reason. [L1410.021, L0645.026]
- Example: right after payment, show the same product or a complementary product at 50% off. The buyer's payment details are already stored, so one click completes the purchase. [L0871.046]
- Discount ladder: second unit of the same product about 25% off, a three-unit bundle offer about 35% off, and a final downsell about 40% off. Later steps can carry higher discounts, but each must still pass the price check against its matching bundle price. [L1141.007]
- Upsell price formula and check:

```
Upsell price = unit price × (1 − discount)
Upsell total = price already paid + upsell price
```

Example with illustrative figures: a 40 USD unit at 25% off costs 30 USD; 40 USD + 30 USD = 70 USD, which must not exceed the 2-unit price on the product page. [L1141.006]

- Worked example for a 50 USD product in a post-purchase offer: 25% off gives 37.50 USD, 35% off gives 32.50 USD and 40% off gives 30.00 USD. Each deeper step must still be checked against the matching bundle price on the product page before the offer goes live. [L1141.033]
- Mistake: offers with unclear math confuse customers. They see a price that does not match the product page bundle and then complain by email, ask for refunds or open bank disputes. Each unchecked upsell price is a potential chargeback source. [L1141.008]
- After checkout, the buyer has already committed, so an add-on is an easy yes when its price is clearly better than the bundle price for the same quantity. Multi-unit add-ons are the strongest offers at this point. [L1141.014]

### Same-item repurchase offers

- Always test selling the same item at a discounted price in a post-purchase upsell first. Same-item offers showed the highest conversion even for expensive or heavy items; one store averaged around $150 per order. [L1410.024]
- A same-item repurchase offer was reported to work even for a store with an average order value near 150 USD and a heavy product. Test a discounted second unit before assuming an expensive or heavy product will not be bought again. [L1412.060]
- Add a low-cost add-on around 5-10 USD, or a post-purchase offer. After a customer has bought, offer the same item again at 20-30% off because the customer is already acquired. [L0363.017]
- Post-purchase offer with a countdown: a countdown of about 10 minutes, with a second unit framed as a gift for someone else, a discount such as 15% off and a decline option shown as a small text link. [L1410.044]
- Pairing a discount with a time limit, such as 30% off for 10 minutes, creates urgency. Adding a countdown or time window to the offer page supports the discount. [L1411.022]

### Case: a $7 ebook with a chain of offers

The project started with a $7 ebook, The 4 Foot Farm, which was being pushed with $15,000 to $20,000 of daily spend on Google Display Network. At a $7 sale price, covering $15,000 a day would have required 2,143 sales every day, a number that was out of reach. After checkout, the seller added a chain of one-click offers: a $9 print edition, a $27 monthly seed subscription, a $20 monthly newsletter, and a $497 physical growing kit that produces up to 100 pounds of food in a 4 by 4 foot space, with an optional $100 lifetime warranty. Around 2% of ebook buyers bought the $497 kit, which raised average order value to $40 to $44. At that order value, the daily break-even volume fell to 375 sales. A similar ebook with equally strong front-end conversion but less closely related upsells never reached the spend level needed to scale. [L0378.033]

| Order | Offer | Price | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Front end | The 4 Foot Farm ebook | $7 | Entry purchase |
| One-click upsell after purchase | Printed version of the book | $9 | Congruent add-on |
| Upsell | Seed subscription for vegetables | $27 per month | Recurring value |
| Upsell | Newsletter subscription | $20 per month | Recurring value |
| Main upsell | Physical growing system for up to 100 pounds of food in a 4 by 4 foot space | $497 | About 2% of ebook buyers took it |
| Add-on to the $497 product | Lifetime warranty | $100 | Protects the main purchase |

[L0378.034]

### Timing and channel

- An upsell does not have to happen at checkout. It can be offered straight after the first purchase or later, when the customer returns, so planning should cover both timings. Older (2021-2022). [L0448.025]
- If the checkout must stay short, place upsells and cross-sells in post-purchase email or SMS instead of inside the checkout flow. This keeps the path to payment to one or two steps while still offering additional products. [L0387.033]
- Before capturing payment for an upsell that ships from your own location:
  1. Email the customer and state the expected delivery time of 10 to 14 days.
  2. Ask the customer to confirm that they still want the order.
  3. Once the customer confirms, capture the payment.

  [L0966.055]

- Full upsell sequence on a store:
  1. The product page shows a comparison chart where a higher-end version fits.
  2. After Add to Cart, the replace-item pop-up offers the upgrade.
  3. The cart shows either the original or the replaced item.
  4. At checkout, the customer is offered an extended warranty and intangible products.
  5. The shipping upgrade is offered before the purchase is completed.
  6. After purchase, a phone call or message thanks the buyer and offers a relevant upgrade if it fits.

  [L1076.050]

### Free-plus-shipping and trial offers

- Free-plus-shipping is the favorite offer for qualifying buyers. Give a valuable product for free and charge only shipping. This gets it into a new customer's hands and reveals which subscribers are also buyers. It is the simplest entry offer for most physical or digital products. [L0523.084]
- Pattern: a free-plus-shipping offer charges the customer only the shipping cost for the main product, so paid ads aim for near-zero net acquisition cost per buyer. The brand recovers its margin later through upsells and add-on products sold to that customer. Older (2021-2022). [L0511.022]
- Example: a $2 product with $2 in marketing cost sold as free plus $6.95 shipping. The $4 cost is covered by the shipping fee, so the campaign is cash-flow positive. [L0643.010]
- Setup: a common Zipify OCU setup makes the main product free and sets shipping to whatever cost covers your CAC or profit target. [L1299.012]
- Example from a Zipify OCU setup: the main product was free with $13 shipping, which produced +$7 revenue per visitor at a conversion rate of about 30%. [L1299.013]
- Conflict on shipping fee: the $6.95 example [L0643.010] and the $13 example [L1299.013] both use free product with shipping fees; the right fee is the one that covers your CAC or profit target for your own product. [L0643.010, L1299.012, L1299.013]
- About 30 percent of the people who took a free-plus-shipping offer bought something else immediately, so the next offer is the easiest sale. Present a related product on the post-purchase page or send buyers to the store. [L0643.039]
- Trial offer: charge a very low amount, most often 1 USD, and bill the full price a few days later unless the buyer cancels. It forces the visitor to enter a credit card and so proves they are a buyer. It works best for digital downloads, software and membership sites, which need no shipping. [L0523.085]
- Post-purchase upsells in Zipify OCU may not display in multi-country setups unless the markets are configured correctly. In the US, the upsell works well without this extra step. [L1299.014]

### Checkout add-ons

#### Bumps

- Bumps are small add-on offers placed on the order form before checkout. They work like impulse items at a supermarket checkout and can change the transaction profitably. Bumps are used to identify and sell to hyperactive buyers at the point of sale. [L0523.090]

#### Tips

- Customers may be willing to add a tip at checkout when they are happy with the store. A tip field can be added as a default in the Shopify checkout to test this. [L0774.043]

#### Shipping protection

- Shipping protection is priced at 3 dollars per order in this Kaching Cart setup. Its cart description reads as protection against the order being lost or damaged. [L0560.023]
- While testing a product, leave out shipping protection and the footer. When scaling, you may offer shipping protection as an opt-in choice, since customers should be able to decide whether to add it. [L0560.024]

#### Warranties

- Use a warranty as the upsell on expensive items. When the warranty is offered as a single checkbox at checkout and the customer does not tick it, send an automatic email shortly after the purchase with the warranty offer and a discount. This works best when the item costs enough that the customer is likely to want protection. [L0334.063]
- At checkout, offer an extended warranty as an add-on the customer can select during the process. [L1076.042]
- Put the upsell offer with the highest chance of acceptance, such as an extended warranty or expedited shipping on expensive items, into the Shopify product upsell app as the post-cart offer. Place other lower-priority add-ons on the product page as selectable options. [L0923.050]

#### Shipping upgrades and priority options

- Offer a shipping upgrade as a single radio button, such as priority domestic shipping in 2 to 3 days, while the standard free shipping shows its average delivery time. [L1076.042]
- A post-purchase priority shipping or priority processing option can add extra margin. Charge about 10 percent of the product price as a small fee, for example $2.95 to $3.95 depending on the price point. [L1382.019]

#### Testing three upsell types

- Test three post-purchase upsell options, depending on the product, offer and entry point:
  1. Direct upsell to the high-ticket item with a discount or warranty.
  2. Quantity increase, for example try 3 or 6 bottles instead of 1.
  3. Full-store discount for the first order, sent immediately after the purchase with a short time limit (in the example, within the next hour).

  In the example, the full-store discount performed best at the time. [L0334.019]

### Apps and setup

- Upsell app setup: after checkout, add an upsell page that offers additional products at a discount, using the app AfterSell, so that average order value increases. [L1131.009]
- Zipify OCU is a post-purchase upsell app. [L1299.012]
- Offer editor settings for upsells in Source D setup:
  - Set Free Shipping as the default. In the offer editor, enable the Free Shipping option and the setting Hide shipping line if the shipping amount is 0.00, so no shipping line appears. [L1141.009]
  - Upsell Product block: Recommendation Type can be Recommended Products (OCU) or a set product; Autoselect variant chooses the variant automatically; the purchase option can be One-time purchase or Subscribe and save. [L1141.012]
  - Subscription section: you can hide the offer whenever the order includes subscription products, turn on the subscription option, or show a subscription offer only when the customer first bought the product as a one-time purchase. [L1141.013]
- ReconvertUpsell: a funnel offer can be changed under the discounts and shipping section, with percentage or fixed amount per unit, the timer, and the button text, for example *Grab another for 15% off* or *Pay now*. Post-purchase offer example line: *It's not too late to grab another for 15% off*. [L1410.025]
- The standard thank-you page after checkout shows order confirmation, shipment tracking and delivery updates. A post-purchase offer page is inserted before it, so the buyer still reaches the order details after the offer. [L1411.026]
- Post-purchase offer pages render differently on desktop or laptop and on phones. Preview and check the offer page on both before launch, because the layout, buttons and visible deal can differ between the two. [L1411.027]

### Offer copy

- Post-purchase upsell right after checkout, with a lower price and urgency copy. Buyers are still excited right after buying, so an add-on shown then is more likely to be accepted. [L1207.044]
- Template:

```
Before you go, today only: add [product name] to your order for [discounted price]. Once this window closes, this offer is gone.
```

[L1207.045]

- Post-purchase upsell copy with a time limit, such as a *before you go today only* offer at a lower price, raises the buyer's excitement right after the purchase. The offer should state that the deal disappears after the limited time. [L1209.030]
- Post-purchase upsell: after the buyer completes a purchase, offer an additional product with *today only* copy that makes the offer look cheaper and time-limited. This uses the buyer's momentum right after the decision. [L1210.022]

### Abandoned-cart offers

- Use the fourth abandoned-cart email for an offer, such as 10% off, while keeping the goal of completing the purchase. If the cart is below the free shipping threshold, such as $100, offer free shipping to push it over, then suggest complementary products that can be added to the order. [L1208.015]
- For abandoned cart recovery, offer a discount of around 10 percent rather than a large discount. A moderate offer recovers the sale without training shoppers to wait for deep discounts. [L1251.042]

### Testing and measurement

- A/B test post-purchase upsells every day. A profitable upsell can cover the loss on the front-end sale, and everything after that is profit. [L1299.015]
- After a single-product purchase, a post-purchase offer accepted by only about 3 out of 100 buyers was treated as a low rate. Measure the acceptance rate of each post-purchase offer separately so weak offers are visible. [L0693.057]
- Judge each upsell by revenue per offer shown, and test a discounted same-item offer first before adding complementary products. [L0378.025, L1410.024]
- Test the three upsell types (direct high-ticket, quantity increase, full-store discount) and compare results per entry point. [L0334.019]

## Value ladders, subscriptions and repeat purchase

### Value ladders: structure and principles

- A value ladder puts value on the vertical axis and price on the horizontal axis, so the highest-value, highest-price offer sits in the top right corner. Each rung must give customers real value so they naturally want to move up. Build the ladder before building any sales funnel. [L0523.006]
- A stranger will not pay a top-tier price before receiving value from you. A cheap, low-risk entry offer lets people experience your value first, and the higher rungs then sell themselves. Never lead with your most expensive offer to cold prospects. [L0523.007]
- Most businesses have only one or two parts of a complete value ladder. Fill the gaps by adding a frontend bait offer at the bottom and premium backend services at the top. A missing rung usually explains why growth stalls. [L0523.010]
- The frontend offer must be attractive enough to get people through the door, not just a necessary service. A dull entry offer for something people buy only when in pain keeps the business struggling. Choose a popular free or low-cost entry that draws traffic, then upgrade customers to the core service. [L0523.013]
- Every value ladder needs a continuity offer you can bill every month, such as software, a membership or ongoing coaching. Recurring billing creates residual income that keeps the business stable and gives customers a reason to stay in the system. [L0523.009]
- Build the ladder so that each rung gives the customer a clear next step. Keep adding higher-value tiers, because there is no natural ceiling on what people will buy. [L0523.012]

#### Example ladders

| Business | Frontend (bait) | Middle rung | Backend (top rung) | Continuity |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Dental practice | Free teeth cleaning | Teeth-whitening kit and custom retainers (over 2,000 USD in one visit) | Cosmetic surgery (about 10,000 USD or more) | Return appointment booked six months later |
| Chiropractic clinic (rebuilt) | Free massage by therapists | Spinal adjustment (about 50 USD each) | Wellness program at 5,000 USD, about ten times more value per visit | Ongoing visits, a few times per week over several months |
| Custom suit maker | Free cufflinks given away online | Entry-level custom service (the original front offer was about 2,000 USD) | Custom suits | Not specified |

[L0523.008]

| Rung | Example offer | Price |
|---|---|---|
| Entry bait | Free book of 108 split test winners | Pay 9.95 USD shipping only |
| Mid-level program | Ignite program | 10,000 USD |
| Upper program | Inner Circle program | 25,000 USD |
| Top program | Million-dollar program | 1,000,000 USD in total |
| Continuity | Monthly software subscription (example: ClickFunnels) | Billed monthly |

[L0523.138]

- A low-price digital product can be extended up the ladder with do-it-yourself plans and a physical version of the same item. A digital guide to building a homemade novelty gadget, for example, can be paired with plans and the finished physical product. Each added piece gives buyers another step up. [L0523.011]
- In one group of high-ticket members, nine of eighteen people bought an additional 30,000 USD offer when it was presented to them that same evening. A percentage of any audience will always pay for premium access. [L0523.012]
- Premium access can also be earned rather than bought at once. Pair a low-cost starter product with a premium item that the buyer earns through a small per-unit credit, such as 5 cents per pound of product bought, until the premium is paid off. Repeat purchases then fund the premium while the customer keeps buying. [L1261.140]

#### Case studies

- A chiropractic clinic charged about 50 USD per adjustment. Each visit lasted about ten minutes, and the patient paid and left, with no other offers beyond occasional multi-visit continuity plans. The owner mapped a complete value ladder and created a wellness program at 5,000 USD, giving clients about ten times more value per visit, which became the top rung. Because a standard adjustment was not an attractive entry point, the clinic added free massages as bait. Two full-time massage therapists gave the massages and were trained to spot spinal or rib misalignment, then upgrade clients to adjustments and to the wellness program. After the full ladder was in place the business turned around noticeably; no revenue figure was given. [L0523.014]
- A dentist offered a free teeth cleaning through a mailed postcard to a new patient. During the appointment he noticed yellowing and offered custom whitening trays, then noticed the lower teeth shifting and offered retainers. Together these cost the patient over 2,000 USD in less than an hour. Cosmetic surgery, which would have cost 10,000 USD or more, sits at the top of his ladder. He also booked the patient for a return visit six months later, adding the patient to a continuity program on the way out. [L0523.015]
- A jewelry brand built a product ladder of three kits. A decision kit serves buyers who do not know the shape. A comparison kit serves buyers who know the shape but not the size. A confirmation kit lets buyers see the final choice on their hand before buying the main product. Each kit moves the buyer to the next stage of the funnel. [L1016.043]
- A try-on decision kit priced at $156 is credited toward the full purchase. This lowers the risk for undecided buyers and moves them into the next step of the buying process. [L1016.042]

### Front-end entry offers

#### Low-cost entry products (older source)

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* The front-end offer funnel uses a low-cost entry product, sold near break-even, to attract customers already buying a competing category. The brand then recovers margin through a sample, a discount card or an add-on product in the same package. Formula: entry product sold at cost or below plus a path to the core product. It works because the customer acquisition cost is paid back by the repeat purchase of the core item. Example 1: a supplement brand gives a travel sample of a shake inside a reusable bottle. Example 2: a skincare brand ships a mini serum in a sleeve with a 15 percent code for the full size. [L0511.009]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Protein powder funnel case: a reusable container that holds protein powder on a carabiner costs about $3 to make and about $5 to get into the marketplace. It sells for $10 and is designed to break even at $5 per unit. A sample packet and a store discount card are included, so the buyer enters the brand's customer base at roughly no net acquisition cost. [L0511.010]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A low-cost reusable container for protein powder can be made from a 20-ounce soda bottle. Cut off the top, seal the cut edge and attach a screw cap so the container can hang from a carabiner. This kind of cheap, useful add-on can serve as an entry product sold near cost. [L0511.026]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A product customers buy before the main product, such as a protective or storage accessory for an item in the same industry, can serve as a lead magnet. Its job is to bring in future buyers who can then be remarketed to. [L1152.008]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A low-cost accessory can carry a printed insert or postcard recommending the main product, such as a pellet grill, and listing its benefits. The card can link to a content page, which moves a buyer of a basic version toward the upgrade. [L1152.024]

#### Trip wires and free-plus-shipping

- Use a trip wire to filter out freebie seekers. Instead of a free bribe, charge a small amount such as 7 dollars, or place a low-price payment step at the bribe or on the next page. Visitors who only want free information leave, while buyers stay on your list. [L0514.033]
- A trial product can be given away for free with the buyer paying only the shipping. The payment covers the product cost while contact data is collected. The buyer is then moved to a larger paid offer, so the first step is a lead capture, not a profit source. This works best when the free item is cheap to ship and the follow-up offer is clearly relevant. [L0919.039]
- Make a buyer qualifying offer immediately after the opt-in, not days later. The offer should be a no-brainer priced around 5 to 7 USD or sold as free-plus-shipping, so nearly every subscriber can say yes. Once buyers are identified, keep the subscriber list and buyer list separate and market to each differently. [L0523.061]

#### Buyer offer types

- A tripwire is a smaller offer that is a splinter of the core product, such as one module or training session at a large discount. It usually costs around 7 USD per item and can be physical or digital depending on the market. Its purpose is to turn subscribers into buyers quickly. [L0523.086]
- A self-liquidating offer costs between 37 and 97 USD and is designed to cover the cost of the ads that brought the buyer in. Upsells then become profit. Free-plus-shipping, trials and tripwires often lose money initially, while a self-liquidating offer is meant to break even on its own. [L0523.087]
- A straight sale is a regular sale of a high-ticket item priced from 97 USD to 5,000 USD or more. It requires more selling, so it is usually offered only to warm traffic or to buyers who have already been through earlier funnels. Stronger trust is needed before buyers pay these prices. [L0523.088]
- Do not assume one buyer offer works for every product. A supplement that failed to convert with free-plus-shipping succeeded as a straight sale. Test each product with different buyer qualification offers before giving up on a market. [L0523.089]
- Hyperactive buyers are people in an urgent state of need who purchase several related items at once. They are the highest-value segment of a buyer list, so identify them fast and present several upsells and downsells while their need is strong. Interest fades within days, so the window is short. [L0523.062]

#### Upsell paths after the first order

- A supplement brand case study: base product at $9.99, average order value of $99.99 (10x markup). Structure: buy base at $9.99, then a first upsell offer at $30 (additional supplement plus brain-capacity PDF) for $39.99 total, then free shipping at a $49 threshold, then a second upsell offer at $500 (8-week coaching program, one-to-one VA accountability sessions and money-back guarantee). Total revenue exceeded $250,000 from $9,000 in ad spend. The domain was bought in October and list building started late October, still during Black Friday. The email list grew to 180,000 addresses over time, enabling plug-and-play launches. [L0307.009]

### Continuity and subscriptions

#### Subscription principles

- Build subscription and recurring offers so revenue continues after the first purchase, instead of depending only on one-time sales. [L0914.012]
- Subscriptions turn one-time buyers into repeat buyers by creating recurring purchases. They are a starting point for raising customer lifetime value, not a complete strategy on their own. [L1232.003]
- Subscription options such as monthly, every two months or every three months can be offered next to a more expensive one-time purchase. This pushes repeat orders and raises customer lifetime value. [L1131.008]
- Upsells, cross-sells, win-back messages and customer lifetime value are essential when a store only breaks even. Raising product prices where possible is also a key lever for creating real profit. [L1215.031]

#### Subscribe and save: setup

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A subscribe-and-save option was shown next to the one-time price, making the saving visible: one-time $12 versus $10 with subscribe and save 17%. The same store labeled a pack SAVE 17% and offered a Travel Sample Pack, a Build Your Pack option and a seasonal collection. [L0409.016]
- Subscribe and Save offer on the product page: 3, 6 or 9 units per delivery, with the 6-unit option preselected as the default. Example: six bars at $42 against $8 per single bar, a saving of about 12.5% on a six-pack. [L0524.023]
- Set the subscription to repeat every three months on the product page, and make the subscription the default choice. This makes the repeat purchase the easy path. [L0524.024]
- Show the subscription saving as an explicit currency amount next to the quantity selector, for example "save £5 every month". A concrete monthly figure is easier to grasp than a percentage and makes the subscription look like a clear deal. [L0919.038]
- On subscription pack pages, a competitor placed a "Flexible delivery schedule" note near the add-to-cart button. A visible note like this tells the customer that recurring deliveries can be changed, which makes the recurring offer easier to accept before checkout. [L1164.056]
- Subscribe-and-save offers are common in competitor listings and can be used to show a lower effective price per period. Compare subscription and one-time options together when you decide whether to offer a subscription. [L1167.014]

#### Fit: which products suit subscriptions

- Subscribe-and-save only fits products that customers reorder regularly, such as daily supplements (e.g. AG1 / Athletic Greens). Avoid it for products with no repeat purchase need, because it does not suit most customers. [L1404.024]
- Subscribe-and-save only fits products that customers reorder regularly, such as daily supplements. A related retainer model gives subscribers ongoing services or discounts over time, similar to a membership, and suits products with a steady repeat need. [L1404.036]
- Seasonal or recurring packages of consumables and recipes, shipped on a schedule and linked to holidays and sporting events, suit niches where customers need regular refills. Build the subscription only after the content audience is already engaged. [L0487.051]
- A physical subscription can be a monthly calendar sold only to US buyers, with a new design on each month's page that the buyer can hang up. The planned price was about $19 to $20 per month. Offering it after a first set purchase turns one-time buyers into recurring revenue. [L1153.080]

### Memberships and exclusive programs

- Membership example: an exclusive membership for $129 gives 25% off every order, 10% cash back, free shipping and a free sample pack, so the total stacked value looks far above the fee. [L0919.024]
- Exclusive memberships and loyalty programs: customers join, sometimes for a fee, and receive exclusive deals in return. This is another offer model to consider alongside the others. [L1404.029]
- Exclusive rewards, such as cashback, early access to special products or other member benefits, are used to bring customers back for more purchases. They reward repeat buying rather than the first order. [L1232.005]

### Gift cards

#### Gift card as a front-end or upsell product

- Sell gift cards at $5 each during Q4; production cost is about $0.50. The margin is 90%, making this one of the highest-profit SKUs during holiday sales. [L0309.009]
- Gift cards were offered on the store but not pushed, and sales from them were weak, so they did not deliver meaningful extra revenue. Focus on discounts and bundles first. [L0519.035]
- Gift card upsells work in two ways: they lift the average order value on the current purchase, and they make a second order more likely because the customer holds a credit to use later. [L1413.010]

#### Discounted post-purchase gift cards

- Post-purchase gift cards can be sold as a separate product in the thank-you email, for example a $50 card sold for $20 to $40. The customer gets a saving and is likely to return, while the store's extra margin on the second order covers the discount on the card. [L0605.030]
- Product margins should be at least about 20% so that a discounted gift card or upsell does not put the store at a loss. Check this margin before offering gift cards. [L0605.031]
- A discounted gift card gives the customer a saving equal to the card's face value minus the price paid. For example, $50 for $20 saves $30, and $50 for $40 saves $10. The store receives the price paid up front, so the margin on the products the customer later buys must cover that discount. Check that the product margin is at least about 20% before offering a larger discount. [L0605.053]

#### When each applies

- Use the $5 Q4 card where the margin is near 90% and gift cards are actively promoted during holiday sales. [L0309.009, L0519.035]
- Where gift cards are not being pushed, fix promotion first, because unpromoted cards produced weak sales; focus on discounts and bundles first. [L0519.035]
- Use discounted post-purchase cards only when the product margin is at least about 20%. [L0605.030, L0605.031, L0605.053]

#### Accounting

- *Older (Source A, 2021):* A spend-and-get gift card promotion needs an accounting check. Ask a CPA how unredeemed gift cards are reported, because they can inflate revenue in the next year if not handled correctly. [L1090.080]

### Post-purchase offers

- Offer existing customers an extra 10-20% off other products in the thank-you email. Because the acquisition cost was already paid, purchases by the same customer add profit with no extra acquisition cost. [L0605.032]
- Choose the size of a post-purchase reward based on what the margin can afford, whether a dollar amount such as $15 off or a percentage such as 15% off. The reward is for the next order, so it should not erase the profit on the current one. [L0476.042]
- After a first purchase you have the buyer's address, phone number and email, so you can follow up with direct mail or a phone call to offer a related product. Winning the first sale is usually the hardest step, and later sales to existing buyers are easier. [L0643.067]
- A supplement seller sold a protein shaker on Amazon, then noticed that buyers wanted a way to carry their meals. The seller released a meal preparation box and sold it to the existing customer database instead of on Amazon. A meal carry bag that also fits the shaker followed later and was sold to the same buyers, so each new product built on the first purchase. [L0643.068]
- Promotions to existing audiences generate sales without new traffic, which adds value to the business. Run them consistently once sales and remarketing audiences are growing, not as an early-stage tactic. [L1205.027]
- Target a bundle offer at leads who viewed the product one, two or three months earlier and have not bought. Their standard remarketing has already run its course, and a new package gives them a reason to return. [L1205.029]
- Flexible bundles help customers discover a new favorite product and can raise average order value. Bundles work best when they are easy to change and are presented as a way to find products the customer will reorder. [L1232.004]

### Retroactive offers

- Starting point: a free digital guide was given away with the product, and some buyers paid 19.95 USD for it. Later, previous buyers who had skipped the add-on were offered the guide free with a "you missed it" message. Reviews from these buyers were five stars. [L0773.064]
- Retroactive value offer: after a free add-on has been tested at a small price, past customers who skipped it can be offered the same item for free. This recovers sales from people who did not buy the first time and builds goodwill. Example: a free sizing chart offered to customers who ordered a month earlier. Example: a free care guide sent to buyers who ignored the upsell at checkout. [L0773.065]

### Referral programs

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Create an automated referral program with associated emails. Depending on your email platform, the program can be linked to loyalty or operate separately. [L0203.010]
- Referral reward structure: for each referral, the friend gets $10 off and the referrer gets $10 back. The reward is valid through five referrals, and the fifth referral earns a $75 coupon, which is roughly break-even when the product sells for $85. [L0790.025]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Without a loyalty point system, a store can give a customer $100 off the next order for each successful referral. Alternatively, the store can pay $100 for each referral made. [L1234.011]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A referral program can be tied in so that referrers earn bonuses. Not every customer will use it, but it adds extra value to the customer journey. [L1522.028]

#### When each applies

- The $10 friend and $10 referrer structure with a five-referral cap fits a product sold at around $85, where the fifth-referral coupon stays near break-even. [L0790.025]
- The flat $100 per referral model fits a store without a loyalty point system; it can be run as credit on the next order or as a payment. [L1234.011]

### Loyalty and points programs

#### Program design (older source)

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Build an automated loyalty program using email marketing templates entered into your email system to reward repeat purchases and encourage customer retention. [L0203.009]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Loyalty points should be given for more than purchases. Common earning actions are creating an account, joining the email list, leaving a product review, making an additional purchase, and referring a friend or family member. [L0956.002]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Most loyalty programs are points-based, where customers redeem points for a fixed dollar amount off a purchase. The app handles earning and redemption automatically, so the store owner only needs to set the rules and values. [L0956.004]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Reward actions that create value for the store, such as leaving reviews, not only purchases. A good review can earn points, which encourages more social proof at little cost. [L0956.013]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Credit loyalty points already on a customer's first order, not only from the second purchase onward. This lets the customer see the program working immediately, and the first program message can report a balance they already hold. [L0956.015]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A loyalty program can reward each purchase with store credit or with a points system that shoppers redeem for a discount. [L1522.028]

#### Economics

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A loyalty purchase carries no paid acquisition cost, so the normal cost per acquisition does not apply to it. When deciding how much reward to offer, add back the acquisition cost saved on a first-time sale, then check the result against margins. [L0956.005]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Do not reduce the reward on each individual order to save money. Loyalty rewards are judged across the whole customer relationship, including referrals and reviews, so penny-pinching on single orders can cut the program's value. [L0956.012]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* A repeat order with almost no margin can still be worth it if the customer refers three new buyers, since each referred first order brings in full-price revenue. When judging a reward, compare its cost with the total revenue from the referrals and reviews it produces, not with the repeat order alone. [L0956.017]

#### When to build a rewards program

- *Older (Source A, 2022):* A rewards or points program is worth considering only when customers buy several times per year. For products bought every two to five years, the program is rarely used. [L1092.056]
- *Older (Source A, 2022):* A rewards program should only be built when repeat buying is frequent, because the value of points is forgotten between long purchase cycles. [L1092.103]

### Repeat business and customer research

#### Customer research

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Customer research helps determine which upsells to offer, when to offer them, and how to generate repeat business from existing customers. [L0872.011]
- Test each product with different buyer qualification offers before deciding a market will not work. Track which offers convert for each product. [L0523.089]

#### Survey rewards

- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Offer a small reward for completing the survey, typically around $10 to $15. It does not need to be large, since the goal is to thank customers for their time. [L1052.009]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* If you run a loyalty program, reward survey completion with loyalty points, because points encourage a return purchase. Without a loyalty program, use a one-off discount code, a cash-back offer on the order, or an Amazon gift card. [L1052.010]
- *Older (Source B, 2021-22):* Cash-back and gift card rewards do not keep the money in your store, since customers may spend them elsewhere. Rewards that work inside your shop, such as loyalty points that can be used on upsell offers, drive repeat sales. [L1052.011]

#### High-value repeat customers

- Reward the best customers with a $50 coupon to encourage a fourth or fifth pair purchase; the discounted sale still carried about a 50% margin. Target this group as a VIP list, for example the top 700 customers who had bought four or more pairs. [L0790.026]
- Hyperactive buyers, identified fast after purchase, are the highest-value segment of a buyer list and should receive several upsells and downsells while their need is strong. [L0523.062]
- Keep subscriber and buyer lists separate, and market to each differently. [L0523.061]

## Risk reversal, guarantees and payment plans

### Risk reversal: the principle and the decision rule

- Risk reversal moves the risk of a purchase from the customer to the seller. Buyers are risk-averse, so leaving all the risk with them lowers conversion. Risk reversal can take the form of a returns policy, a price-match guarantee, another satisfaction guarantee, or a worry-free installation or pre-assembled delivery for products that need setup. [L0411.015]
- A money-back guarantee is the strongest offer element because perceived risk is the main purchase objection. It makes any offer more attractive, provided the supplier is reliable and product quality is good. [L0683.010]
- Treat risk reversal as a must-have for making a lot of money in high-ticket dropshipping, because the customer must be protected if something goes wrong. [L0414.019]
- Place the guarantee after the value stack, so it reinforces a strong offer. Stack the value first, then remove the risk with a money-back guarantee or another risk reversal, such as a statement that it costs nothing if the buyer is not satisfied. [L1365.039]
- Make the guarantee the main headline when the guarantee is strong and the offer is clear. A guarantee boosts response, and the better the guarantee, the better the response. Strong guarantees outperform weak ones, even though some marketers claim guarantees are outdated. [L1370.077]
- Guarantees must have no caveats if you make a big claim. A lowest-price guarantee should only exclude things like secondhand products. Every extra rule or state-specific exception makes the whole offer look weak. [L0411.019]
- A guarantee is more convincing when it comes from someone the buyer knows and trusts, such as a local dealer who signs a written pledge to refund. A distant, unknown company's guarantee is less persuasive. [L1261.098]
- Guarantees are not limited to products. They can apply to service levels such as lowest fare, maximum downtime or a fixed meal service time. [L1370.080]

### Money-back and trial guarantees

#### Basic structure

- Offer clear refund guarantees and money-back policies within a specified timeframe. If customers are unsatisfied, they receive a full refund. Guarantees eliminate purchase risk and build trust, directly increasing perceived value. [L0233.008]
- Basic money-back guarantee: refund for any reason if the buyer is not fully satisfied. Use livelier wording such as "delighted" or "thrilled". If it is true, say why no competitor offers the same guarantee. [L1370.078]
- Refund and keep the premium: the buyer can cancel, get a full refund and keep a free bonus gift. This makes the guarantee feel generous and certain. Redundancy also helps, so state the refund promise two or three times in different words, such as "a full refund of every penny paid". [L1370.079]
- A 30-day money-back guarantee reverses the buyer's risk. Loss aversion makes this guarantee strong, because the shopper does not feel the purchase can go wrong. [L1207.037]
- Pattern: "[number] day money back guarantee, [condition or reassurance]". A clear time limit turns a risky purchase into a reversible one. Examples: "60 day money back guarantee, no questions asked" and "14 day money back guarantee on every order". [L1207.038]
- Pattern: a money-back guarantee tied to a specific short time and effort, such as "results in X minutes a day or your money back". It cuts the perceived time to results, reduces the effort required and lowers buying risk, which raises the value of the offer. Use it only after the product has been tested and its quality is confirmed.
  - Example 1: "Ease your morning stiffness in 5 minutes a day or your money back."
  - Example 2: "See a cleaner kitchen in 2 weeks of 10 minutes a day, or get a full refund." [L0871.042]

#### Trial periods and free examination

- A trial period with a refund, such as three to four days or one week, lowers the buyer's risk and raises perceived value. Offer it only when the business can afford the refunds, and design the terms to keep the refund rate as low as possible. [L0502.010]
- Offer a free examination period of 6 days, one week, 10 days or 30 days before the buyer pays. The seller pays shipping both ways, so returning the product costs nothing. Make clear that the buyer owes nothing unless he keeps the product. [L1369.009]
- A free trial with a return window, such as returning the product for a full refund within 90 days, turns the guarantee into a no-risk trial. [L1370.080]
- A 100-day free trial or lifetime warranty can work for furniture-type brands. Avoid a free-trial offer for a low-cost product that breaks often or for a stingy customer base, because it causes problems. [L1404.027]
- Offer a service the buyer can test before paying, such as samples, a week of use or approval shipments, instead of asking for a purchase up front. Let the customer prove the product's claims at no cost or risk. [L1261.086]
  - Case: a sewing motor was offered for a one-week home trial with a technician who showed how to use it. About nine in ten trials led to a sale. The offer removed the risk of buying and let the product sell itself. [L1261.087]
  - A "try, then pay" offer converts far better than a "try, then return if not satisfied" offer. The customer's decision to keep is already made once they have used the product. Where possible, ship first and bill after use. [L1261.088]

#### Results-based and no-cost guarantees

- Guarantee a result with a no-saving-no-cost offer. Ship a full trial batch at your own expense and charge only if the buyer's measured saving meets the stated level, such as 25 percent. If the saving falls short, the buyer keeps the trial at no cost. [L1369.008]
- Just-pay-shipping offers (product free at checkout, customer pays shipping) can work, although they look scammy and need careful brand positioning. A variant requires a minimum-spend subscription after the free product, which produces recurring revenue and encourages use. [L1404.028]

#### Refund-on-request

- Offer a refund only when a buyer contacts you and says the content is not valuable. Do not heavily advertise the guarantee. [L0513.021]

### Guarantee wording and proof

- Vague guarantees such as "satisfaction guaranteed" fail. They set no customer expectation and seem risk-free but are not credible. Specific guarantees such as "30-night money-back" or "7-day results or your money back" work because they communicate confidence and clarity. [L0313.010]
- Example: the Posture Pro Corrector offered a 30-night money-back guarantee at a $39.99 price point. The ad claimed relief from back and neck pain in as little as 7 days. This removed purchase friction by offering a full refund if the benefit timeline was not met. The lower price ($39.99) than the discounted version ($49.99 regular, 20% off) suggests the offer is positioned as a trial price. [L0313.015]
- Timeframe logic for Posture Pro: the 30-night money-back period is set against a claimed benefit of "as little as 7 days". This lets the customer verify the benefit well before the refund deadline. [L0313.020]
- A 90-day money-back guarantee with no questions asked was shown on the landing page and used in the ads as a risk reversal. The guarantee is a USP that sits next to the mechanism and the price. [L0374.027]
- Competitor pages can differ in guarantee length. One page advertised a 90-day 100% money-back guarantee and another a 365-day money-back guarantee. Guarantee length is an offer element you can test against conversion rate. [L1164.055]
- A guarantee tied to a recognized seal or certification should state a clear refund or replacement window, such as two years. A concrete time limit reads as more credible than an open-ended promise. [L0340.082]
- Case: a seller mailed about 2,000 packages with a guarantee to pay $25, or donate $50 to charity, if the recipient read the whole letter, watched the video, listened to the audio and still felt it was a waste of time. The guarantee cost roughly $100,000 at most if every claim was paid. The mailing produced a return of 10 to 15 appointments per 100 letters, and 2 to 3 of those converted into clients with about $10,000 first-year value each. [L1370.081]
- Case: a seminar letter guaranteed the content. The author paid out less than $200 in claims from mailing to nearly 4,000 prospects and earned over $100,000 in profit. Split tests showed that adding a letter guarantee increases readership and response. [L1370.082]

### Landing page reassurance

- Lower perceived risk on the landing page by stating visible reassurance, such as a free trial period, easy cancellation, a money-back guarantee, and clear answers about returns, refunds, help and what happens if the product is not wanted. Place these near the hero and the offer. [L0918.007]
- For subscriptions, use an "easy cancel" message that states there is no contract and that the customer keeps control. Flexible subscription plans lower the fear of being trapped. [L0919.028]

### Returns and restocking fees

#### Matching return policies to suppliers

- Return policy length should match each supplier's return policy. A 30-day return window can be offered on products priced from $1,000 to $2,500 only when the supplier accepts returns. (2020) [L0664.057]
- Cap the return window rather than offering unlimited returns. Match the return policy to the supplier's policy, for example 30 days when the supplier allows 30 days. (2019, older) [L1069.053]
- Your return policy does not have to match a competitor's policy. If the supplier does not accept returns for a product, set that product as not eligible for returns. (2018, older) [L1085.071]

#### Restocking fees

- Set the restocking fee 10 to 15 percent above the supplier's restocking charge, to cover return shipping costs. Check competitors first, since not charging a fee may affect conversions. (2023) [L1094.080]
- Return shipping fees can become the customer's responsibility if the return policy says so. Check how top competitors word their return policies first. (2023) [L1094.081]
- Before matching competitors' restocking fees, which can reach 20 percent, or their upgraded delivery options, calculate your margins. Match those offers only if the margin still works after the cost. (2021, older) [L0801.045]
- Mistake: advertising a 30-day return or free returns while hiding a 25% restocking fee destroys trust. On a $1,000 product that fee is $250. Any return conditions that make the promise conditional must be disclosed plainly, or the promise should not be made. (2021-22, older) [L0411.018]
- Case: a seller changed the returns policy with little else changing, and monthly revenue rose from about $40,000 to about $80,000. The source notes that this scale of increase is not typical and that other businesses showed much smaller gains. (2021-22, older) [L0411.016]

### Return fee options: Checkout Plus

#### Fee levels

- Average US return fees are around $8 per return. The speaker estimated Australian fees at about $5 AUD but said this needed double-checking, so treat that figure as unverified. [L0955.006]
- A common Checkout Plus baseline is a pre-purchase fee of $2.98, against a later return fee of $10. These two figures are a starting point that suits many brands, not a fixed rule. [L0955.007]
- In the demo, the unlock fee was $1.98 on a $600 snowboard (total $601.98). Without it, a return triggered a $12.00 handling fee. The store can exclude certain items from the service. [L0955.018]
- The right return fee depends on shipping rates, the product vertical and product weight, and on the brand's current return rate. Heavy or bulky products such as appliances or furniture can carry higher fees, and so can higher-AOV brands. [L0955.008]
- Test fee levels before choosing one, for example $2.99 against $3.99, and measure uptake. The vendor recommends an A/B test first if a brand is skeptical about adding a cart fee. [L0955.016]

#### How it works

- Checkout Plus is a pre-purchase option in the cart and checkout. The shopper pays a small fee to get a free return, avoiding a larger return fee later. Shoppers who do not opt in pay the normal return fee if they return. [L0955.009]
- Checkout Plus can be framed as peace of mind rather than insurance. The shopper pays a small fee now to make sure a later return does not cost $10 to $15. [L0955.013]
- Checkout Plus can be configured as a flat or a dynamic fee, with many customisable options. Package protection can be bundled with it, and the bundled fee is higher to cover both protection and free returns. [L0955.014]
- Package protection is priced by cart value. The speaker's understanding is that it is about 2% of cart value, and the price rises when it is bundled with Checkout Plus. [L0955.015]
- The Checkout Plus option is never added to the cart automatically. If the shopper does not opt in, it is not added at checkout. This keeps the shopper's decision intact. [L0955.019]
- In the demo, the cart showed Checkout Plus at the bottom with the price and a details modal. [L0955.018]

Procedure:
1. The shopper clicks the Checkout Plus button in the cart. [L0955.010]
2. At checkout the option appears already selected or not, based on the cart choice, and the shopper can change it. [L0955.010]
3. If the shopper later returns and opted in, the return is covered and no handling fee applies. [L0955.010]
4. If the shopper did not opt in, the option still appears at checkout unselected, and a return triggers the normal return fee. [L0955.010]

#### Economics

- Example economics: with a 20% return rate and 50% of shoppers opting in, the pre-purchase fees can fully cover return software and return shipping costs. The vendor says it absorbs part of this cost too. [L0955.011]
- Pre-purchase revenue is roughly the share of shoppers who opt in, multiplied by the fee, compared against the share who actually return, multiplied by the return cost. Vendor data cited about 70% opt-in against typical return rates of 20-30%. The gap between opt-ins and returns covers return operations and often leaves a surplus. [L0955.012]
- Shoppers who pay the fee are not necessarily the ones who return. The opted-in cohort's return rate was slightly higher, and the non-opted cohort's was slightly lower, so the overall return rate stayed stable. [L0955.017]

### Price match and price guarantees

#### The MAP rule

- Suppliers that enforce minimum advertised price (MAP) forbid resellers from selling below a set price. Because of this, a price guarantee is low-risk, since competitors cannot legally undercut you and rarely offer a lower price. [L1130.004]
- Most online shoppers do not know that dropship suppliers enforce MAP, which requires all authorized retailers to advertise at the same price. Because customers are unaware of this constraint, they comparison-shop across competitors looking for discounts that legally cannot exist. This ignorance is why a price guarantee is such a powerful conversion tool: it appears to offer pricing assurance (which is genuinely true due to MAP) while creating almost no refund liability. [L0145.013]
- Price matching works with MAP products because all sellers keep the same price. With non-MAP products, a competitor can cut prices by a large amount at any time, and you would be forced to match a price where no margin is left. [L1051.011]
- A non-MAP competitor can cut its price by as much as 30% within a week. A price-match promise can force you to sell with no margin. Use price-match guarantees only when all sellers are bound by the same MAP, which keeps prices equal everywhere. [L1051.014]
- A price-match promise only makes sense in MAP markets, where retailers sell at an enforced common price, so matching costs little. Outside MAP, the same promise forces you to match competitors' prices on products you would sell at a loss. [L1146.014]

#### Which suppliers can be covered

- If your suppliers enforce MAP, offer a price-match policy and show it as a benefit on the site. Competitors often do not have one. [L0894.013]
- Price-match guarantees only apply to MAP suppliers. Do not offer one for suppliers without MAP. [L0963.064]
- Do not offer a price-match guarantee on non-MAP supplier products. Price-match guarantees only make sense with MAP pricing enforced. [L0962.101]
- Products from non-MAP suppliers should be excluded from any price-match guarantee you offer. [L0671.009, L0671.160]
- Products from non-MAP suppliers should be excluded from the price-match guarantee. A price-match guarantee on products without MAP can create conflicts with other sellers. [L0967.066]
- Exclude suppliers that do not enforce MAP from any price-match guarantee. Otherwise customers can force you to match a price that does not make money. (2022, older) [L1281.033]
- Do not offer a price-match policy on your website in markets without MAP. Customers could ask you to match prices on products where you would lose money, so the policy can only hurt you. [L1146.010]
- Suppliers without MAP can be excluded from the policy, and those product pages should not show it. [L0970.107]

#### When to offer, and when to drop it

- A price-match guarantee is optional. Competitors offering a six-month price guarantee can influence buyers, so a missing guarantee can cost sales. (2021, older) [L0671.051, L0671.190]
- A price-match policy can be left off, especially at launch, since customers who find a slightly lower price elsewhere may ask for a refund. (2023) [L0970.107]
- Remove the price-match policy when most of your suppliers do not enforce MAP. A few brand exclusions are acceptable, but if half or more suppliers are non-MAP, do not list the policy at all, because it causes confusion. (2023) [L0835.053]

#### Price-guarantee mechanics

- The price guarantee applies to the price shown on the website before any coupon code is applied. If a competitor is cheaper, the store matches that price, so the guarantee should state this clearly. [L1130.005]
- If you promise a price match, state it in one short line on the page and link to the full policy. The promise works as a trust element for resellers selling products that are also sold elsewhere. (2021-22, older) [L0409.046]
- A price-match policy, if offered, should be published on the product page so buyers can see it without leaving. [L1346.023]
- A price-match guarantee page promises that the store aims for the best price and will match a lower price a customer finds at another online store. It is one of the trust pages set up before launch, and the promise has to be one the store can actually keep. Write its conditions, such as the matching rules, only after the store has decided how it will handle them. (2021-22, older) [L1264.022]

### Warranties

- Contact your suppliers to ask about extended warranty options and their pricing. Extended warranties typically cost approximately $25 per product for one year of coverage. If your customers value warranty protection, include the extended warranty free with purchases to differentiate from competitors. [L0314.014]
- Offer a warranty for expensive items as a single checkbox at checkout. If the customer does not tick it, send an automatic email shortly after the purchase offering the warranty with a discount. [L0334.020]
- Extend.com is a Shopify app for selling extended warranties on high-ticket products. You earn a commission on each warranty sold. Extend.com handles all warranty fulfillment, claims processing and customer service. Source A members recommend it as a way to add revenue without additional operational complexity. (2023) [L0251.062]

### Discounts and payment methods

- Discounts and promo codes can be applied with every payment method, including installment providers such as Klarna. Do not exclude installment payments from the discount. (2021, older) [L1279.092]

### Financing options: Shop Pay, Klarna, Affirm, PayPal Credit

#### Shop Pay Installments (Shopify rules)

- Shop Pay Installments is available for orders from $35 up to $30,000. Orders below $35 do not qualify, and the financing limit for a single purchase is $30,000. [L1295.007]
- Conflicting activation threshold: one source states that Shop Pay requires a minimum customer order value of $40-50 to activate as a payment option, and that products under $30 do not qualify. The Shopify tier above lists $35. Check the threshold in your own store's settings before setting any product price or messaging. [L0324.018, L1295.007]
- For orders from $35 to $50, customers can pay in two interest-free bi-weekly installments of 50% of the total each. Customers can also simply pay the full amount at checkout. [L1295.008]
- For orders from $50 to $999, customers can pay in four interest-free bi-weekly installments. For example, an $800 order would be paid over about two months with no extra cost. [L1295.009]
- For orders from $150 up to $30,000, customers can pay in monthly installments. Interest rates depend on each customer's credit approval with Shopify. The merchant does not set or see these rates. [L1295.010]
- For orders above $25,000, the customer pays $5,000 upfront to Shopify, and the remaining balance is split into equal monthly installments that carry interest. This case is rare and mainly applies to large B2B orders. [L1295.011]
- Example: a hoodie priced at $44.50 falls in the $35 to $50 tier, so the product page shows an option to pay two interest-free installments of $22.25 each. Shoppers can ignore this and buy at full price, or choose the installment option at checkout. [L1295.012]
- The installment tiers and interest terms are preset by Shopify and cannot be changed by the merchant. The merchant decides only whether the option is switched on for the store. [L1295.013]

#### Shop Pay financing limit (older figures)

- Shop Pay financing limit varies by store. It was originally $500 and is now higher on many stores, but some stores still have lower limits. Check the financing limit before assuming it covers your average order value. If the limit is below your average order, keep Klarna as a backup. (2023) [L0274.044]
- Conflicting limit: the $30,000 per-purchase figure above applies to the Shopify installment programme as currently described. The $500 original limit (2023) applies only where the store's own limit has not been raised. Use the limit shown in your store settings as the governing value. [L0274.044, L1295.007]

#### Deciding whether to add Klarna

- When deciding whether to offer Klarna alongside Shop Pay: first check whether your average order value fits within Shop Pay's financing limit. If Shop Pay's maximum financing covers your typical order size, Klarna is not necessary. Add Klarna only if Shop Pay's limit is too low for your average order value and you want to offer more customers financing options. (2024) [L0253.050]
- Buy-now-pay-later services (Afterpay, Klarna) increase customer acquisition, especially across the top five countries. The default risk falls entirely on the customer's credit. You receive full payment upfront from the BNPL provider, which eliminates merchant chargeback exposure. [L0324.042]
- Offer payment options like Shopify Pay installments, Klarna or similar financing, so customers can spread payments, especially in weaker economic conditions. (2022, older) [L1113.075]
- A financing app allows customers to pay in installments. It directly increases conversion rate by removing upfront payment barriers for price-sensitive buyers. [L0040.006]

#### Affirm and PayPal Credit

- Offer PayPal Credit for customers. It gives six months with zero payments and 0% interest. Affirm integrates directly with Shopify, and both options can be offered together. (2019, older) [L1110.013]
- Payment plan via PayPal Credit: a visitor with a PayPal business account can offer this option on purchases above $99, with six months of no interest and no payments. The seller receives the full amount right away, and the buyer pays PayPal. (2018, older) [L1085.067]
- To accept PayPal Credit, use a PayPal business account and enable the option in the account settings. If the option is missing, contact PayPal through live chat to set it up. (2018, older) [L1085.068]
- PayPal Credit is enough for a typical order value. Stores with an average order value near $10,000 use more customised financing options. (2018, older) [L1085.069]

### Installment framing and payment plans

- Concealed price through payments: present the cost as a small monthly or instalment payment rather than the full price. In one test, "$39.95", "2 payments of $19.95" and "3 payments of $19.95" produced no difference in sales. Use payment framing carefully and test it, since it can lift the price a buyer accepts. [L1370.073]
- Case: a $5,000 business equipment item was sold as four payments of $1,250. A premium version at $7,000 was offered as eight payments of $875, which is a lower monthly payment than the cheaper version. 72 percent of buyers picked the higher-priced premium version. [L1370.074]
- For higher-priced items, split payment into a small first installment and monthly payments. Examples: $5 down with the balance over nine months, $1 a month for 12 months, or $2 a month for a large set. A low monthly amount makes the decision feel small. [L1369.010]
- An illustrative $1,000 product could be offered on a 12-month payment plan with roughly $100 due at the first payment. Showing that smaller first amount next to the full price can make a high price feel affordable to shoppers who would otherwise hesitate. [L1295.019]
- Buyers who use installments carry the payment obligation, not the merchant. Keep the installment terms visible at the price point and the checkout so that the frame is not a surprise. [L1295.012, L1295.013]

### Testing and decision steps

#### Testing a payment option

Procedure:
1. Run the Shop Pay vs. Klarna test for one month. [L0274.043]
2. Review the last three months of sales and note the Klarna transaction count. [L0274.043]
3. Remove Klarna from the site by disabling the payment method. [L0274.043]
4. Enable Shop Pay installments. [L0274.043]
5. Run for exactly one month. [L0274.043]
6. After the month, compare: did Shop Pay transactions exceed, match or fall below the Klarna baseline? [L0274.043]
7. Decide whether to keep both, switch, or revert. [L0274.043]

#### Testing a fee or price level

- Test fee levels before choosing one, and measure uptake at each level. [L0955.016]
- Test guarantee length against conversion rate, since guarantee length is an offer element that can be measured. [L1164.055]

## Scarcity, urgency and price framing

### Stock-based scarcity

- Show limited inventory on product pages and in ads, for example "only 4 units in stock" or "limited supply." Scarcity triggers fear of missing out and raises perceived value and purchase urgency. [L0233.006]
- Scarcity works by making a product look hard to get and more desirable. Phrases such as one-day sale, limited offer, while supplies last, and first come first served signal limited supply. Limited availability also suggests exclusivity. [L0340.045]
- Scarcity means limiting the quantity of an offer. A fixed stock, such as only five units left, creates pressure because the value disappears once the stock is gone. [L0683.001]
- Anything that looks limited or rare is seen as more valuable, so limited-stock or limited-availability wording increases demand. [L0516.022]
- Show remaining stock next to each variant as plain text, such as "only four left." The lesson recommends this over installing an app for it. [L0502.008]
- State the exact number left, such as 790 coats, 137 sets or 46 sets, instead of saying stock is limited. Specific figures are more believable than vague scarcity claims. [L1369.012]
- Scarcity can be communicated in three ways, each stated plainly on the store:
  1. Limited product supply.
  2. Limited number of bonuses, for example free shipping only for the next 20 buyers.
  3. A bonus available only until a set date and never again afterwards. [L0683.002]
- Use these announcement templates in store copy and email campaigns, adapted to your offer. Send them within a limited-time special-offer email to remind customers of the limit:

```
Scarcity announcement templates to use in store copy and email campaigns, adapted to your offer: "Only a few items left in stock." "Only 20 free necklaces available. After that, no more free necklaces." "A free book, only available this week, and after that you won't get it anymore." Send these within a limited-time special-offer email to remind customers of the limit.
```
[L0683.003]

### Deadlines and time-limited offers
