- Urgency comes from a time limit, not a quantity limit. A defined deadline for buying or acting makes customers decide faster, because missing the window means losing the offer. [L0683.004]
- Urgency comes from a time component, such as a limited drop, a limited edition, or an offer valid only today or this week. The time component triggers fear of missing out and increases perceived value. [L0502.009]
- Time-limited offers, such as limited-time drops, limited editions, 48-hour sales or deadline-based promotions, create urgency. The time restrictions must be clear and genuine. [L0233.007]
- Limited-time offers create urgency and push customers to buy sooner, which raises conversion rate. Use a clear end date so the urgency is credible. [L0436.018]
- Limiting an offer to a specific time frame or a specific action creates a need to decide now and overcomes the tendency to delay. Time-limited offers are used in direct response letters to prompt fast action. [L1261.099]
- Case: one advertiser offered a premium for six packages and proof of purchase, for a single week. Hundreds of thousands of buyers responded, so a short, specific deadline drove fast action. [L1261.100]
- Flash sales and limited-time discounts tied to a specific date, such as Black Friday and Cyber Monday, create urgency because the discount exists only on that day. Customers feel they cannot buy it earlier or later. [L0683.005]
- Example: a Posture Pro Corrector launch at 20% off the regular $49.99, marked as a launch offer with a specific end date ("Offer ends Sunday"). The discount is tied to time-limited event positioning to drive immediate purchase rather than comparison shopping. [L0313.016]
- Show a visible expiry on the coupon, such as "ending soon," rather than offering only an automatic discount. A visible deadline makes buyers act at the moment they see the offer. [L0655.016]
- Use a deadline as the most basic urgency builder, alone or combined with other tactics. Make it visible with a handwritten or rubber-stamped date. Printed and simulated handwriting can replace a true stamp on larger runs. [L1370.099]
  - Case: an insurance agent mailing 100 letters a week to cold prospects offered a free road atlas for a quote. Adding a rubber-stamped response deadline in red ink raised response from 1 to 3 percent to 5 to 8 percent. [L1370.100]
- Use time-limited urgency offers such as 5% off, 10% off or free shipping. Show a dynamic countdown that ends when the promotion ends. Time-limited offers give buyers a reason to purchase now. [L1346.021]
- A small urgency added to an offer improves conversion, for example a limited-time free shipping offer or a message that the price returns to the usual level within 24 hours. [L1382.018]
- Urgency offer pattern: pair a time-limited perk (free shipping or a discount) with a clear statement that the regular price or discount returns after a set period, such as 24 hours.
  - Why it works: the deadline pushes hesitant buyers to decide now.
  - Examples written for this pattern:
    - "Free express shipping today only, then standard shipping returns tomorrow"
    - "Save 40 percent until midnight, then the usual price applies." [L1436.044]
- Early-bird pricing schedule for events and courses:
  1. A lower price if enrolled by a set date.
  2. A middle price for enrollment after that date but before a later cutoff.
  3. A higher price at the door.

  The same schedule works for advance orders of new products, event passes, subscriptions and renewals. [L1370.102]
- Example: a 48-hour window, with 20% off shown with a coupon code in the ad, sent to the product page where the viewer can buy. The 48-hour window is generally the time the offer runs for that person. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0799.003]

### Limited groups, slots and waiting lists

- Example: a consultant announces a limited number of open client slots and warns that the waiting list will close for years once filled. This combines scarcity with an urgent timing signal, making action feel immediate. [L0340.046]

### Countdown timers and coupon expiry

#### Setup

- Show a countdown timer with a message such as "this deal ends in." Activate it by adding a product tag that starts with "sale," followed by the end date of the offer. Place it under the buy buttons, and change its background and colors if needed. [L0976.039]
- Add scarcity with a timer in the announcement bar that shows when the sale ends. A discount with no end point looks less credible. [L1194.039]

#### Keeping timers real

- Keep the countdown timer and the wording in sync with the real expiry time. One email showed a 17-hour timer while the text said the offer ends at midnight. This conflict is noticed by the shopper. [L0567.028]
- Countdown timers help conversion only when the deadline is real, for example a sale that ends at midnight Eastern time on Black Friday. A timer that resets every day is a gimmick and does not help. (Older advice, Source A 2022.) [L1092.055]

#### Timers versus coupons: two approaches

The sources disagree on whether a timer should restart. Both values apply in different cases:

| Approach | Rule | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Coupons that expire | Run coupons that expire once or twice a week, not a timer that restarts every three hours. Expiry dates should look like real promotions, not automatic resets. | Default for ongoing countdown urgency. | [L0355.019] |
| Daily reset during an extended sale | Reset the site countdown timer and email timers every 24 hours. Segment non-openers and non-buyers so they receive the extension message separately. Alternatively, brands can just say "final days" for a week, with no end time. | When extending a sale. | [L1225.002] |
| Daily reset at scale | A large brand kept an 18-hour countdown timer on its website during a week-long Cyber Monday sale and renewed it every day, while personalizing messages by customer segment. | A level to grow into, not a starting point. Smaller teams can copy the daily-reset idea without full segmentation. | [L1225.004] |

- Note: the older advice (2022) calls a timer that resets every day a gimmick. The newer lesson allows daily resets when a sale is extended. Use the daily reset only when the sale really is being extended, and do not present it as a permanent deadline. [L1092.055, L1225.002]

### Scarcity must be true

- Use scarcity and urgency only when they are true. False deadlines or invented shortages destroy trust, which is hard to rebuild. [L0378.031]
- Scarcity claims must be true, because false stock messages damage trust. [L0516.022]
- Mistake: a discount left without a reason or a deadline marks a random dropshipping store. Always name the sale and state when it ends. [L1194.041]
- Mistake: a generic countdown with text such as "ending soon" or "limited supply" adds little scarcity. Tie the urgency to the specific problem the product solves. [L1194.042]

### Highest number first

- When you show numbers, start with the highest figure that is still realistic and defensible. People compare prices automatically, so the first number becomes their reference point. [L0378.022]
- Example: a consultant who once charged $50,000 for a project that generated $7.5 million in sales anchored the first quote at $25,000 upfront. The high number was credible because it referred to a real result from a client. [L0378.023]

### Comparison prices and anchoring

- Showing a comparison price next to your price creates a frame that makes the discount look bigger and shifts perceived value upward. Framing the price against another price changes how the buyer judges value. [L1207.006, L1209.006, L1210.004]
- Raise the compare-at price to make the discount look larger, as part of a small urgency offer. [L1382.018]
- Pattern: [compare-at price] shown next to [sale price] with a simple discount label.
  - Why it works: the strike-through frame makes the saving obvious at a glance.
  - Examples: "Was 90, now 60: 30 off" and "Was 120, now 60: half off." [L1207.008]
- Compare your price itemized against what each part would cost separately, showing each item's price and the total. In one set offer, 418 works worth 472.05 dollars at retail were sold for 39 dollars, a saving of 433.05 dollars. [L1369.015]
- Formula: name a higher price the buyer already knows and show your price below it by a stated amount, then say why your price is available.
  - Why it works: a concrete comparison to a familiar price is easy to check, so the buyer trusts the saving.
  - Example one: "our 25-pound feed sack costs 6 dollars less than the same sack at the regional farm store, which charges 34 dollars."
  - Example two: "our three-piece tool set costs 45 dollars less than the same set at the hardware chain, which charges 189 dollars." [L1369.019]

### Price against the alternative

- Compare the price to a more expensive alternative that delivers the same result, not to a cheaper product in the same category. [L1370.068]
- Example: a $95 recorded program was set against at least $195 for live seminar enrollment, plus travel, overnight lodging and days away from work. Standard audio albums sold for about $10 per tape. The buyer must see the total cost of the alternative, because that is what makes the price feel small. [L1370.122]
- Show a price against the alternative. A chiropractor visit at $300 to $500 per month makes a $50 product look like a much better value. Show the cost and time saved, not only the product price. [L0871.041]
- Compared with a monthly chiropractor cost of $300 to $500, a one-time $50 product costs roughly 6 to 10 times less. Showing this gap in the offer makes the value of a low-priced product easier to see. [L0871.093]
- Show the price against professional treatments in the same category, with a clear price per session or per visit, so the product looks small next to the alternative. Pair it with the own product price. [L0374.025]
- Price comparison a competitor used on its landing page to make its product look cheap next to professional treatments: [L0374.024]

| Treatment | Typical cost |
|---|---|
| Microneedling sessions | $200-$700 per session |
| Laser resurfacing | $1,000-$3,000+ |
| Chemical peels | $150-$400 per peel |
| Dermatologist visits | $100-$300 per visit |
| Brand's serum (landing page) | $39.99 |

- Price is rarely the real decision factor, but it can stop a sale early. Minimise its impact before the reader sees the number. Decide the price strategy before writing the copy, and where price matters a lot, make the comparison work in your favour. [L1370.067]
- Sort offers into monthly payment bands rather than showing full prices. A car dealer's sale letter grouped vehicles into bands of $199, $249 and $399 per month, so the reader compares small monthly amounts and not the total. Use this grouping only when the monthly figures are accurate and the total cost is disclosed in the offer terms. [L1370.123]

### Dollar versus percent framing

The sources disagree on the format of a discount. No unit sets a threshold for choosing between them, so each value is listed with the reason its source gives.

| Format | Example wording | Reason given | When it applies | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fixed dollar amount | "save 7 dollars with a code" | Worked better in the operator's experience. | Savings shown as a dollar amount, including codes. | [L0519.034] |
| Fixed dollar amount | "save $20" | Customers can quantify money more easily than a percentage. | Savings shown as money. | [L1315.031] |
| Round, plain figure | "50 off" | Avoids making the shopper calculate the saving; calculating adds cognitive load. | When the discount should be read at a glance. | [L1207.007, L1210.004] |
| Exact percentage | "50% off" | Customers do not want mental math; a clear number removes friction. | When a clear percentage is the headline. | [L1209.007] |
| Percentage under regular or wholesale price | "25 percent under the original price for the same set," or a quarter, a third or half off wholesale | Frames the reduction against a known price. | Only for reductions you can document. | [L1369.018] |

- Pattern example for the round form: "Was 90, now 60: 30 off" and "Was 120, now 60: half off." [L1207.008]
- Both values exist in the sources. Choose the format that the buyer can check fastest, and keep the figure consistent across the store and emails.

### Per-unit, daily and ROI framing

#### Per-unit and bulk value

- Cost per unit = album price divided by number of units. Example: a six-cassette album priced at $49.95 to $69.95 works out to about $8.33 to $11.66 per tape. Use the same division to show a buyer that a single item's per-unit price is lower than a bundle's. [L1370.069]
- Show the price as a bulk of value: list how many pieces of information, items or benefits the product holds, using large, specific numbers such as 1,001 items, so the buyer sees a big bundle for the money. This also applies to physical items, where each nutrient or benefit can be listed to show the volume of value packed into a small product. [L1370.070]

#### Cost per day

- Express a total price as a small daily cost by dividing it by the number of days the product will be used. For example, a 120-dollar product used for one year costs about 33 cents a day. Keep the period realistic so the figure stays accurate. [L1369.112]
- Some competitor stores frame price as a cost per day, for example less than 0.60 USD per day, for daily-use products. Use this framing when the product is taken every day and the daily cost is low. (Source does not state a date.) [L0600.022]

#### Quantity saved

- For a concentrated product, state the saving in quantity used rather than in price per container. If the product is three times as strong, the buyer needs only one-third of the usual amount. The cost per use falls even if the sticker price is unchanged. [L1369.114]

#### Return on investment

- Show return on investment, which means presenting the offer as selling money at a discount. Frame the ROI as dollars made, such as a course that produced net gains for many users, or as dollars saved, such as a newsletter that saves more than its cost in taxes. State the figures so the buyer sees the cost as small. [L1370.075]
- Formula: ROI in a sales letter = gain divided by cost, expressed as a percentage. Example: a $1,000 income gain for a $199 course reads as roughly a 500 percent return.
  - Exaggerate the claim first, then bring it down to a conservative figure, such as saying the claim is only half right. This signals honesty. [L1370.076]

### Free offers paired with the same product

- Offers framed as free, such as buy-one-get-one or a free gift, tend to convert well when the paired item is the same product as the one being bought. [L1410.045]

## Writing and displaying the offer

### Offer copy formulas and patterns

- Make the offer explicit in ad copy. Generic offers such as "50% off + free shipping" are now expected and will underperform. The offer directly affects both CTR and CVR, so choose an enticing, differentiated offer. [L0206.006]
- Use one offer per ad variant so the results can be compared. [L0392.010]
- Most-aware customers already want the product and only need a reason to buy now. Push a direct offer (percentage discount, free gift or promotion) with a clear buy-now action. Extra explanation is usually unnecessary at this stage. [L0516.010]
- For a prospect who already knows the product and wants it, the headline needs only the product name and a bargain price, with the price as the news. The body summarises the strongest points and ends with a store name or coupon and a call to buy. This is a mechanical job and should be paid accordingly. [L0330.020]
- Value equation: value rises when the dream outcome and the perceived likelihood of achieving it go up, and when the time, effort and sacrifice needed to get there go down. Copy should raise the first two and shrink the last three at the same time. [L0516.014]
- Higher-priced products need more copy to justify the price and create the need. A low price that looks like clear value needs less copy. A low price that seems too cheap to be credible needs more copy to build trust. [L1366.055]
- Vivid, detailed pictures of the satisfaction a product gives reduce how much the buyer cares about the price. The more concrete scenes of use you can present without repeating yourself, the stronger the demand and the weaker the price resistance. [L0330.101]
- If a product seems to need several units to be useful, such as a small accessory a buyer might think only needs one, frame it as a gift for someone else to remove that objection. Gift framing can support single-unit purchases alongside multi-unit offers. [L1172.055]
- Offer call-to-action formula: pair one concrete price benefit, such as a percentage discount, with a second version that gives a free gift with purchase today. A clear, time-bound incentive turns interest into action. [L0392.010]
  - Examples: "Get your set at 25% off before the weekend ends." and "Order today and get a free travel case with your purchase." [L0392.010]
  - The example call to action used a 40% discount on a pair of the product, and a second version offered a free gift with purchase on the same day. [L0392.011]
- Stacking pattern: stack a percentage discount, a free bonus and a shipping or time condition in one line, so the buyer who already knows the product gets more than one reason to act now.
  - Formula: discount % + bonus item + condition (free shipping, online only, or a deadline). [L0794.055]
  - Examples: "Take 30% off today and get a travel case free with your first order." and "Save 15%, ship free, and add a refill pack at no cost, online orders this weekend only." [L0794.055]
- Spend-threshold headline formula: Save $AMOUNT on all orders over $THRESHOLD! It works because it states the exact saving and the condition to unlock it in one line, so the shopper knows what to spend. [L0757.019]
  - Examples: "Save $25 on all orders over $200!" and "Save $80 on every order over $400!" [L0757.019]
- Seasonal sale formula: [season or event] sale: free shipping plus [discount percentage] off with code [short word tied to the store theme]. The event name creates timing, free shipping removes the cost objection, and a concrete percentage with a themed code makes the offer specific and memorable. [L1325.008]
  - Examples: "Summer sale: free shipping plus 20% off with code SURFSIDE" and "Winter sale: free shipping plus 25% off with code FROSTBITE". [L1325.008]
- Dynamic day coupon text formula: a short call to action that names the discount code, states the percentage off and ends with a day name that limits the offer. The code, the saving and the deadline are visible in one line, which lowers hesitation at checkout. [L1450.015]
  - Examples: "Enter SPRING15 now and save 15% through Sunday only." and "Use code BREW20 today to get 20% off until Friday only." [L1450.015]
- Bundle title pattern: name the outcome the bundle gives and the exact saving in the same line, so the discount is visible before the shopper reaches the cart. [L0559.020]
  - Examples: "Start your morning and save 12%" and "Build your tea set and save 15%". [L0559.020]
- Combined value formula: show the combined value of the items, then the bundle price, then the saving in dollars or percent. Buyers judge a deal by the amount they save, so a visible saving makes an add-on feel almost free. [L1260.064]
  - Examples: "Complete the set: total value 74.99, today 59.99, you save 15." and "Add the second item and save 20% on the pair, shipped to one address." [L1260.064]
- Keep the offer simple. An offer that confuses visitors hurts sales more than it helps. Use one clear offer with a simple structure that people can follow straight through to checkout. [L1017.024]

### Offer naming

- Magic Offer naming structure: MAGNET + AVATAR + GOAL + INTERVAL + CONTAINER. You do not have to use all five elements. [L0549.023]
  - MAGNET is the hook, such as free, giveaway, percent off, spring, summer, new or holiday. [L0549.023]
  - AVATAR is the target customer or interest. [L0549.023]
  - GOAL is the problem solved or benefit, such as pain free, stress relief, hair cure or thicker hair. [L0549.023]
  - INTERVAL is the time to the result, such as 5 minutes, 30 days, 21 days or 6 weeks. [L0549.023]
  - CONTAINER is the format, such as bundle, program, shortcut, rebirth, attack, blueprint, sprint, catapult, comeback pack or journey. [L0549.023]
- Keep offer names short and punchy. Words that rhyme or start with the same letter sound better, and the name should signal the target customer, the problem solved and how quickly the result comes. [L0549.024]
- Example of a combined offer name using an interval, a goal and a container: "five minute hair enhancer bundle." [L0549.026]

### Ad copy and headlines

- Ice Cap migraine relief ad: 25% discount plus free shipping. It uses multiple short testimonials for social proof ("Finally real relief without meds", "I feel in control again", "Stops my migraines fast"), a pain-point opening ("Sick of the migraines?"), and emphasises 50K+ users relieved. Holistic positioning ("wholistic and calming solution") appeals to health-conscious customers. [L0313.013]
- Ice Cap pricing: 25% discount plus free shipping, with the exact price not shown in ads. This suggests a sufficient profit margin to absorb shipping cost while keeping margin on the 25% discount. [L0313.018]
- RYZe Mushroom Hot Cocoa ad: single image with minimal copy ("NO MORE SLEEPLESS NIGHTS"), an ingredient list with benefits (Reishi for stress relief, Melatonin for sleep support, etc.) and a simple CTA ("TRY IT"). This format works for supplement and wellness products where ingredient proof is strong enough without testimonials. [L0313.017]
- Bottom-of-funnel static ads for offers use 25% or 50% discounts (BWF ads). They need little research because the offer itself carries the message. [L1195.043]
- Point the ads at the single collection where a buy-X-get-Y offer applies, so the ad message and the discount match. In the example the offer needed two items from that one collection, and the free item also came from it. [L0650.067]
- Cut any offer shown in a copied video that does not appear on your landing page, such as buy one get one free, so the ad promise matches the store. [L0704.007]
- AI-generated product names and descriptions should not be copied and pasted without editing. The output needs human judgement and split testing before it goes live. [L0683.021]
- Full buyer guides target people who are not ready to buy, so create them only after ads are profitable and dialled in. Comparison charts are the better early option for buyers who are ready to purchase. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0664.055]
- Compare discounted and undiscounted listings side by side. A product shown with a discount in an ad usually gets a much higher click-through rate than the same product at MSRP beside it. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0671.058]
- Rule for discount claims in ads and banners: state the exact percentage, the product scope and whether a promo code is needed. List any excluded categories or products, and never show a discount larger than the one you actually run. [L0972.102]

#### TikTok

- Buyers arriving from TikTok rarely buy a regular-priced product, so the offer must be strong. Show the offer in the ad text or within the first three seconds of the video. [L1382.016]
- TikTok buyers expect a deal before they consider the product, so show the offer in the ad text or within the first three seconds of the video. [L1436.041]

### Sale banners and product page graphics

- A product page graphic sits right above or below the add-to-cart button and is used for sales or urgency. It can be removed, changed or swapped out with the season, and works best with a promo code and an expiry date. [L0342.030]
- Sale banner formula: a large heading with the discount percentage, a line showing the coupon code as the action to take, and a small expiry line in a smaller font. Use white text on a bright background that matches the brand colours. [L0342.031]
  - Examples: "Summer Clearance: 15% Off All Boards" with "Use code SUMMER15 at checkout" and "Ends Sunday", and "Flash Sale: 20% Off Gear" with "Enter code FLASH20 at checkout" and "Expires Friday 11:59 PM". [L0342.031]
- A product page sale graphic should show the offer, the coupon code, where the code is entered and, optionally, an expiry date to create urgency. Keep the wording short so it reads in a second. [L1045.026]
- Sale graphic formula: a bold headline with the offer and the discount, a line with the coupon code, a line saying where to apply the code (such as at checkout), and an optional smaller line with an expiry date. The buyer sees the offer, the code and the next action together, right next to the buy button. [L1045.027]
  - Example 1: "Spring deal: take 20% off every order / Use code SPRING20 / Apply at checkout / Ends 31 May." [L1045.027]
  - Example 2: "Free gift with every order / Add code WELCOME at checkout / Limited to this month." [L1045.027]
- Do not put a sale graphic over a product image in Google Shopping ads, because the ad can be disapproved. (See Google Shopping and promotion display.) [L0470.063]
- Use a product-page pop-up for items with long lead times, such as six to ten weeks. The pop-up tells the visitor the wait before they add the product to the cart and asks them to confirm before they continue, which prevents disappointed customers later. [L1472.041]
- Use "Add-on" or "Bundle" imagery: create an image in Canva (for example with the "product offer" template) showing the main product with the free bonus, and place it in the product photo gallery as the second image. [L0577.014]
- On the product page, show the main item in the default photo and display the add-on items being worn or used by the model in other photos. The upsells are visible without cluttering the main gallery. [L0838.013]

#### Procedure: unique offer template for one product (Shopify)

1. In Shopify, go to Online Store > Themes and click Customize. [L0667.008]
2. Open the product template dropdown, choose Products, and select the default product page template. [L0667.008]
3. Click Create template, name it for the offer (for example "product page 50 off"), and base it on the default product page. [L0667.008]
4. Click the expiring discount code block, change its text to the new offer (for example "$50 off and free shipping"), then save. [L0667.008]
5. Go to Products, open the target product, and in the right sidebar under Theme template change the default template to the new one. [L0667.008]
6. Save and preview to confirm the unique coupon shows only on that product. [L0667.008]
- A template assigned to zero products is not visible to shoppers. [L0667.008]

#### Procedure: free-gift banner on a product page

1. Design a free-gift banner image that matches the site's layout and colours. [L0667.034]
2. Show the product image, the product name and its value, for example with a message that it ships free with the order today. [L0667.034]
3. Place the banner above the product description, close to the add-to-cart button, so every visitor sees it. [L0667.034]
4. To insert it, press Enter above the description in the product page editor, click insert image, then save. [L0667.034]

### Announcement bars and site-wide offers

- A store-wide discount code can be placed in the announcement bar at the top of the site. A code that applies only to specific products is shown on those products' pages through a dedicated product page template. [L0667.090]
- A promotion bar at the top of the homepage that offers free shipping plus a percentage discount with a code gives visitors a reason to act sooner. The reviewed example suggested free shipping plus 15% off during a seasonal sale. [L1325.007]
- Announcement bar and exit-intent discounts can be swapped for free shipping offers when the margin does not allow a discount. [L0855.051]
- Use announcement bars to explain promotion rules where shoppers are focused, for example to say a free gift applies without discount codes. Announcements can also link to collections or blog pages using inline HTML hyperlinks. [L1231.024]
- Show the offer (what exactly the customer receives) near the top of the page, because visitors want to know what they are getting before they read further. [L1363.005]
- Homepage sections can be added and filled later to highlight product promotions. Start with only the basic homepage blocks at launch and add promotional sections as campaigns require. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L1371.051]
- Show the offer, the price and the call to action on the homepage, product pages and About Us page at minimum. The curve offer can also appear on the cart page, in emails and in ads, but it must always be clearly visible. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0399.007]
- Give customers with a specific need a direct link from the product page to a matching collection, such as a sensitive-skin collection introduced with the question "Sensitive skin?". This keeps the main product page focused while still catching niche buyers. (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.) [L0409.017]

### Trust rows and reassurance

- Pre-footer trust-building headlines: Free Shipping $100+ (incentive), Surfer Owned & Operated (authenticity), Fast & Secure Checkout (convenience), Easy Returns (confidence). [L0101.014]
- Example trust row from a reseller homepage, placed right below the header, with four points:
  - Free Shipping ("all the time, every time") [L0409.045]
  - Give Us a Call (phone number shown) [L0409.045]
  - 30 Day Easy Returns ("don't like or need it, send it back") [L0409.045]
  - Price Match ("find a better price, we'll beat it") [L0409.045]
  - (Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)
- Show free shipping on orders over $50 and a 90-day 100% money-back guarantee in the offer area of the product page. [L1164.041]
- Free-shipping promotions can be shown on product pages with images and graphics. The shipping option itself appears in the cart. (Older advice, Source A 2022.) [L0963.070]
- Show free extras such as white glove delivery or bonus gifts on the product page, the same way you show bonus gifts, so buyers see the full value of the offer. Free white glove is worth offering only when the product price is high enough that the margin covers its cost. (Older advice, Source A 2020.) [L0961.007]
- Note: a no-sales-tax policy can be shown in ads by default if competitors add sales tax and yours does not. Mention the policy on product pages or in the header announcement bar, not in ads. [L1094.079]

#### Sales page structure (long-form offer)

1. Reveal the special report as the main offer. [L1365.040]
2. Introduce the subscription service, under which the report is received when the prospect joins. [L1365.040]
3. Show what others say, using testimonials for the service. [L1365.040]
4. Use a false close that anchors a high price. [L1365.040]
5. Present extra bonuses and show how each one has value. [L1365.040]
6. Show the value stack with the total value of everything. [L1365.040]
7. Cut the value down and reveal the final price. [L1365.040]
8. Add a guarantee as the risk reversal. [L1365.040]

### Price display and savings math

- Compare-at price display: show the higher compare-at price (for example 79.99 USD) struck through, show the sale price (for example 39.99 USD) in a larger font, add a "50% OFF" label, and optionally add a time-limited scarcity line such as "Limited time" or an event sale. Page builders such as PageFly or GemPages allow custom background colours for this highlight. [L1259.027]
- Highlight the discount in the price block in this order: product name first, then the compare-at price, then the sale price in a larger font, then the text "50% off". An event or time-limited scarcity line such as "limited for today" or a seasonal sale can be added. [L1345.017]
- Visitors and shoppers read from left to right, so the first price they see should be the higher reference price, and the real sale price should be larger. The contrast between the two numbers makes the saving visible at a glance. [L1260.062, L1345.019]
- Caution on heavy percentage banners: avoid heavy 50 percent off banners with limited-time urgency, because many consumers now distrust them. Show clear prices and present the store as a trusted brand instead. [L1251.043]
  - Reconciling sources: the "50% OFF" label in the price block is used in [L1259.027] and [L1345.017], while [L1251.043] warns against heavy 50% banners. Use the label with clear, real prices and a restrained banner; reserve heavy urgency banners for offers you can defend. [L1251.043, L1259.027, L1345.017]
- In an offer lead, state the regular price, the special price and the dollar saving together, for example a $59 cover price offered at $19 saves $40. The dollar amount makes the value concrete for the reader. [L0756.076]
- Shopify savings label: enter the sale price and the MSRP (compare-at price) in Shopify. Example: a sale price of $800 and an MSRP of $900 show the $900 struck through, $800 as the sale price, and a "Save $100" label, displayed automatically on every product page without extra work. [L1130.018]
- Do not list the sale price alone as the minimum advertised price. Always show the discount against the MSRP, and keep the expiring coupon promotion visible in addition to it. [L1130.019]
- Listing only the sale price gives visitors no sense of how much they save. This weakens conversion even when the price is competitive. [L1130.020]
- Keep the time-limited coupon promotion in a separate banner on the product page, above the price, rather than merging it into the MSRP display. Visitors can then see the MSRP saving and the extra coupon saving at the same time. [L1130.035]
- Price-tag caution for supplier pricing: with MAP suppliers, the displayed price cannot be lowered. Use the Google Shopping promotion feature to show a discount tag, and apply the actual reduction with a coupon code at checkout. (Older advice, Source A 2022.) [L1092.053]
- Higher-priced products and sale copy: a free gift or discount described on the page should match the price logic. Free white glove and similar extras only pay when the margin covers their cost. [L0961.007]

#### Keeping the math clear

- Calculate the exact saving before you write it on the store. The 20% example given for two 34.99 USD items does not work out to a 10 USD saving; 20% off 69.98 USD is about 14 USD. A wrong figure damages trust. [L0774.015]
- Savings label formula for a compare-at price: (compare-at price minus sale price) divided by compare-at price, then rounded. Example: a $69.99 compare-at price with a $49.99 sale price gives (69.99 minus 49.99) divided by 69.99, which is about 28.6% and is shown as "SAVE 29%". [L1188.016]
- Savings label calculation: MSRP (compare-at price) minus sale price. Example: a sale price of $1,199.99 with an MSRP of $1,299.99 displays "Save $100" automatically on the product page. [L1130.034]
- Discount email and checkout copy must match the math: state the exact percentage and the expiry, and never promise more than the store runs. [L0972.102, L1188.016]
- Discount claims in visuals and text must match the stored discount exactly. [L0972.102]

### Bundles and add-ons

- Bundles and free add-ons make the customer feel they receive several items for the price of one, which lifts offer appeal. Make bundles visible on the site as a bundle block, dropdown or image gallery. [L0577.013]
- In a bulleted value recap, place a small product image beside each bullet rather than text alone, so the stack of items looks physically substantial. Show each item's dollar value next to its image so the total is easy to follow at a glance. [L1356.075]
- Merchandising matters: the order in which products appear on the site influences results. Keep the offer simple and the website design clear so customers understand how the offer works, because confusing offers fail even if they are strong. [L1404.031]

### Emails for offers

- Pattern for the discount email: state the action and the exact percentage, then the code and the expiry time.
  - Examples of my own: "Save 10% on your bag, valid until midnight tonight" and "Take 20% off your basket before the timer hits zero". [L0567.026]
- Do not upsell an abandoned cart by default. When a discount cuts your margin, add a best-seller product block to the offer email as an AOV booster. The block lets the shopper add items with one click and updates the cart. [L0567.029]

### Cart and checkout messages

- Cart message for a pre-purchase return option. Replace the placeholders, then show the message at the bottom of the cart with a link to a short details panel that explains the fee. [L0955.050]

```
Add free returns for {fee} now. If you pay this fee, any eligible item you return ships back with no return handling fee. If you skip it, a return will cost you the {handling_fee} handling fee. Some items may be excluded under our store policy.
```

- Source: [L0955.050]

### Google Shopping and promotion display

- Google Shopping promotions can show offers such as free shipping or a discount code. Do not put a sale graphic over the product image, because the ad can be disapproved. [L0470.063]
- A Merchant Center promotion displays the offer in the Shopping ad, where the shopper can hover over or click it to see the promotion details. Shopping ads get the same extra incentive that promotion extensions give search ads. [L0712.014]
- Promotion extensions show a sale offer, such as a Black Friday sale, directly under the search ad, giving searchers an extra incentive to visit the site. The lesson calls them a strong way to increase CTR and notes the feature was recently rolled out, so check its current availability in Google Ads. [L0712.011]
- Google Merchant Center promotions: add a promotion such as a 5 percent off coupon code in Merchant Center. Google then shows a promo tab on your Google Shopping ad with the promotion details, which can increase click-through rate. (Older advice, Source A 2021.) [L0801.054]
- Price-tag caution for MAP suppliers: see the MAP note under price display. [L1092.053]

### Shopify setup and store-wide settings

- A store-wide discount code can be placed in the announcement bar. A product-specific code is shown through a dedicated product page template. [L0667.090]
- Use the product template procedure above for a unique offer on one product. [L0667.008]
- Use the free-gift banner procedure above for a free gift on a product page. [L0667.034]

#### Further details

- A free product offer that requires a $9.95 shipping and handling payment, placed prominently on the homepage, does not fit a store selling gifts and undermines trust. Review offers like this for fairness and fit before publishing them. [L0355.020]

## Competitor offer research and differentiation

### Why competitor research comes first

- Skipping competitor research leads to guessing about differentiation, because you cannot judge whether your offer is unique until you know the offers competitors already run and that your target customers already buy. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0898.003]
- A quality offer is one that customers perceive as unique, or at least partly unique, compared with what competitors sell in the same market. Perceived uniqueness counts, not the seller's own view of the product. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0898.002]
- The offer does not need to be the best in the world, but it must be better than what competitors offer, ideally clearly better, because buyers can open several competitor tabs at once and a weaker offer is easy to spot. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0408.008]
- Competitors selling the same product should not stop you from selling it. Victory comes from a superior offer structure (bundled bonuses, guarantees, benefit-focused naming) and faster fulfillment, not from product exclusivity. With a strong offer and operations, you outperform competitors with identical products but weak offers. [L0233.021]

### Identifying real competitors

- Do not treat products sold by low-volume one-off sellers, such as many Etsy shops, as direct competitors, because they usually have no large inventory. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0968.083]
- If competitors sell near Amazon prices, they are often Chinese sellers. Avoid very large brands, but small competitors whose ads get about 500-3,000 likes or engagements can be out-marketed. [L1217.061]
- When competitors buy cheap and fulfill through AliExpress with very low margins, the same product can often be sold at a higher price. Their short-lived stores do not build a brand, which leaves room for a stronger store. [L1171.027]

### Building the competitor offer map

#### Core rule and procedure

- Compare your current offer with the offers of competitors, using the same offer elements, to see where you stand in the market. Where you are behind, improve the offer first and record the changes in the curve offer builder. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0399.006]

Procedure *(older advice, 2021-22)*:

1. Pull out the competitor map you built earlier, with each competitor's offer and USP, and review it. If it does not exist yet, first run a competitor-research process and fill it in.
2. Compare your current offer with the competitors' offers, using the same offer elements, and judge where you stand in your market.
3. Where you are behind, improve your offer toward the strongest curve offer you can and record the changes in the curve offer builder. [L0399.021]

#### What to record for each competitor

| Field | What to record | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Offer and USP | Each competitor's offer and unique selling proposition | L0399.021 |
| Price position | Whether the competitor's price is the same as yours, lower or higher. Price positioning matters more when you sell a large range. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* | L0893.027 |
| Extra sales terms | Any sales terms beyond the price. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* | L0893.027 |
| Bonuses and premiums | Freebies or content upgrades given to entice a customer to buy. If a competitor offers none on its product pages, record that as none. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* | L0893.019 |
| Extra value | Value beyond the base product, which is a comparison point for building a differentiated offer. Competitor analysis must include it. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* | L0898.006 |

### Comparing offer elements

#### Multi-buy tiers

- A competitor used tiered offers: buy 2 get 1 free for visible improvement with free shipping, and buy 3 get 2 free for real results with a free post-serum jelly. Free shipping applied to orders over $40. [L0374.026]
- Competitor stores commonly use multi-buy offers such as buy 2 get 1 free and buy 3 get 2 free, as well as buy 2 get 2 free and buy 3 get 3 free for longer supplies. Test which tier structure sells best before expanding it. [L0600.018]
- Bundle offers seen in the field include:
  - buy 1 get 1 free (2 bottles in total)
  - buy 2 get 2 free (4 bottles in total)
  - buy 3 get 3 free (6 bottles in total)
  - a two-bottle pack with free bonus champagne [L1164.039]
- Buy-one-get-one-free offers appear on competitor stores and are used in the fashion niche as a visible hook, which can be tested in a similar form. [L1161.032]
- Common competitor offers include buy one get one free. A stronger differentiator is a free ebook or guide given with the purchase. Offers should be paired with an authority signal to make the store look more credible. [L0335.042]

#### Subscriptions

- Competitor stores offer an automatic refill or subscription option with a saving of around 20 to 25 percent. Show the saving next to the subscription option so it is clearly compared to the one-time price. [L0600.019]
- A competitor's serum page combined multi-unit tiers (Buy 2 Get 1 FREE, Buy 3 Get 2 FREE) with an Auto-Refill subscription that saves 25 percent and a 90-day guarantee. Multi-unit tiers plus a subscription discount are a common structure to model when pricing a bundle. [L0772.026]
- The same competitor's tiered offer also included a monthly auto-refill at 25% off with cancel anytime. [L0374.026]

#### Shipping thresholds and delivery

- Competitors often advertise free shipping only above a threshold, such as $49. If you offer free shipping on all orders, show that as the main message and place an expiring coupon code in the header bar above it. [L0894.017]
- Estimated ship date and delivery window are easy to add to product pages and can separate you from a competitor that never mentions arrival time. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0243.012]
- A competitor charged $150 for white glove delivery and $99 for Room and Choice service, while basic threshold delivery (delivered off the truck) was included. [L0894.018]
- A $1,500 chandelier with free white glove delivery beats a competitor that adds $150 for the same service. Shoppers comparing both sites will choose the store with the free option. [L0894.019]
- If margins allow, include white glove free on selected products. Otherwise sell it as a paid premium upgrade. [L0894.018]

#### Guarantees and warranties

- A competitor's serum page paired its add-to-cart button with a visible 90-day guarantee and a free-shipping message, both placed near the price. Placing the risk reversal next to the buy button is a structure worth copying when you test a product page. [L0772.048]
- If a competitor mentions a one-year warranty but does not emphasize it, highlight the warranty prominently. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0243.012]
- Guarantees are part of a superior offer structure that competitors selling identical products often lack. [L0233.021]

#### Bonuses, accessories and gifts

- Include accessories such as light bulbs as a free gift when the supplier provides them at a good price per order. Free inclusion beats a competitor that says the item is not included. [L0894.034]
- Bonus items such as wrist rest pads with desk purchases are a way to add value. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0243.012]
- If a competitor's top sellers in Google Shopping have similar photos, plain websites, a bundle and a pop-up, you can beat them with an offer they lack, such as a free extra accessory or a free gift on the first order. [L1508.033]

#### Price, total cost and margin

- A competitor's low item price may be offset by a separate shipping charge, so compare the total cost at checkout rather than the displayed product price. Otherwise the margin estimate for a cheap product can be wrong. [L0655.026]
- A competitor listed a product below MAP. Compare the full price a customer would pay, including sales tax, shipping and any discount the competitor adds. A lower listed price can still end up higher than yours at checkout, which changes whether the listing is a real threat. [L0470.103]

#### Discounts

- When a competitor runs a discount on the same product, beat it with a stronger offer, such as moving from 50 percent off to 70 percent off, raising the compare-at price, or adding a buy-one-get-one-free deal. Keep the ad copy the same while improving the offer. [L1382.073]
- If a competitor offers 50 percent off, beat it with 70 percent off, buy-one-get-one-free, or a higher compare-at price so that the offer looks larger. [L1436.045]
- In the most competitive product brands, find out which discounts your top competitors give and be ready to match them. Experts disagree on best practice for discounts, so the right level depends on your specific competition. [L1357.007]

#### Customization

- Products with customization options can sell at higher prices, and competitors often do not build the full options. This can be a differentiator for a store. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0967.073]

### Verifying competitor prices

- The same product showed different prices across one brand's pages and ads, so check the price on each page and each ad before you rely on it. A two-formulation bundle priced under $40 was promoted with free shipping on TikTok, and the bundle price was a repeated USP. [L0374.028]
- The hosts recalled a starting price of about $48 for a blender but were unsure whether the price had gone up or down as the product became popular, so verify exact price history before relying on it. [L0657.002]
- Price points from research as examples:
  - a nostalgia gift toy at $39.99 USD or CHF 34.99 with a buy-one-get-one-50%-off offer
  - a customized sister jewelry gift at $19.99 with 148 active ads running in Canada, Australia and the US [L1185.008]

### Finding gaps

#### Feature gaps

Pattern *(older advice, 2021-22)*:

- Feature-gap differentiation: identify a need that recurs in customer feedback and that no competitor addresses, then build the product around solving it.
- Pattern: find the single complaint or request mentioned repeatedly that the only available option does not solve, and make the core of your version the solution.
- Example 1: a camping stove seller notices customers complain about bulk, so they launch a compact version that packs into a single pouch.
- Example 2: a kitchen scale seller hears that users want to weigh liquids without a cup, so they launch a scale with a tare-friendly, removable bowl. [L1155.012]

#### Competitor weak spots

- Competitors without promotions, urgency, bundles or bonus offers leave room to win. [L0894.016]
- When a competitor offers free shipping on heavy or large items, check how they make money on it by reviewing their individual reviews, since some competitors lose money without knowing it. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0967.074]
- Read competitor reviews to see why a competitor's price is low. [L0358.015]

### Creating a unique offer

#### Improve the offer, not only the price

- Differentiate from competitors by improving their offer: provide lower pricing, bundle multiple units, or reframe the product message for new use cases. [L0209.034]
- To beat a competitor in a crowded niche, improve the offer rather than only the price. Keep the same product and add value such as a free gift or a lower price with an extra item. [L1220.036]
- Example: a competitor sells a necklace at 40 USD. A competitor-beating offer might be 30 USD plus a free ring. [L1220.036]
- Improve the offer to compete in a new niche, such as jewelry, instead of copying competitors. Offer more value for the same or less money, for example a lower price with a free extra item. [L1220.037]
- A competitor charges 40 USD for a necklace. Undercutting to 30 USD and adding a free ring is an offer that positions the store above the competition. [L1220.038]
- Check what competitors offer before setting up your own bundles, so your offers are not weaker than the market standard. [L1299.006]

#### Brand value

- If the same product is sold cheaper on Amazon, a brand-owned store can still charge more by adding complementary items or a stronger offer. One seller reported pricing about 15 USD above the Amazon average and still selling well. Perceived value and branding are the levers, not matching the lowest price. [L1428.019]

#### Tested differentiation

- Customized offers that competitors do not make are a stronger way to stand out than free shipping, free white-glove delivery, bonus gifts or 10% off. They should be tested and measured before wider rollout. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1086.004]

### Avoiding easily copied terms

- Avoid offers that let competitors copy the same terms, such as matching guarantees or undercutting, because the advantage disappears when rivals adopt them. Find an offer structure that is hard to match. [L1261.103]
- Free shipping, free white-glove delivery, bonus gifts or 10% off are easy for competitors to copy and do not create separation. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1086.004]
- Shoppers often value a strong bonus offer more than a competitor's longer history, so compete on the offer rather than on store age. [L0857.025]
- When supplier copies happen, run the same offer with suppliers that do not sell direct, or build a more premium bundle with extras that the supplier does not offer. Offers cannot be copyrighted, so differentiate with better bundles. [L1204.059]

### Keeping the map current

- Expect competitors to answer a successful store with bundles, packages, or a redesigned site. Plan periodic reviews of offers and design so the store does not lose ground while it is being run. [L0826.025]

### Responding when competitors sell at MAP or below

#### Competitors sell at the same MAP price

- When competitors all sell at the same MAP price, price cannot be the reason a shopper chooses you. Win on services, shipping, included accessories, bonuses and clear product information instead. [L0894.043]
- When selling at MAP, stand out through bonus offers and expiring coupon codes. These create differentiation without breaking the MAP policy. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0967.068]
- When a competitor sells the same product at the same price, add a bonus offer that the shopper values highly to win the sale. [L0857.024]

#### Competitors sell below MAP or non-MAP, and you are on MAP

- If a competitor sells a product below the MAP or non-MAP price, do not match them on price, because that leads to a race to the bottom. Revisit your own numbers for room to offer a special deal, and research the competitor's reviews to see why their price is low. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L0358.015]
- If a non-MAP competitor sells the same product for less, do not start a price war. Run paid traffic only on products where the margin remains healthy, and send free traffic to the products where competitors undercut you. [L0835.054]
- When a competitor sells below your landed cost, their per-sale costs (card processing fees, paid traffic, hosting, and staff for live chat, phone and email) are usually higher than your margin. Their price most likely reflects cheaper sourcing or shipping. Ask your supplier about better pricing before you react with a price cut. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1002.078]

#### Retailer breaks MAP and the manufacturer does not enforce it

Options *(older advice, 2021-22)*:

1. Document that you have followed the MAP policy and raised the issue with the manufacturer on several occasions.
2. Remove the product from your store and sell alternatives from other suppliers with better margins and support, if the gap is too large to compete with.
3. Or match the lower price to stay on a level playing field, accepting that the retailer may drop you from its supply.
4. Or, instead of bidding harder in Google Shopping, drive more organic or other traffic to that product page. [L0879.039]

#### Choosing a response

| Situation | Approach | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Competitor is below you and the gap is small, with room in your numbers | Offer a special deal from your own margin; do not match the price | L0358.015 |
| Competitor is non-MAP and cheaper | Do not start a price war; run paid traffic only where margin stays healthy, and send free traffic to undercut products | L0835.054 |
| Competitor discounts the same product | Beat it with a stronger offer (for example 70% instead of 50%, higher compare-at, or buy-one-get-one-free) | L1382.073, L1436.045 |
| Competitor is in a highly competitive brand | Know their discounts and be ready to match; experts disagree on best practice, so the right level depends on your competition | L1357.007 |
| Retailer breaks MAP and the manufacturer does not enforce it | Follow the four options above; matching the price is one option that accepts loss of supply | L0879.039 |
| Competitor is cheap and below your landed cost | Ask your supplier for better pricing before cutting your price | L1002.078 |
| Competitor is at the same MAP price | Win on services, shipping, accessories, bonuses and product information | L0894.043, L0857.024 |

Note on conflicting advice: L0358.015 and L1357.007 both address matching price, and L0879.039 names matching as a valid option. Use the table above to decide which applies. In a non-MAP niche where a lower price is paired with an extra item, the lower-price approach shown in L1220.036 and L1220.038 can work, because the extra value is part of the offer. [L0358.015, L1357.007, L0879.039, L1220.036, L1220.038]

## Promotion calendar, seasonal sales and Q4

### Offer arsenal and monthly rotation

- Build an offer arsenal: a list of at least four offers you can pull from whenever you need one. The list is the pool you rotate through over the year. [L0757.015]
- Brainstorm ideas without pricing them first, then check each idea's cost and margin before scheduling it. Keep the list written down so you can reuse, adjust and pull from it each month. [L1204.023]
- A monthly offer-brainstorming exercise can produce around 12 unique offers, more than the five needed for Q4. Keep a running list so you always have spares. [L1224.008, L1287.056]
- Plan Q4 offers in this order:
  1. Brainstorm about 12 possible promotions. [L1285.081, L1223.008]
  2. Select five and assign one to each holiday. [L1285.081, L1223.008]
  3. Record the offer, eligibility and theme for each promotion in an offer calendar planned a year in advance. [L1285.081]
  4. For each promotion, check the historical best-performing email subject lines and ad headlines, ranked by opens, clicks and sales, and pick those that fit the brand. [L1223.008]
  5. Use the same message in the email subject lines and in the remarketing ad headlines. [L1223.008]
  6. Set up the email segment and the remarketing audiences, then launch. [L1223.008]
- Promote one offer per month. Offers can be recycled and reused throughout the year. [L0757.024]
- Run only one offer per month. Proven offers can be recycled every quarter or every six months, ideally with a new twist, but not repeated every month. [L1205.017]
- *Older advice (2020):* Rotate offers monthly rather than repeating the same promotion. Two promotions in the same month, such as during November, are not too much. [L1088.054]
- *Older advice (2022):* Rotate the type of promotion each month, such as a bundle, a fixed percentage off, or something else, instead of repeating one offer. [L1281.036]
- Vary promotions across the year instead of repeating the same discount on each holiday. Choose the deal, such as a free gift, a free item, or a buy-one-get-one-free offer tied to a supplier deal, based on what the target customer wants. [L1076.071]
- Monthly promotions usually run for three to five days, announced to the email list and on social media. [L1281.036]
- Each month can follow a fixed theme. Example rotating offer calendar, one offer per month and reused across the year:

| Month | Offer |
|---|---|
| September | 10% off |
| October | Free white glove delivery |
| November | Bonus gift with all orders over $1,000 |
| December | Free bonus gift with all orders |

  [L0757.041]
- Offer calendar layout: one row per month, with a scheduled promotion window of 3 to 5 days. Recycled offers can be reused in later quarters with a new twist.

| Offer | Offer eligibility | Scheduled month | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Free bonus item (e.g. a recipe pack) | Any purchase of a specific starter kit | June | Tie the note to an upcoming holiday, e.g. a July 4th barbecue |
| Free physical product (e.g. a book) | Any qualifying order | July | Short personal note explaining the gift |
| Prize entry (e.g. a trip) | Any order above $300 enters the customer in the draw | August | Note explaining the entry and the prize |

  [L1205.024]
- Four promotion types exist: monetary promotions, bundles, physical product bonuses and digital product bonuses. Each is used to turn existing leads who have not bought into customers. [L1205.004]
- A sale or promotion section can be linked in the header and refreshed about once a month. Sales are allowed when a supplier is discontinuing a product or when a promotion bundles extra items. [L1281.037]
- Sales mainly during Black Friday, with otherwise selling out each collection before moving to the next. Do not discount constantly. [L1438.033]
- Sources differ on how many sales a year to run. Monthly promotions are advised in the rotation rules above [L0334.018, L1094.078, L1205.017]. Sell-out-by-collection with Black Friday as the main sale is advised in [L1438.033]. Use the monthly rhythm for a rotating offer arsenal, and the collection rhythm for a range sold in collections.

### Offer types and gift structures

- Percent-off offers are the simplest monthly promotion. Raise your usual coupon for a short window, for example from 5 percent to 10 percent off all orders. [L1204.026]
- Fixed-amount offers work best on higher-priced products, for example save 50 dollars on all orders over 500 dollars. [L1204.026]
- Free expedited shipping works well in November and December, when customers worry about delivery before the holidays. Promote a cutoff time, for example 5 p.m. for same-day or 24-hour dispatch, or a supplier ship-by date about a week before Christmas. It can be run for only part of the catalog if the supplier does not offer it store-wide. [L1204.029]
- Buy-one-get-one-free offers work especially well in November around Black Friday and Cyber Monday. Shoppers buy extra units as gifts, or buy two and keep one and give one away. [L1205.007]
- A bundle promotion can run for roughly one week within a chosen month, not the whole month. Example: a golf bag sold at $400 with a $100 carrier upgrade. A single $500 bundle that adds golf balls, a glove and a cleaning kit is a plausible one-week offer. [L1205.028]
- A bundle can include a free gift and a free ebook download, shown clearly in the ad so the customer sees the deal immediately. [L0692.054]
- Choice offer: the customer picks one of two free gifts. It keeps a product offer fresh and can rescue results when the old offer is worn out. Examples: "Pick your free gift: a travel pouch or a bottle of cleaner with any order." and "Choose one free bonus: an extra case or a matching cable with your purchase." [L0950.043]
- Seasonal relabelling: keep the same discount and shipping offer, but rename it for the season, such as Black Friday or Cyber Monday, to make it look new. Examples: 10% off plus free shipping for Black Friday; 10% off plus free shipping for Cyber Monday week. [L1085.063] *Older advice (2018).*
- Tie promotions to current events, holidays or seasonal moments where possible. The more timely and relevant the promotion, the more interest and urgency it creates and the more likely older leads will convert. [L1205.023]
- Any seasonal reason can justify a discount event, including Halloween, a spring sale or a holiday. Short, recurring sales give buyers a reason to act now. Keep the offer clear and time-limited so the reason to buy stays believable. [L1363.058]
- Non-discount promotions can compete with large retailers, for example free white glove shipping, free expedited delivery for holiday timing, a raffle entry, or a bonus gift valued at around 100 dollars. [L1113.071] *Older advice (2022).*
- Gift-with-purchase offers work during the holidays because the buyer can keep the gift or give it to someone else. The perceived value of the gift is often higher than its cost to the store. [L0467.060] *Older advice (2019).*

**Gift-with-purchase formula for expensive products** *(older advice, 2019)*
- Pattern: a percentage discount combined with a free gift above a spend threshold.
- Formula: Save [X]% and get a free [gift] on orders over $[threshold].
- Example 1: Save 10% and get a free gift box on orders over $500.
- Example 2: Save 15% and get a free set of RGB lights on orders over $1,500.
- It works because the discount keeps the price appealing, the gift adds value the buyer can also give to someone else, and the threshold raises the average order. [L0467.058]
- Case: a store with high-margin products ran a holiday promotion giving a discount plus a free gift. The gifts included an $80 item and a set of RGB lights costing the store about $50. The RGB lights offer was designed to push orders toward roughly $2,000 per customer. [L0467.059]

**Free gift threshold** *(older advice, 2018)*
- Pattern: a percentage discount plus a free gift on orders above a set amount, used only in peak sales periods by stores that already know their numbers.
- The gift should be worth about $50 to $100 and be something the buyer can keep or give away.
- Example 1: 10% off plus a free $80 gadget on orders over $1,000.
- Example 2: 15% off plus a free $60 accessory on orders over $500. [L1085.064]
- Use it only where the store knows its average order value and margin, and only around holidays and sale days, not all year. Set the threshold above the average order value so the gift is affordable. [L1085.065]
- Gift-friendly offers can sell products that would not normally be bought as gifts. During holiday shopping, buyers are already choosing presents for family, so a free gift removes one decision and pushes them toward your store. [L1085.066]
- *Current figures (2024):* A low-cost gift unrelated to the product, sent to buyers before the holiday deadline, can drive sales when inventory is limited. The gift must be worth more to the customer than its cost of about $80 shipped. [L1285.082]

### Recycling offers

- Recycle proven offers two to three times a year, or every quarter or six months, rather than always creating new ones. Repeat the offers that keep converting with the audience. [L1204.042]
- Sources differ on frequency. [L1204.042] says two to three times a year; [L1205.017] says every quarter or every six months, with a new twist; [L1404.032] says cycle offer types at most about once a year, and a BOGO that performed strongly at launch can fail on a repeat run. Use the once-a-year limit for offer types that have already faded.
- Over 90% of visitors to a dropship store are first-time visitors who came from a paid ad for a specific product. Because the audience is new each time, the same offer can be recycled very frequently without losing effect. [L1085.062] *Older advice (2018).*
- An offer that was used before can still work if it is packaged as a Black Friday or Cyber Monday deal. [L1223.013]
- Vary offer types and cycle them at most about once a year. [L1404.032]
- Repeated offers stop converting after a few weeks. Use a different offer for each promotion instead of the same discount, such as 5% off. [L1223.007]
- Repeating a 5 percent offer five times gets fewer responses because customers have already seen it. [L1285.080]
- Repeating the same discount on each promotion trains the audience to ignore it. Plan the offers in advance so none is recycled within the same quarter. [L1224.007]
- A holiday promotion using a repeated discount with a holiday name, such as 10 percent off with a holiday name, loses impact after the first use. [L1287.056]
- Each promotion in the quarter needs its own unique offer. Thanksgiving has no separate offer when it runs together with Black Friday. [L1222.003]
- Keep the offer you already run, such as free shipping, 5% off or 10% off, but change the coupon code to a holiday-related word. Announce the holiday promotion on Facebook and send the same promotion to your email list. Holidays are a sales opportunity and not a reason to pause making money. [L0357.068] *Older advice (2019).*

### Audiences for promotions

- A promotion works best when it is shown to a warm audience that has engaged for months, because the goodwill makes the offer feel natural. A buy-one-get-one or free-add-on promotion timed to a holiday converts far better in front of that group than cold. Examples in the same pattern: "Order a set of two candles this week and get a third free for the holidays" and "Buy the starter brewing kit now and get the seasonal ingredient pack free." [L0487.050]
- Time product offers to promotions and seasonal peaks such as summer, Black Friday and Christmas. Keep building the audience between them. Regular content can mention products with a link in the description without being a sales video. [L0487.048]
- Wait before making offers to a new audience so it can grow and trust the brand. The example suggested about six weeks. Making offers right away is possible if resources exist to sell and grow at the same time. [L0487.049]
- Promotions to move less popular products or seasonal items work best when sent to warm audiences such as your email list and remarketing audiences of past visitors. New visitors from cold traffic usually buy what they searched for, regardless of promotion. [L0801.052] *Older advice (2021).*
- Example warm audience size: around 2,000 people on your email list and around 10,000 people in a remarketing audience. Push promotions to those groups first rather than to new visitors. [L0801.053] *Older advice (2021).*
- Run a discount or flash sale every month, targeted at one segment of the email list. Revenue was higher in every month with a significant discount, so brands that fear discounting for premium image should test it. Online competition for attention and inbox space makes a sale a strong tool. [L0334.018]
- Sales event opt-ins can stop visitors from buying immediately, because they wait for the event. Run them close to the event rather than too far in advance to limit that effect. [L0571.023] *Older advice (2021-22, Source B).*
- Run giveaways before Black Friday. This builds early interest at low CPM, and the engaged audience can then be converted during Black Friday. [L0797.021]
- Source conflict on channel priority: for Black Friday and Cyber Monday in the first month of a new store with a small email list, storefront promotions matter more than email campaigns. Make the store's promotions match the holiday, and add them once the first suppliers are live. [L1116.061] Warm-audience targeting in [L0801.052, L0801.053] applies to older stock and promotions aimed at existing buyers.
- Promotions aimed at the store's existing buyers did not move slower products in one 2021 case. Results were mediocre, and select brands on sale up to 20 percent off sold few units, while regular best sellers sold more. [L0801.050] *Older advice (2021).*
- Case, 2021: a store ran a tiered Black Friday promotion spending 400 dollars for 15 percent off, 800 dollars for 20 percent off and 1,200 dollars for 25 percent off, with average order value around 350 dollars. The next month without promotions still produced more sales than December. [L0801.050] *Older advice (2021).*

### Measuring and tuning promotions

- Store promotions perform differently from one another and affect monthly revenue, so plan a promotion calendar and track results per promotion. Use the best-performing promotions as a baseline for future months. [L0807.048]
- Coupon codes on the store were valid on Wednesdays and Sundays, chosen because traffic is similar each day. The host did not split-test the days. [L0804.089]
- Use coupon codes tied to the holiday season, such as Black Friday, Cyber Monday or Xmas. Promote them in email, on social media pinned posts and in cover images. [L1111.089] *Older advice (2020).*

### Promotion duration and timing rules

- Each offer promotion should run 3 to 5 days. Use the shorter end when quantity is limited, and the longer end when you need more hype or when the purchase is a large decision for the audience. [L0757.025]
- Each promotion runs for 3 to 5 days, and the rest of the month the normal percentage discount applies. Example: a promotion may run from January 1 to January 5 only. [L1205.018]
- Use shorter promotions when quantity is limited. Example: when a supplier allows only 20 units, the offer runs for three days or until the 20 units sell out, whichever comes first. [L1205.019]
- Use a five-day promotion for big purchase decisions, high-ticket items or when more hype needs to be built. [L1205.020]
- For larger purchases, send one pre-promotion email before the offer starts to tell the audience something big is coming and prepare them for it. [L0757.026]
- Monthly promotions of 3 to 5 days can be run, tied to a holiday or event relevant to the niche. Promotions are typically used with promo codes rather than price changes. [L1094.078]
- A promotion with a real deadline, such as a holiday, creates urgency. It can run for a single day or through a weekend, and it should be promoted with a sale announcement on the website and social media. [L0837.035]
- A 3-day Halloween promotion example: October 29 to October 31, 2024, with 10% off the whole store using the coupon code "happyhalloween", and one promotional email each day of the sale window. [L1105.005]
- A holiday promotion can be run as a short fixed window, such as Monday to Friday of one week, with a percentage-off discount code for all customers. Set the start and end dates before writing any promotional copy. [L1286.004]
- Source conflict on length: a 3-to-5-day window is standard; a five-day window is for big-ticket items [L1205.020]; a week-long window is used for Black Friday [L1115.105, L1224.005]; a one-day window is used for Cyber Monday [L1224.004, L1287.055]; a 48-hour window is used for flash sales [L1400.002]. Each applies to its own case.
- Weekday rule: end offers on a weekday, and use Friday for the more expensive offers. Tie the promotion to current events or holidays where you can. [L0757.027]
- Plan promotions at least three months ahead, covering Halloween, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Thanksgiving, Christmas and New Year, with one specific offer per event. [L1113.069] *Older advice (2022).*
- Holiday promotion routine, from 2019, applies when you have no unique offer ready: rename the existing discount code, and keep the same offer under a holiday code. [L0357.068] *Older advice (2019).*
- A holiday promotion can still be worthwhile when planned only about one week ahead. In that case, a simple change such as renaming the existing discount code to a holiday word is enough, while a unique offer requires more lead time. [L0623.024]
- A holiday promotion can be assembled, configured, announced to your email list and posted to social media in approximately one hour. A discount of 5% or 10% paired with a holiday-specific code (for example "MemorialDay" instead of a generic discount) has measurable impact even with a small audience; converting one or two additional sales justifies the effort. [L0294.008]

### Holiday planning for the US calendar

- U.S. holidays such as Memorial Day and the 4th of July, and monthly occasions, provide recurring promotional triggers throughout the year. [L0294.008]
- A national holiday such as the 4th of July is a reason to run a special promotion that differs from everyday discounts. This applies only to stores that sell to customers in the US. [L0623.001]
- If there is no time for a unique holiday offer, the fastest starting point is renaming the existing discount code to a holiday word such as "July 4th", "freedom" or "fireworks". Set that code to expire at the end of the promotion window. [L0623.002]
- A holiday promotion in the lesson ran for five days, from Monday 1 July to Friday 5 July, with the code expiring at midnight on the final day. Normal promotion codes in the same business typically ended on Wednesday or Sunday. [L0623.003]
- 4th of July promotion plan:
  1. Create a holiday-themed promotion, or rename the existing discount code to a holiday word such as "July 4th", "freedom" or "fireworks". [L0623.004]
  2. Set the code to expire at the end of the five-day window, Friday 5 July in the example. [L0623.004]
  3. Show the code on the store and promote it to new visitors coming from ads. [L0623.004]
  4. Send promotion emails to the existing email list on every day of the window, from Monday to Friday. [L0623.004]
  5. Run static holiday remarketing ads to older website visitor audiences. [L0623.004]
  6. Run dynamic product ads to recent visitors who have not bought, excluding recent customers. [L0623.004]
- Start holiday promotions weeks early, since suppliers get busy, some close, and shipping carriers are backed up in the peak shipping period before Christmas. Holiday offers should state the gift benefit clearly, such as a Christmas gift or a holiday promotion. [L0467.057] *Older advice (2019).*
- Holiday promotions were run on all dropshipping stores, so promotional calendars should be planned per store. [L1002.037] *Older advice (2019).*
- Holiday-specific bundles can be promoted earlier than the holiday, for example during Black Friday or Cyber Monday, since many people shop for gifts early. [L1285.083]
- Holiday bundles or special products do not need to wait until the holiday itself to be promoted. Testing them earlier, for example during Black Friday or Cyber Monday, is worth doing because many people shop for Christmas during those events. [L1223.015]

### Q4 planning

- Prepare five unique offers for the Q4 promotion season. Because Thanksgiving runs inside the Black Friday promotion, the five offers cover Halloween, Black Friday with Thanksgiving, Cyber Monday, Christmas and New Year. [L1221.002]
- Run five unique offers across five promotions in Q4 instead of repeating the same discount. [L1285.080]
- Use five unique offers for the five holiday promotions instead of repeating the same discount, such as 5% off, because repeated offers stop converting after a few weeks. Each offer should give customers a different reason to buy now. [L1223.007]
- Plan a promotion for each Q4 event: Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas and New Year. [L0626.023]
- Create the promotional images and promotional email copy for each event before it starts. [L0626.023]
- If a store has no Black Friday promotion ready, it is getting late, but it is still possible to plan and launch one before the event. [L0626.023]
- Existing store offers, such as a site-wide discount code or free premium white-glove shipping, can be the basis for one of the five holiday promotions. Each promotion still needs a different offer, not the same discount repeated. [L1223.033]
- Plan at least the next three months covering the holidays. To prepare holiday promotions:
  1. List every holiday that can carry a promotion. [L1113.070]
  2. Choose one offer for each event. [L1113.070]
  3. Create the Klaviyo emails for each promotion. [L1113.070]
  4. Create the images for Facebook remarketing ads. [L1113.070]
  5. Create the images for Google display ads. [L1113.070]
  [L1113.070] *Older advice (2022).*
- Set the start and end dates of the promotion before writing any promotional copy. [L1286.004]
- Use a single discount code applied to all customers, such as 10% off, combined with a delivery promise before a holiday deadline. Only promise a delivery date you can actually meet with your supplier and shipping times. [L0620.003]
- Q4 is estimated to add about 30 percent to monthly revenue. The actual gain scales with the current revenue level. [L1116.041]
- Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales rise even without specific promotions. Once the first suppliers are live, add holiday-relevant promotions on the store to boost sales further. [L1116.040]
- Change the sale reason every three to four days, following a calendar: early November sale, 11.11, pre-Black Friday, Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas and end-of-year sale. [L1194.040]
- A monthly offer-brainstorming exercise can produce around 12 unique offers. Keep a running list so you always have spares. [L1224.008]

**Q4 calendar 2024** (keep the same five-promotion cycle every year and adjust only the dates)

| Promotion | Promotion day | Email sends | Offer closes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Halloween | Thu 31 Oct | Tue 29 Oct, Wed 30 Oct, Thu 31 Oct, plus an extension email Fri 1 Nov (4 emails in total) | Fri 1 Nov |
