| Thanksgiving + Black Friday | Thu 28 Nov (Thanksgiving) / Fri 29 Nov (Black Friday) | Mon 25 Nov, Tue 26 Nov, Wed 27 Nov, Thu 28 Nov, Fri 29 Nov (5 emails) | Fri 29 Nov |
| Cyber Monday | Mon 2 Dec | Early morning 2 Dec and about 17:00 on 2 Dec (2 emails) | 2 Dec (one day only) |
| Christmas (buy-by reminder, not a sale on 25 Dec) | Wed 25 Dec | Mon 9 Dec, Wed 11 Dec, Fri 13 Dec (3 emails) | Not a discount close; the message is the last order date before Christmas |
| New Year's | Wed 1 Jan | Mon 30 Dec, Wed 1 Jan, Fri 3 Jan (3 emails) | Fri 3 Jan |

Remarketing ads run alongside the emails for the Halloween, Black Friday, Cyber Monday and New Year's promotions. Total planned emails: 17. [L1223.009]

**Q4 calendar 2025, version A** (inferred dates marked)

| Promotion | Start | End | Emails | Remarketing ads |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Halloween | Wed 29 Oct | Fri 31 Oct (Halloween) | 3 | Yes |
| Thanksgiving + Black Friday (combined into one week) | Mon 24 Nov | Fri 28 Nov (Black Friday) | 5, one per day (Thanksgiving is mentioned in the Thursday email, 27 Nov) | Yes, whole period |
| Cyber Monday | Mon 1 Dec | Mon 1 Dec (one day) | 2 (one in the morning, one in the evening) | Yes, all day |
| Christmas | Mon 8 Dec | Fri 12 Dec | 3 | Not stated |
| New Year's | 31 Dec (New Year's Eve) | Not fixed in the lesson; must not end on New Year's Day. Fri 2 Jan 2026 fits the Friday rule (inferred) | 3 | Yes |

[L1224.004]

**Q4 calendar 2025, version B**

| Promotion | Dates | Emails | Ads | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Halloween | Oct 29 to Oct 31 (ends on Halloween) | 3 | Remarketing | Q4 starts here |
| Thanksgiving and Black Friday (combined) | Nov 24 (Mon) to Nov 28 (Fri) | 5, one per day | Remarketing for the whole week | Thanksgiving is mentioned in the Thursday email, Nov 27 |
| Cyber Monday | Dec 1 (Mon) | 2, morning and evening | Remarketing all day | One-day promotion |
| Christmas | Dec 8 (Mon) to Dec 12 (Fri) | 3, one on each of Dec 8, Dec 10, Dec 12 | Remarketing | Message: order now to get it before Christmas |
| New Year | Dec 31 to Jan 2 (Fri) | 3 | Remarketing | Ends on Friday, not on New Year's Day |

[L1287.055]

- Version A and B disagree on Christmas remarketing (not stated in A, yes in B) and on the New Year end date (fixed by the lesson in B, inferred in A). Both values stand. [L1224.004, L1287.055]
- The 2024 calendar has 17 planned emails; the 2025 calendars add up to 16 emails across the five promotions. [L1223.009, L1224.004, L1287.055]

### Halloween

- A 3-day Halloween promotion example: October 29 to October 31, 2024, with 10% off the whole store using the coupon code "happyhalloween", and one promotional email each day of the sale window. [L1105.005]
- The 2024 calendar places Halloween on Thursday 31 October, with emails on 29, 30 and 31 October and an extension email on Friday 1 November, which is also the close. [L1223.009]
- The 2025 calendar runs Halloween from Wednesday 29 October to Friday 31 October, with 3 emails and remarketing. [L1224.004, L1287.055]
- Halloween is one of the five Q4 promotions, each with its own offer. [L1221.002, L1223.008]

### Black Friday and Thanksgiving

- Black Friday and Cyber Monday sales start before Black Friday and end at midnight on Cyber Monday. Long promotions tend to perform well, and one planned campaign included seven emails. [L1090.078] *Older advice (2021).*
- Start Black Friday promotions and ads at least one day before Black Friday. [L1092.054] *Older advice (2022).*
- Start the Black Friday sale on the Monday before Thanksgiving and run it through Black Friday, rather than extending it after the event. Longer sale windows produced more sales in this approach. [L1285.102, L1224.005]
- Black Friday promotions perform better the longer they run, so start the sale before Thanksgiving rather than extending it after Black Friday. A five-day email run from Monday before Thanksgiving through Black Friday covers the Thanksgiving shopping window. [L1223.011]
- A Black Friday promotion can run as a week-long event that includes Thanksgiving Day as one of its days, rather than as a single day. [L1115.105]
- Combine Thanksgiving and Black Friday into one roughly week-long promotion instead of a month-long Black Friday event. Start it on the Monday before Thanksgiving and end it on Black Friday. [L1224.005]
- Thanksgiving is a US-specific holiday, so it is folded into the Black Friday promotion instead of getting its own campaign. The Thanksgiving-day email is themed around the holiday and repeats the discount code. [L1223.012]
- For Black Friday and Cyber Monday, use aggressive discounts. Discounts of 60 to 70 percent perform best; discounts above 80 percent tend to be seen as too good to be true and convert worse. You may need to raise the product price beforehand so the discount still leaves a margin you can afford. [L0387.008]
- Escalating discount ladder for an event: start one month before the event with 20% off, then increase weekly to 30%, 40%, 50% and 60%. Raise prices incrementally as discounts increase to maintain margin and justify the perceived value of the offer. [L0309.001]
- Run your main event (Black Friday or Christmas) with increasing offer pressure. [L0309.004]
- Black Friday pricing on Google Shopping depends on the supplier agreements. Without a MAP restriction, lower the price in Shopify so the lower price shows in Google Shopping. [L1092.052] *Older advice (2022).*
- A bundle offer can run across the Black Friday to Cyber Monday window on the store, while Google Shopping ads stay unchanged. Promote the bundle on-site and through email and remarketing rather than as a Shopping promotion. [L1088.093] *Older advice (2020).*
- Pre-sale momentum: build it with a smaller offer that grows toward Black Friday, rather than a long sale that runs for weeks. Keep the largest offer for Black Friday itself. [L1225.005]
- A 24-hour sale extension is a common Black Friday tactic, especially for brands that rely on acquiring new customers, but it weakens urgency if the messaging is careless. [L1225.001]
- Black Friday and Cyber Monday are a sales dip-and-peak cycle. A sales dip before Black Friday and again in January and February is a normal ebb and flow for e-commerce, so plan cash flow around it rather than reacting with panic. [L1225.013]
- Free express shipping is used near shipping cut-off dates, for example before Black Friday, to let customers receive orders earlier and squeeze a few extra days of sales. [L1404.022]
- Source note on Black Friday timing: the minimum for ads is one day before (2022) [L1092.054]; the planned start is the Monday before Thanksgiving [L1224.005, L1285.102]. Both apply, with the Monday start as the plan.

### Cyber Monday

- Cyber Monday works as a separate promotion just two days after Black Friday in the lesson's wording, because shoppers actively look for deals and respond to repackaged offers. The 2024 calendar places it on Monday 2 December. [L1223.013, L1223.009]
- The 2025 calendar runs Cyber Monday on Monday 1 December as a one-day promotion, with 2 emails (one in the morning and one in the evening) and remarketing all day. [L1224.004, L1287.055]
- Cyber Monday 2024: early morning 2 December and about 17:00 on 2 December, two emails, one day only. [L1223.009]

### Christmas planning

- Christmas promotions run before Christmas rather than on Christmas Day, with a message that the buy-by date for delivery before the holiday is closing. Allowing about two weeks for delivery, the emails go out in early-to-mid December. [L1223.014]
- The target is gift buyers and customers who want high-ticket or bulky items, such as furniture or appliances, in time for the holiday. [L1223.014]
- The Christmas offer message is "order now to get your product before Christmas". It drives a large spike, and it only works if suppliers can deliver within about two weeks. Confirm delivery times first. [L1224.009]
- The Christmas promotion runs early in December because shoppers have usually finished their spending by Christmas and are less active online then. The main message is to order now to receive the product before Christmas, which drives a sharp spike in sales. [L1287.058]
- Example Christmas offer: discount code "xmas" gives 10 percent off, combined with a guarantee that the product will be delivered before Christmas. This only works if the supplier can deliver within about two weeks. [L1287.059]
- The 2025 September call paired a 10% off code with a guarantee that orders arrive before Christmas Day. [L1286.004]
- The 2024 Christmas calendar uses emails on Monday 9, Wednesday 11 and Friday 13 December, with the message as the last order date before Christmas. [L1223.009]
- The 2025 Christmas calendar runs Monday 8 December to Friday 12 December, with 3 emails on 8, 10 and 12 December. [L1224.004, L1287.055]
- Plan a Christmas sale for the second week of December so products can be delivered before the holiday. [L1090.079] *Older advice (2021).*
- Christmas offers can be smaller than Black Friday because most of the buying has already been done. [L1225.006]
- For a gift product, frame Christmas offers as gifting rather than as a buy-one-get-one deal. Use an experiential gift angle for buyers purchasing for a partner or themselves. [L1225.014]
- Example Christmas structure: free express shipping above a spend threshold plus around 20% off site-wide, with an added gift to keep the momentum. This should be lower than the Black Friday deal so the Black Friday buyers are not upset. [L1225.016]
- Gift wrapping: in Q4, offer free gift wrapping on high-tier bundles for giftable products to increase appeal to customers buying gifts for others. [L0232.009]
- Christmas packaging upsell: offer a simple checkbox on the checkout cart for "Add holiday gift packaging". It costs $0.50 to produce and sells for $3-4, adding $3-4 profit per order with no friction if presented as a non-intrusive option. [L0309.008]
- Gift-friendly holiday bundle example: a holiday offer can bundle the main product with a free gift and a free ebook download. [L0692.054]
- Source note on gift wrap and packaging: free gift wrapping on high-tier bundles [L0232.009] and a paid packaging checkbox at $3-4 [L0309.008] are both valid; the first is a bundle benefit, the second an add-on.

### Holiday shipping cut-offs

- Stop selling Christmas-delivery products by December 10-12 to guarantee shipping arrives before the holiday. Cutoff dates prevent logistics bottlenecks in late December when carrier capacity is exhausted and packages routinely arrive after December 25. [L0309.007]
- Non-seasonal products (jewelry, gadgets, apparel, lifestyle items) can continue selling past December 10-12 for Christmas gifts if you clearly state on the order page that Christmas delivery is not guaranteed. Manage customer expectations through follow-up emails. [L0309.043]
- Seasonal products (Christmas ornaments, holiday decorations) must stop by December 12 to prevent chargebacks. Organic channels with higher margins tolerate late-delivery complaints better than paid ads with lower margins. [L0309.043]
- Procedure for the cutoff:
  1. Add a clear cutoff date to your website and order confirmation page stating "Orders placed after [date] cannot be guaranteed Christmas delivery." [L0309.012]
  2. Include a 24-hour cancellation window on the confirmation email for customers who miss the cutoff. [L0309.012]
  3. Update your refund policy to reflect that late-placed orders are ineligible for refunds due to shipping timing beyond your control. [L0309.012]
- Holiday deadline: for the 2025 calendar, Christmas runs 8 to 12 December, within the 10-12 December cutoff range. [L0309.007, L1224.004]
- Expedited shipping offers: promote a cutoff time or a supplier ship-by date about a week before Christmas. [L1204.029]

### New Year

- Plan a New Year sale running from December 31 into January 1 as an end-of-year promotion. [L1090.079] *Older advice (2021).*
- Avoid ending a promotion on New Year's Day, when buyers are usually least active. [L1224.006]
- Promotions should end on a Friday, because people are typically paid on Friday and spend more then. Avoid ending a promotion on New Year's Day, when many shoppers are not buying. [L1224.006, L1287.057]
- The 2024 calendar runs New Year from Monday 30 December with Wednesday 1 January and Friday 3 January emails, closing Friday 3 January. [L1223.009]
- The 2025 calendar runs New Year from 31 December, ending on Friday 2 January 2026 (see calendar version A, inferred). [L1224.004]
- The 2025 calendar version B runs New Year from 31 December to Friday 2 January. [L1287.055]
- Source conflict: the 2021 advice ends the New Year sale on January 1 [L1090.079]; the 2025 rule avoids ending on New Year's Day [L1224.006, L1287.057]. The later rule applies for new plans.

### Closing the Friday rule for promotions

- Close promotions on a Friday whenever possible, because people are paid on Fridays and will otherwise ask for more time. If a promotion must end on Wednesday or Thursday, send an extra email on the Friday after with the same offer extended by one day. [L1223.010]
- Promotions should end on a Friday because people are typically paid on Friday and spend more then. [L1224.006]

### Flash sales

- Flash sales are time-limited promotions with a strong offer, such as a high-ticket item at a deep discount, a product bundle, or a trending seasonal product. Pairing the time limit with limited stock creates urgency and triggers fear of missing out. [L1399.002]
- Flash sale pattern: a promotion limited to a fixed time window, built around a high-value offer such as a high-ticket item at a deep discount, a product bundle, or a trending seasonal product. Pairing the time limit with limited stock creates urgency and taps into fear of missing out (FOMO). Example: a 48-hour sale on a premium bundle with only 50 units available. [L1400.002]
- Pair flash sales with a fixed window: a 48-hour window on a premium bundle with 50 units, or a three-day window when quantity is limited. [L1400.002, L1205.019]
- A monthly flash sale aimed at one email segment raises revenue in each month with a significant discount. [L0334.018]

### End-of-season clearance

- End-of-season discounts clear stock that needs to leave quickly. Shoppers also tend to wait for a sale before buying big-ticket items to get the largest savings. [L1399.011]
- End-of-season discounts clear out stock that needs to leave quickly. Shoppers also tend to delay purchases of big-ticket items until a sale, so a seasonal discount captures that demand. [L1400.008]
- Mystery box: a "trust us" bundle used to clear unsold or weaker stock. It only works if the brand keeps enough real quality in the box, otherwise the offer produces many returns and complaints. Because it can usually be run only once, the box can be a limited edition created for that one promotion. [L1404.020]
- A brand that runs frequent sales can keep its image by building a strong brand experience around promotions, including the ordering experience and merchandising, planned in advance so that the discounting is part of the brand. [L1404.021]
- Clearance pricing for an extension: 70% off, with the message "Almost out of stock: Black Friday stock still available". [L0309.004]
- Sell out each collection, then move on to the next collection, rather than discounting constantly. [L1438.033]

### Extending a promotion

- Continue the promotion for two weeks after the event concludes. In the extension period, message as "Almost out of stock: Black Friday stock still available" and offer 70% off to clear inventory. [L0309.004]
- A 24-hour sale extension is a common Black Friday tactic. It weakens urgency if the messaging is careless. Extend only the same offer under the same terms, because adding deeper discounts or a new offer for late buyers beyond early loyal customers creates trouble. [L1225.001]
- Only change or extend an offer if the team can execute it fully, since a new offer means a website redo, updated email flows and new ad creatives. Extending a sale without proper execution hurts more than it helps, while a long sale period risks offer fatigue. [L1225.003]
- If you extend the sale or change the offer after Black Friday, make it exclusive to BFCM so the Black Friday offer keeps its value. [L1225.006]
- Extension email: if an event ends on Thursday or Wednesday, send a Friday email with the same offer extended by one day. [L1223.010]
- The 2024 Halloween calendar uses a Friday extension email on 1 November, which is also the close. [L1223.009]
- Source conflict: the extension of the 2024 Halloween and Black Friday sales is one day in the Friday rule [L1223.010]; the two-week extension at 70% off is a clearance tactic [L0309.004]. Use the one-day extension for offers still in their promotion, and the two-week clearance only for stock to be cleared.

### Closing a promotion

- Close an expired offer:
  1. Swap the expired offer for your default offer. [L0757.039]
  2. Revert every change made for the offer: remove it from the notification bar and the exit intent overlay. [L0757.039]
  3. Revert the offer-dependent changes: custom product pages, content pages, edited product pages, and the Bold Sales Motivator threshold display. [L0757.039]
- Leaving an expired offer on the site after it ends, including banners, overlays, pages and progress trackers, sends shoppers to a dead promotion. Always swap in the default offer and undo every promotion change. [L0757.040]
- Set the code to expire at the end of the promotion window so it stays time-bound and relevant to the holiday. [L0623.002]
- Always keep a default offer, such as 5 or 10 percent off, live after a promotion ends. People click promotional emails weeks later, and they should still find something to buy at a fair price. [L1204.053]
- Promotions should end on weekdays, with Friday used for the more expensive offers. [L0757.027]
- Use a sell-out close for limited stock: the offer runs for three days or until the 20 units sell out, whichever comes first. [L1205.019]

### Default offer after promotions

- Keep a default offer, such as 5 or 10 percent off, live after every promotion ends. [L1204.053]
- When an offer expires, swap in the default offer and undo every promotion change on the site. [L0757.039, L0757.040]

### Discount depth

- Escalate by week before a large event: 20%, then 30%, 40%, 50%, 60%. [L0309.001]
- For Black Friday and Cyber Monday: 60-70% performs best; above 80% converts worse. [L0387.008]
- For monthly promotions: raise the usual coupon for a short window, for example 5% to 10% off all orders. [L1204.026]
- A repeated 5 percent offer loses responses after several uses. [L1285.080]
- Source conflict: 60-70% is the best range for Black Friday and Cyber Monday [L0387.008]; the 70% clearance is for stock after the event [L0309.004]; the monthly rule is a 5-10% coupon [L1204.026]. Each figure applies to its own promotion type.

#### Further details

Run holiday promotions each month, with ad campaigns built for the biggest dates:
1. Plan a promotion for each month, with the largest ones around the holiday season.
2. Build ad campaigns for the specific promotion, such as Black Friday, Cyber Monday, Christmas or New Year.
3. Create the promotion in Google Shopping promotions where available.
[L0470.064]

Running a bundle promotion that is not a Google Shopping promotion:
1. Leave the Google Shopping ads unchanged for the promotion period.
2. Show a notification bar on the website that promotes the bundle during the promotion window.
3. Use an exit intent overlay that announces the bundle price and links to the bundle page.
4. Email the store's list about the bundle.
5. Run remarketing ads on Facebook and Google to visitors who have not yet purchased.
[L1088.053]

## Testing offers and diagnosing weak conversion

### Offer test method and budgets

#### Offer structures to test

- Offer types that improved conversion when pre-click metrics were strong but sales were low: BOGO (Buy One Get One Free), Buy 2 Get 1 Free, Buy 2 Get 50% Off, a free gift item bundled with the purchase (even a $1-$2 item), and tiered bundle packages labelled Single, Duo, or Trio. These create the perception of value and urgency, turning browsers into buyers. [L0229.013]
- Free gifts often outperformed bundles and percentage discounts in one operator's stores. Buy-more-save-more offers (such as buy 2 get 10% off, buy 3 get 20% off) also worked. Choose a gift relevant to the audience's problem, and test many offers. [L1315.029]
- Buy 2, get the third free worked well for a collection of multi-item products. A single-product store with buy 2, get 2 free also worked where the product could be used in multiples. [L0388.010]
- Buy-one-get-second-free or 50 percent off the second item worked best in countries such as Australia and New Zealand. In Europe, more refined offers were needed. [L1131.011]
- Different bundle offers convert differently, so test several at the beginning instead of betting on one. [L1299.003]
- Test several offer structures, such as buy one, buy two and get one free, or buy three and get one free, because it is not possible to know in advance which offer converts best. Keep the margin check in place so every bundle is still profitable. [L0692.012]
- Keep offers simple: a quantity discount or a clear bundle is easier for customers than complex options. Test simple offers before complicated bundles. [L0773.042]
- Design many candidate offers relevant to the customer and test them against each other to see which one wins, rather than relying on one offer. [L0919.023]
- Offer test with an introductory report: write two or more versions, each with a different angle or report name, and run them before choosing one. Change only the emphasis and naming, keeping the same product, so the result shows which angle the audience responds to. Keep the best performer and drop the others. [L1356.074]

**Necklace case: four offer variants tested**

| Variant | Offer | Result | Id |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | One necklace €19.90 alone | Minimal conversions | L0233.017 |
| 2 | One necklace €29.90 + second necklace free | Moderate conversions | L0233.017 |
| 3 | One necklace €29.90 + free gift + free styling ebook | Good conversions | L0233.017 |
| 4 | One necklace €29.90 + second necklace free + storage box + style guide + free shipping | Highest conversions ("game-changer") | L0233.017 |

- Variant 4 succeeded because customers perceived €80+ in value for €29.90 while the seller kept healthy margins. The bundled offer made saying no emotionally difficult. [L0233.017]
- A necklace store ran ads with a good CTR but had no offer, and customers were expected to pay the full price of about $35. The owner switched to a buy-one-get-one-free offer, where buying the main necklace gave the second best-selling item free. Daily sales rose to roughly 1k to 4k per day (currency not stated, likely USD). The offer alone made the difference. [L1469.004]
- Charging $30 for a single necklace with no bundle or compelling offer can stop a store from converting even when the audience is right. Test several offers to find one that makes sense to customers, especially for digital products, and check the conversion rate first. [L0678.020]

#### Price and copy variables

- Vary bundle structure and unit pricing together, not just the discount percentage. Test single-product pricing against bundle structure against per-unit price combinations. Example: a home decor product failed at $29.90 as a single price and at "buy 1 get 1 free," but succeeded at "buy 2 get 2 free" with an increased per-unit price. The new price point plus bundle structure created perceived value that matched customer expectations. The optimal offer combines discount depth, bundle structure, and unit pricing. [L0307.035]
- Treat the name of a bundle offer as a variable you can test separately. Keep the same bundle, try different names, and keep the name that converts better. [L0549.054]
- Stores A/B test price and offer together, for example $71.91 versus $58.46 for the same product. Test both variants and compare the results before choosing a price. [L1164.038]
- A lower average order value can still earn more if conversion rises enough. Test each offer and track AOV and conversion rate together, never AOV alone. [L1164.044]

#### Testing cadence and rotation

- Test a new offer roughly every two days during the testing phase to find which converts best, rather than keeping one offer for a long time. [L1216.006]
- Run one offer at a time, not several side by side. Let each offer run for two to three days. Check the conversion rate and the CTR. If the CTR is high but there are no sales, replace the offer with a new one. Keep testing new offers about every two days until the store conversion rate is above two to three percent. [L0502.024]
- Rotate offers every second day and track add-to-cart, checkout and purchase numbers:
  1. Day 1: lower the price.
  2. Day 2: create a bundle, for example a bundle at 50% off.
  3. Day 3: buy two, get a third free, for products where several units can be used.
  4. Day 4: buy one and receive a complementary product as a bonus.

  Keep the offer that produces the most add-to-cart actions and purchases. [L0388.009]
- Judge an offer change over several days of data, not a single spike. One store changed its offer to a free second item while adding new interest audiences almost daily. The first morning brought about 250 sales, but sales then dropped to about one sale per day for the rest of the day. [L0642.056]
- Offer tests can be run from day one, for example with buy one get one free, because a weak offer can be the reason a product fails. Changing the offer is a test to run before deciding to abandon a product. [L0648.040]
- Example result: a store with 10 ad sets at $5 per day and about $30 total spend produced two sales and about $60 profit after ad spend and product costs. [L0650.044]

#### Test setup

- Duplicate the product in Shopify, or build the offer in funnel software. Duplicate products make store analytics messy, so send all traffic to one product page to keep the data easier to analyse. [L0502.044]
- Keep a winning product page running while testing a new offer. Duplicate the product and landing page, attach the new offer to the duplicate, remove the duplicate from collections so only ad traffic reaches it, and run traffic to it separately. [L1315.027]
- Offer test setup:
  1. Duplicate the product in Shopify.
  2. Duplicate the product page (landing page) or create a new template in Shopify.
  3. Attach the new page to the duplicated product.
  4. Remove it from all collections so only paid traffic can reach it.
  5. Run traffic to the separate product and compare it to the original. [L1315.028]

#### Judging results

- Treat the name of a bundle, the copy angle and the price as separate variables, and change one at a time so the result shows which factor responds. [L1356.074, L0549.054]
- Only judge the product after several offers have been tested over enough time. [L1131.014]
- Test offers before giving up on the product, because the offer is the first lever to improve landing-page conversion. Work through BOGO, volume discounts, and complementary bundles one at a time. [L1345.027]

#### Budgets per offer test

| Situation | Budget or rule | Id |
|---|---|---|
| Product costs about $30 | $10 daily per asset is suitable for testing; such products are much easier to sell than much more expensive ones | L1107.031 |
| Product costs about $70 to $136 | $10 daily per asset is too small because sales come slowly; use a bigger budget per asset. Suggested testing budget: half of the break-even cost per purchase | L1107.030 |
| Real testing budget | Run an A/B test of two completely different offers, then put more budget behind the winner. Do not test two small variations of the same offer | L1299.004 |
| Tight budget | Run a basic offer already known to convert, such as BOGO, buy 1 get 2, or buy 3 | L1299.005 |
| Per product test | Spend up to about $300 per product before judging it. Stop early at $10 to $50 if CTR stays below 1% or CPC is above €1 | L1316.037 |
| Early data good, no sales | If CTR and CPC look good but the product does not sell, change the theme and test different offers and prices before abandoning the product. A bad CTR or CPC early on is a reason to stop sooner | L1316.038 |

### Testing sequence

- Offer testing is a loop. Create an initial offer that bundles multiple value-adds (bonus items, free shipping, guarantees, discounts). Launch ads and monitor CTR and store conversion rate daily. If ads achieve high CTR but store conversion is low, the offer is weak and needs redesign. Test a completely new offer every 2 days. Continue until one converts consistently at acceptable margins. If lacking ideas, consult the resource PDF for more offer templates and examples. Once the offer converts reliably, proceed to price optimisation. [L0233.019]
- Fix the offer and the fundamentals before any cosmetic changes. Improving the conversion rate of existing traffic is the lowest-hanging fruit and yields a better return before buying new traffic. [L0414.015] *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*
- When traffic is good but conversion is weak, first check whether the offer is a CURV offer. If it is not, fixing the offer comes before testing anything else. [L0473.013] *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*
- Compare the offer directly with competitor offers, because customers compare them anyway even when the business does not. Many businesses under-review this step. [L0473.014] *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*
- Check that the offer matches the traffic. If visitors search for something that is not visible in the offer when they land, the offer must change. [L0473.015] *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*
- If conversion is unsatisfying, test different offers before changing other parts of the funnel. Several offers should be tested until a good conversion rate is found. [L0678.021]
- Test the offer before changing the product. The offer (price, branding, USP, promotion, guarantee) plays a large role in conversion. Testing is the only way to know whether a bundle or promotion works for a niche. [L1259.025]
- Test a price cut only after simplifying the page and removing hidden costs. In one case the seller had already lowered the price with little effect, and break-even was already tight at about 2.3x. [L0950.044]
- Bundle discounts can work. Test whether the bundle path is tracked correctly and whether customers see the bundle before deciding whether to lower the price. [L0950.045]
- Price and offer testing are central to finding profit. In one lesson's experience, around 20 offers were tested and only two were profitable enough to scale, so testing many offers is expected. [L0999.018]
- When a product with high CTR gets no sales, test new offers and prices, including a price below cost, to find out whether price is the barrier. If sales come, then improve the perceived value. [L1432.024]
- When a product underperforms, test different offers, countries and age groups before dropping it. Focus budget on the countries that show sales and make sense for the product. [L1017.013]
- Offer testing for a new product with weak sales:
  1. Define the core problem the product solves and the core promise.
  2. Test several offers, such as a price change, a bundle, or a free complementary product.
  3. Compare results per country, because offers that work in one market can fail in another.
  4. Only judge the product after several offers have been tested over enough time. [L1131.014]
- A clear special offer made the difference between no sales and sales for a store with a weak website. Improving the offer, images and customer contact were done together. [L1439.035]
- Test several offers for a product before deciding. Offer structure is not known in advance, so the test decides. [L0692.012]

### When to blame the offer, traffic or page

- High CTR with low store conversion means people see the ad and click, but the offer does not convince them. The offer, not the ad, is then the first thing to change. [L0502.023]
- If ads get a CTR above 1.5% but produce no add-to-carts or sales, the offer is a likely cause and should be tested. Try different offers before assuming the ad or the page is the problem. [L1400.001]
- If the ad metrics are poor but cost-per-click and clicks are fine, the offer itself may be weak. Compare your offer with competitors' offers, or ask a coach for feedback on improving it. [L0874.015]
- Note the two CTR thresholds: 1% CTR (with CPC above €1) is the early stop signal in a budget test [L1316.037]; above 1.5% CTR with no add-to-carts points to the offer [L1400.001].
- Check that the offer matches the traffic (see Testing sequence). [L0473.015] *(older advice, Source B 2021-22)*

### Diagnosing carts without sales

- A store gets traffic and add-to-carts but no sales. Diagnose in this order:
  1. Check the Shopify orders for abandoned checkouts and see whether customers entered their contact details at checkout.
  2. If customers do not enter their information at all, test new offers and rotate a new offer every two days until one converts.
  3. If customers enter their information but do not complete the purchase, check that the payment providers are working.
  4. Also check the shipping options at checkout.
  5. Keep the offer test running for the period the store is spending on ads. [L1216.005]
- Test a new offer roughly every two days during the testing phase (see Testing cadence). [L1216.006]
- Test a price cut only after simplifying the page and removing hidden costs. [L0950.044]

### Testing by market

- Test geographic markets with one ad set per country, using a top-five analysis to see which country wins. Test English-language markets as well as local-language markets, because the sales approach determines which countries respond. [L1315.047]
- Buy-one-get-second-free or 50 percent off the second item worked best in Australia and New Zealand. In Europe, more refined offers were needed. [L1131.011]
- Compare results per country, because offers that work in one market can fail in another. [L1131.014]
- When a product underperforms, test different offers, countries and age groups before dropping it. Focus budget on the countries that show sales and make sense for the product. [L1017.013]

### Pre-launch checklists

**Store review before launching ads**

1. Have an experienced reviewer check the store, including the product descriptions and the overall look of the store.
2. Check the price against the total dropshipping cost, including the product cost and shipping, and decide the launch price together with the reviewer.
3. Check the product reviews on the store.
4. Review the ad creatives and the video, and fix any that do not show the product clearly. [L0562.031]

**Offer launch checklist**

1. Confirm the email flow for the offer is ready.
2. Confirm the Facebook ad assets for the offer are ready.
3. Confirm the website is ready and explains the offer clearly.
4. Plan the launch on the marketing calendar with enough lead time to prepare everything.
5. Check stock for each discounted product; exclude products that may sell out, or remove them from the site.
6. Set the order in which products are displayed on the website, since merchandising affects results.
7. Align the whole company, including customer service, on the offer, because a large discount generates support tickets.
8. Keep the offer and the web design simple so customers understand the deal without effort. [L1404.035]

- Before launching an offer, check that email, Facebook assets and the website are ready. Use the marketing calendar to plan enough lead time, and align the whole company, including customer service, because a sudden large discount generates tickets. Check stock levels, exclude products that might sell out, or remove them from the site. [L1404.030]

## High-ticket, B2B and negotiated deals

### Deciding whether high-ticket and B2B fits your store

- High-ticket products are judged by the margin left after freight, returns and damage. A higher unit price alone does not guarantee profit. [L0660.024]
- Above roughly 90 to 100 USD in the US market, the store, ads and offer must build trust before the sale, because buyers research reviews and search the brand before purchasing at this price. [L0693.052]
- A high-ticket product with healthy margin leaves room for a deeper discount when one is needed to close a larger deal, but discounts should never reduce the sale below a worthwhile profit. (Older, 2019.) [L1068.056]
- Bulk orders from businesses can be given a larger discount and accepted at a lower margin, since total profit still stays high. (Older, 2020.) [L1277.082]
- Lead-generation example: mobile-only lead generation worked up to about $997 per offer, and a $30,000 program converted through mobile leads. The advertiser found no limit on mobile above that point, and desktop was not required. [L0643.058]

### Concierge, VIP and trade access

- A concierge or VIP service can be your USP, especially in business-to-business niches. Customers placing very large orders value personal contact and custom quotes, and such orders can range from $10,000 to $100,000 or more. [L0413.044]
- Pattern: tell visitors on the site that a real person is available to talk, then provide custom quotes and work with them directly on large orders. This fits B2B niches, where single orders can reach $10,000, $50,000 or $100,000. [L0413.053]
- Create a separate trade or business customer page on the store, because business buyers often want bulk discounts and negotiation. Be ready to offer custom deals. (Older, 2021.) [L0801.055]
- Show a phone number on the trade page so businesses can call directly. Discounts are based on order volume, and custom quotes are available. [L1088.056]

### Trade request procedures

**Procedure A: trade request with supplier quote (older, 2021)** [L0834.122]

1. Add a trade or business-to-business form page to your store. [L0834.122]
2. Collect the customer's name, email, phone number, company name, products of interest, quantity, and the date they need the order by. [L0834.122]
3. Request a quote from the supplier for that exact quantity, delivery location and date. [L0834.122]
4. Pass the supplier's quoted price to the customer. Do not promise a trade discount upfront, because it varies from order to order. [L0834.122]

**Procedure B: trade pricing instead of a fixed coupon (older, 2021)** [L0968.081]

1. Create a page on the store aimed at trade buyers, not at a product. [L0968.081]
2. List known business clients on the page. [L0968.081]
3. Add a contact form asking for name, phone, email, products of interest, units per product and purchase timeline. [L0968.081]
4. When a request arrives, ask your supplier for the best wholesale price on those quantities, then send the buyer a quote. [L0968.081]

**Example (older, 2021)**

- A trade buyer asks for 10 units of item one and 10 units of item two. Ask the supplier for the best wholesale price on exactly that order, and quote the buyer a price for that order only. [L0968.082]

**Procedure C: trade page with volume-based discounts (older, 2020)** [L1088.056]

1. Create a page for business customers and link it from the footer menu. [L1088.056]
2. Explain that discounts are based on order volume and that custom quotes are available. [L1088.056]
3. Show a phone number so businesses can call directly. [L1088.056]
4. Embed a contact form that asks for name, company name, phone number, email, products of interest and quantities. [L1088.056]

### Quoting prices to prospects

- Discuss price and delivery terms only after you know the prospect wants what you sell, subject to other issues being worked out. Confirm the product fit first, then address price, and do not let price questions replace the discovery process. [L0770.046]
- Budgetary quotes are rough estimates based on expected production quantity, order size and other variables that often change before ordering. They can be prepared with little effort for a prospect who needs early pricing, but first evaluate the probability of winning his business. [L0770.047]
- If the product meets the prospect's criteria, do not give a precise price for every quantity or option before he has committed to buy. Detailed quotes take the estimating team's time and rarely pay off when the prospect has not committed. [L0770.048]

**Net saving formula for a new packaging or process**

- Labor savings + storage savings = maximum extra material cost the prospect will accept.
- If the material price stays below that amount, the net overall cost is lower and he can commit to an order. [L0770.055]

**Example: concrete price test**

- A prospect wants extra advertising value on the outer packaging. The packaging costs about one dollar more per unit. Ask whether he will buy if the net cost stays in line with his current spending. This commitment is needed before any numbers are prepared. [L0770.056]

### Volume-based business discounts

- Business discounts should depend on order volume. Very large orders, such as those over 50,000, can justify deeper incentives than orders around 2,000. (Older, 2020.) [L1088.055]
- Do not give a large discount upfront in B2B deals. Start from your default discount and negotiate from there, with the size depending on margin and order size. (Older, 2018.) [L1109.028]
- Example: one large B2B order of about $66,000 to $67,000 received a 15% discount after negotiation. A few orders of this size per year can add significant revenue and profit. (Older, 2018.) [L1109.029]
- B2B and bulk-buyer discounts can use any agreed percentage, such as 2%, 5%, 10% or 12%, set per negotiated deal. The 12% level was used as the example for a large order. [L1322.014]
- Discounts shown on the standard income line do not include custom quotes made through draft orders. Large stores with bulk buyers may give steep negotiated discounts that never appear in the discount line, so check draft orders separately. [L1046.037]

### Personalised discount codes for business buyers

- A reusable discount code named after the business and given only to that business suits an ongoing account, such as an interior designer who buys for clients. (2025.) [L0472.055]
- A code limited to one use in total suits a unique offer to a single buyer, because nobody else can redeem it once used. This protects the agreed price from being shared or reused. (Older, 2021.) [L1322.018]

**Procedure: personalised discount for one B2B buyer** (older, 2021) [L1322.015]

1. Click Create discount and choose Amount off order. [L1322.015]
2. Name the discount code after the buyer, for example the buyer's first name. [L1322.015]
3. Set the value type to percentage and enter the agreed rate, for example 12%. [L1322.015]
4. Set maximum discount uses to once per customer. [L1322.015]
5. Under customer eligibility, choose specific customers and enter the buyer's email address. [L1322.015]
6. Set the end date to 24 hours from now. [L1322.015]
7. Click save discount and send the code to the buyer. [L1322.015]

**Template: personal discount email** (older, 2021) [L1322.017]

```
Hi [CUSTOMER NAME],

I have created a personal discount code for you: [DISCOUNT CODE]. You can apply it at checkout on our store to get [DISCOUNT PERCENTAGE]% off your order.

This discount is valid until [END DATE AND TIME, TIME ZONE], so please place your order before then.

If you have any questions, just reply to this message.
```

### Deposits for custom and made-to-order products

- Taking a deposit on a custom-built or made-to-order product is common practice, similar to construction or home building. Deposit size matters. A large deposit, such as 50% or 60%, may discourage buyers because the deposit is non-refundable and they fear losing it. A small deposit, such as 20%, lowers the barrier to committing but raises the chance that customers back out, leaving the store with a built product. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0721.023]
- State clearly that deposits on custom orders are non-refundable, in the checkout or product wording, before the order starts building. [L0721.024]
- Test the deposit model on the store by offering a few options and measuring which gets more interest, rather than relying on one theory. Options include split payment, pre-order and deposit schemes. Apps exist for pre-selling and pre-ordering. (Older, 2021-22.) [L0721.025]

### Negotiated offers and make-an-offer promotions

**Make an Offer app on the store**

- Procedure: [L1204.032]
  1. Install the Shopify app called Make an Offer. [L1204.032]
  2. Set the promotion to run for a fixed window, for example three days. [L1204.032]
  3. A Make an Offer button appears under Add to Cart. [L1204.032]
  4. The shopper chooses an offer price and enters an email address and phone number. [L1204.032]
  5. You accept the offer, counter with a higher price, or decline it. You are never forced to accept. [L1204.032]
- Example on a 3,000 dollar product: offers within about 10 percent below the list price were accepted, while offers around 20 percent below were countered. Expect offers at both levels when you run this. [L1204.034]

**Make-offer promotion by email for a few days per month**

- Procedure: [L1205.010]
  1. Email the list and run Facebook, Instagram and Google remarketing ads to long-standing audience members, announcing that offers are accepted for a stated number of days. [L1205.010]
  2. Customers reply to the email or send a private message on Facebook with offers on specific products. [L1205.010]
  3. Accept, counter or decline each offer, since no offer has to be accepted. [L1205.010]
  4. Shopify App Store apps can add a make-an-offer button, but manual handling is recommended for the first attempt. [L1205.010]
- Mistake: the make-offer approach is time intensive because someone must respond to every offer. It can still produce many extra sales, so the team needs capacity to answer offers during the promotion window. [L1205.011]
- A make-an-offer or name-your-price email invites subscribers to propose a price, and you accept or counter each reply. This tactic runs only through email, not ads, and it is risky for large lists. [L1224.010]

**Make-an-offer email procedure (2025)**

1. Send an email to the list inviting customers to make an offer or name their price on a product they want. [L1287.060]
2. Ask them which products they are interested in and how much they would pay. [L1287.060]
3. Accept offers that are fair and reply to the rest with a counter price. [L1287.060]
4. Use this for promotions that are not public, since it carries risk if the list is large. [L1287.060]
- Mistake: a site-wide make-an-offer button was removed after it caused problems with Google. Keep make-an-offer offers in email instead of a public button on the store. [L1287.061]

### Dealers, free goods and referral credit

- Avoid extra discounts or loading offers to dealers, because they often shift sales between stores rather than adding total demand. Spend that budget on acquiring new customers instead. [L1261.104]
- Free goods given to dealers must be sold with advertising. Each free case means the ads must sell proportionally more to earn the same return. Figure the cost of free goods at your selling price before offering them. [L1261.105]

**Template: referral credit letter for an existing customer** [L1261.085]

```
Dear [CUSTOMER_FIRST_NAME], [REFERRER_NAME] tells us that you are one of her good customers. She says you pay as agreed, so we have opened a credit account for you with us, ready for use at any time. When you want anything from our range, just place your order and pay nothing in advance. We are glad to send it without any further investigation, since you were recommended to us in this way.
```

- Keep the recommendation as the reason for the account, and adjust the names and offer to match your product and terms. [L1261.085]

### Where sources differ

- **Site-wide make-offer button:** an app can place a Make an Offer button under Add to Cart for a fixed window, such as three days. [L1204.032] A later source reports that a site-wide button was removed after it caused problems with Google, and that offers should run through email. [L1287.061, L1224.010] Use the app only for a short, monitored window. Use email for the default channel. [L1205.010, L1287.060]
- **Discount code use limit:** a reusable code named after the business suits an ongoing account. [L0472.055] A single-use code suits a unique one-off offer. [L1322.018] The personal-code procedure sets the limit to once per customer, which is a per-person limit rather than a total limit. [L1322.015]
- **Upfront discount size:** a large discount should not be given upfront. [L1109.028] Depth of discount can rise when margin and order size support it, and can be used to close a larger deal. [L1068.056, L1277.082] Trade buyers should not be promised a trade discount before the supplier quote is known. [L0834.122]
- **Margin:** high-ticket products are judged by the margin left after all costs. [L0660.024] Bulk orders may be accepted at a lower margin only when total profit still stays high. [L1277.082]
- **Trade request data:** Procedure A asks for quantity, delivery location and date, then requests a supplier quote for that exact order. [L0834.122] Procedure B asks for products, units per product and purchase timeline, then asks the supplier for the best wholesale price on those quantities. [L0968.081] Use the fuller data set for large orders where delivery terms affect the supplier price.

## Affiliates, influencers and partner offers

### Choosing which products affiliates may promote

- Offer affiliates your top 10 bestselling products, not your whole catalog. Affiliates cannot tell which items are worth promoting or consistently in stock if everything is open to them (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.006]
- Do not give affiliates access to your entire catalog and expect them to find the winners. Offer your best sellers and tell affiliates which products sell, because affiliates want offers that convert (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.029]
- Do not run affiliate offers on products that are frequently out of stock. Promoting unavailable inventory wastes affiliate traffic and effort, and you still owe commissions on cancelled orders without recovering the payout (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.007]

### Setting affiliate commission rates

- Choose product-specific commission rates rather than a blanket rate across your entire catalog (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.004]
- Set each rate from the profit margin of that product or brand, not as one blanket rate. The commission must still leave you profit and must be more attractive than alternatives such as the Amazon affiliate program (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.028]
- Common ranges for high-ticket products are 5-10% of the sale price, or a fixed amount such as $50 per transaction (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.004]
- Worked margin case: with a 30% supplier margin where you eliminate advertising costs, an affiliate commission of 8-10% still leaves healthy profit, because customer service and fulfillment remain your only variable expenses (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.012]
- Reconciling the two figures: the 5-10% range is the general benchmark, while the 8-10% figure applies to a product with a 30% supplier margin and no advertising cost. Calculate from your own margins before using any benchmark (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.004, L0213.012]
- Calculate affiliate commission rates from your product margins and cost structure before offering them (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.012]
- Affiliate offers at 3-9% commission are not worth it for core products. Promote an affiliate product only when it helps customers reach the same goal and you do not sell it directly (older advice, 2021). [L0962.104]
- Example: a treadmill store can promote meal delivery kits as an affiliate because treadmill buyers often want to lose weight. The store does not sell the meals itself and earns a commission on promotion (older advice, 2021). [L0962.105]
- Example of a high commission on a digital product: a treadmill store offers a $37 ebook on abs with a 75 percent affiliate commission, which means about $28 per sale. [L0923.052]

### Cookie windows and affiliate motivation

- Longer cookie windows (60 days or unlimited) encourage affiliates to keep promoting, because they earn commission on delayed conversions. Affiliates stop promoting when sales do not trigger payouts within your cookie duration (older advice, 2021-22). [L0213.010]

### Onboarding a new affiliate

1. When a new affiliate joins, look at their site and the products they already recommend (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.030]
2. Choose the products on your store that fit their audience and that you know sell (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.030]
3. Send those products to the affiliate, or let them switch an existing link to your store if you offer a better rate (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.030]
4. Keep the product list to products you can actually sell, since an affiliate will not sell what you cannot sell yourself (older advice, 2021-22). [L0719.030]

### Affiliates and MAP pricing

- Affiliate commissions do not violate MAP. The customer still sees the advertised price at MAP, and the commission paid to the affiliate is a separate business transaction. [L0276.066]

### Where to find affiliate programs

- For information products, ClickBank is the largest affiliate marketplace for digital products. For physical products, use Commission Junction, ShareASale or Amazon Associates. [L0923.025]
- If your niche has nothing on ClickBank, search for niche resources, forums, communities and blogs to see what your audience buys. [L0923.025]

### Partner and cross-promotional bonuses

- Partner with indirect competitors, meaning businesses in the same industry that sell something else, ideally a digital product or service. They gain audience, and you gain a bonus offer your margin could not fund alone (older advice, 2021). [L0469.068]
- Example: a store selling a $500 home-brewing kit partners with a homebrew conference whose tickets normally cost $100, offering buyers a 75 percent discount or a free pass. It also partners with an online brewing school whose $47 intro course is given free. Both partners gain exposure and members (older advice, 2021). [L0469.069]
- Partner bonuses are digital services from another company, such as a free first lesson, a free 14-day or 30-day trial, or an extended trial of a membership. These partners are usually happy to send you customers, so they often agree to free trials or extended discounts. [L1204.040]
- Cross-promotion within one store's funnel, using a treadmill dropship store as the starting point:
  1. Visitors have their email captured and are pixeled on Facebook and Google. [L0923.052]
  2. They are then offered a free weights store and a home gym store (both dropship stores). [L0923.052]
  3. They are offered the $37 ebook on abs with a 75 percent affiliate commission. [L0923.052]
  4. They are offered a Nutrisystem subscription, which pays recurring commission for as long as the customer receives the meals. [L0923.052]
  5. They are offered a three-week diet product on ClickBank that pays more than $100 per signup. [L0923.052]
  6. Smaller accessories such as jump ropes sit in an Amazon Associate store that is used only for retargeting. [L0923.052]

### Revenue sharing with partner stores

- In a brand partnership you receive a percentage of each sale, similar to a traditional affiliate relationship, and you do not handle fulfillment for those orders. The lesson does not state a specific commission rate (older advice, 2021-22). [L0869.008]
- Offer a partner store an affiliate-style cut only on products whose margin can cover that fee. Sharing revenue is viable only when you can afford the commission (older advice, 2021-22). [L0869.011]

### Setting up a low-cost affiliate funnel

- Starting costs for a bootstrapped affiliate funnel: domain $10, first-year hosting $12, and autoresponder free for 30 days, for a total of $22. After the trial, autoresponder prices were about $19 per month and rise as the subscriber count grows. [L0514.030]
- Example: an affiliate program paying $300 per referral for a digital course. If the site costs $22 to set up, the first sale nets about $278, and the payout rises when the course price goes up. [L0514.032]
- When you charge a small amount, such as $7, to filter out visitors who only want free information, place the payment step on your own affiliate site, either at the opt-in or on the page right after it. Do not run this paid step through Facebook ads. [L0514.037]

### Influencer and creator fee negotiation

- Pricing sequence for a creator fee:
  1. Define the usage terms and exclusivity you need. [L0785.011]
  2. Open the negotiation and document each party's rights. [L0785.011]
  3. Assess campaign complexity and the effort required. [L0785.011]
  4. Define the exact deliverables and their scope. [L0785.011]
  5. Evaluate the creator's track record and expertise, then negotiate the final fee with all of these factors included. [L0785.011]
- Check whether a top-tier creator fits your budget before you pursue them. Influencer fees range from about $50 to about $1 million per campaign, so decide where your budget sits in that range first. [L0785.012]
- Budget and negotiation sequence:
  1. Assess your overall marketing budget and set a reasonable share for influencer marketing that does not compromise your wider strategy. [L0785.013]
  2. Prioritize creators who fit within that budget and your goals. If you cannot afford a creator, pick one within your range. [L0785.013]
  3. Research industry standards for audience size, engagement rate, content quality and niche, so you neither overpay nor underpay. [L0785.013]
  4. Adjust the market rate for expertise, campaign complexity and exclusivity, and reflect these adjustments in the contract. [L0785.013]
  5. State your budget and constraints openly and communicate throughout the negotiation. [L0785.013]
  6. Show flexibility, for example through long-term collaboration, mutual promotion or affiliate arrangements. [L0785.013]
  7. Emphasize the mutual benefit of the partnership, since a brand that offers unique value to the creator's audience can get rate adjustments. [L0785.013]
  8. Discuss a long-term partnership, where a multi-period deal can lower the rate, gives the creator steady income and gives you a reliable creator. [L0785.013]
- Paying more or less than the market rate because you are uninformed about benchmarks for the niche and creator size leads to overpaying or underpaying. Research benchmarks before making an offer. [L0785.014]
- Pursuing a high-profile creator who is outside your budget wastes time and money. Do not commit to a creator you cannot afford, even if they are your first choice. Find one who fits both your budget and your goals. [L0785.015]
- Creators are often willing to come down to your budget when communication is good and they genuinely want to work with the brand. Be transparent about your budget limits from the start. [L0785.016]
- Long-term partnerships usually lower the cost per campaign: the creator gets regular income, and the brand gets a creator who keeps working with it. Discuss a reduced rate in exchange for a longer commitment. [L0785.017]
- A brand's first offer to a creator was $200 and was negotiated upward toward $400, and the full amount was paid via PayPal. Starting low leaves room to pay more and still close the deal. [L1379.016]
- Set aside about $500 as a test budget for influencer campaigns. The minimum a creator typically accepts is about $200, and some campaigns will fail. [L1379.015]
- Use a discount code for influencers only when your margin allows it. A 25% discount code was affordable only because the margin left enough profit on each sale, and the code tracked the influencer's campaign. [L0790.027]

### Maximum affordable fee and break-even

- Calculate the maximum affordable influencer fee before negotiating: multiply the follower count by the engagement rate, then by a conservative conversion rate such as 1 percent, and multiply the result by the product price. Compare the expected revenue against the cost of the promotion to see whether it is profitable in the worst case. [L0789.019]
- Example: an influencer with 75,000 followers and a 5 percent engagement rate reaches about 3,750 people per story. At 1 percent conversion, about 37 buyers at a $39 price bring about $1,462 worst-case revenue. A $600 investment is then covered on the first promotion. [L0789.020]
- Break-even formula: Break-even units = promotion fee / profit margin per sold unit. Example: a $500 fee with a $25 margin per unit requires 20 units sold to break even. [L0789.021]

### Influencer payment structures

- Cash-flow split when the brand funds all costs up front: 80 percent of the deal goes to the brand and 20 percent to the influencer for the first three months. After that, a 60/40 split in favour of the brand is mentioned as the next step. Use 80/20 for the initial period and 60/40 afterwards, depending on the phase of the partnership. [L0789.022]
- If you have no cash flow to fund the promotion, offer a 50/50 revenue split instead. The brand provides value upfront by showing results, using relevant case studies from other industries if its own track record is missing. Use 50/50 when the brand cannot fund costs, and 80/20 when it can. [L0789.023, L0789.022]
- For smaller creators, a free product exchange is often accepted if the product is good and the creator's niche fits. If they decline, offer payment instead. [L1379.013]
- Payment options for a paid creator post:
  - Pay the full amount up front, or
  - Pay half up front plus the product, and the second half after the video is posted and has stayed live for the agreed period (for example 24 hours or longer). [L1379.014]

### Affiliate-style creator incentives

- An affiliate commission for a creator can be a fixed amount per sale, for example $2 per order, or a percentage. Tiered commissions based on sales volume are also possible. [L1379.017]
- Affiliate incentives let a creator keep earning from promoting you after the initial post, which builds a longer relationship. This is useful when cash budgets are limited. [L1379.018]

## Digital products, services, private label and direct-response offers

### Choosing a monetization model

1. Traditional dropshipping with a direct-response approach. [L0237.011]
2. Private label products at averaged prices, shipped via a fulfillment center under a different brand name and distributed through your own site and other online retailers. [L0237.011]
3. Custom recipes or subscription box offerings, if your niche supports them. [L0237.011]

- Set a consistent sales baseline of 7 days per week. Weekly revenue of $500 with 20 to 30 orders indicates readiness to invest in private labeling. [L0324.015]

### Digital products: validation and build

- Validate demand before writing the product: get a sale first. Create only the cover and the landing page with the offer, run ads, and if a sale comes in, tell the buyer the delivery window is 3 to 4 days, then write the ebook. [L0513.018]
- Host the store on Shopify and build the landing page with PageFly. Include the pricing and the offer on the page before the ebook exists. [L0513.019]
- Ebook length depends on the niche. Check how long competing ebooks are and aim for maximum value with minimum effort. A three-page ebook is probably too short for most niches, but there is no fixed page count, and 500 pages is not needed. [L0513.022]
- Test a digital product yourself before selling it, because the file may contain errors. [L1431.061]
- A free ebook from a PLR library can be given to buyers as a bonus or used to build goodwill, adding perceived value without production cost. Confirm the license allows giving it away first. [L0513.045]
- Planners, ebooks, and health or diet plans sell well around January, when people set resolutions. Production cost is almost zero, and a logo can be added through Fiverr. [L1431.060]

### Digital products: pricing and margins

- Digital products have very high margins because there is no cost of goods. One seller reports a profit margin of around 90 percent on a digital product, compared with lower margins on physical clothing products. [L0789.048]
- Research the competitor price for the same type of digital product and set a similar price, then add more value so the offer looks better for the same money. Check that the margin still covers the cost of acquiring one customer through ads before you finalize the price. [L0513.043]
- Digital add-ons such as an ebook need no shipping, and the PDF already exists, so they can be sold at low cost. They work best when the ebook has real page count and value the customer can see is worth the add-on price. [L1141.011]
- Quantity tiers on an ebook product page (Source D 2026 example): [L1030.031]

| Tier | Offer | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Buy 1 | 35% saving | [L1030.031] |
| Buy 2 | 50% saving | Flagged as Most Popular [L1030.031] |
| Third tier | As stated in source | [L1030.031] |
| Gift variant | Buy 1 and give 1 to someone you love | [L1030.031] |

- Each tier also included a free bonus cheat sheet, shown as worth $19 or $25 depending on the book. [L1030.031]

### Bundles, upsells and cross-sells

- Ebook bundle example: a single recipe ebook sells for 19 euros, while the bundle of all four problem ebooks costs 40 euros instead of 80 euros when bought separately. The bundle is used as the upsell to raise average order value. [L1131.010]
- At the end of the ebook, offer a paid one-to-one call (for example 100 to 200 dollars via a booking link) for personal questions the ebook cannot answer, turning a digital product buyer into a coaching client. [L0513.020]
- Match information products to the physical product a customer buys: a power tool buyer can be offered a woodworking plans ebook, a drum set buyer an online drum course, and a gun safe buyer a paid community membership. A high-end wallpaper site can sell listings to local interior designers. [L0923.026]
- Service add-on example: a pool table store found local partners who could level the table and glue down the felt, then offered a paid assembly service at checkout. Partners are paid a fixed amount per job, and the store keeps the margin. [L0923.027]
- Top rung example: a premium done-for-you service was priced as a 100,000 USD fee plus 10% of the client's profits, with the total capped at 1,000,000 USD. A top rung can combine a fixed fee with a share of the results it produces. [L0523.137]

### Memberships and subscriptions

- A paid community membership at about 30 dollars per month works when customers pay for belonging to a community they already engage with, not for a product box alone. Its value comes from the content and people it connects. [L0487.052]
- A weekly digital subscription can deliver 5 to 20 new coloring designs each week for about $7 per month. The price was still being tested between $5 and $7. Digital files can be sold worldwide, so the offer works in countries where the physical product cannot be shipped yet. [L1153.079]
- Digital products that provide ongoing value to a niche, such as product research tools, newsletters or advice, can be sold as a monthly subscription. Subscriptions suit recurring content for a specific audience. [L1439.051]
- A membership trial can remove the standard obligation to buy more after the first purchase, and state that plainly: for example, choose any 4 books for $1 each with no obligation to buy anything else. Describe it as an experiment to see whether it attracts readers who value the membership itself. This lowers the risk of the first step. [L0756.077]

### Services: yield and pricing

- Hourly yield equals price per unit multiplied by units completed per hour. A service charged at $25 per unit and finished at 2 units per hour earns $50 per hour. Compare this with market rates of $12 to $15 per unit. [L0378.035]

### Private label and customization

- A low-margin accessory that customers buy repeatedly can stay in the store as a loss leader, provided you use it to sell other products to the same buyer. Alternatively, produce it yourself so the margin works. (Source B, 2021-22, older.) [L1152.003]
- Worked margin example (Source B, 2021-22, older): a manufacturer sells a product at about $21, and it costs them roughly $10, or possibly around $7. If you can source it made for about $7 and sell it at $30, each sale earns roughly $10 to $15 more than the dropship version. [L1152.005]
- Products that can be customized can let customers configure them on the product page and buy directly. Customers accept longer shipping times for custom products. (Source A, 2021, older; the identical unit appears twice in the source and is merged here.) [L0671.056, L0671.195]

### Amazon and eBay pricing

- The ideal Amazon FBA price range is $20 to $50. With recent Amazon changes, about $25 to $60 can work. The price must be high enough to earn profit and low enough for an impulse buy. [L1153.024]
- A price of about $20 or more gives enough margin to outbid established competitors with Amazon paid ads. Products priced far above $50, such as $200, make buyers research more, which lowers impulse conversion. [L1153.025]

### Free offers and trials

- Free consultation as first step: the prospect receives something valuable at no cost and is then converted to a paid service. The formula is to communicate a dream outcome with minimal effort and time, which makes the value clear. [L0440.046]
  - Example A: a free trial month for a fitness coaching service. [L0440.046]
  - Example B: a free photo session for a real-estate listing before a paid package. [L0440.046]
- Make free offers look like they carry value by charging a token amount for trial packages, because a fully free item is often seen as cheap. A trial product the maker is willing to sell at retail signals real quality. [L1261.089]
- Giving a product away free can damage its perceived value and make it harder to sell later, because people resist paying for something they once received for nothing. Charging the normal price for a trial, even a small one, protects the product's value. [L1261.090]
- A trial package priced at a low fraction of retail, such as 15 cents, retained the product's credibility better than a free package did. Customers then saw the product as worth buying. [L1261.091]
- Charging a price to request a sample, or asking customers to split the cost, lowers the response rate sharply and can cost $0.40 to $1.00 more per reply. Keep sample requests free and state that they are free. [L1261.092]
- Partial-price or buy-one-get-one-type offers that ask prospects to share in the cost of trying the product usually fail in direct response testing. Before a prospect has bought, getting them to accept half-price is nearly as hard as selling full-price. [L1261.093]
- Use the word "free" in ads when the offer is genuinely free, because it attracts far more readers and responses. A paid trial offer will generally reduce the number of readers who respond more than it saves. [L1261.094]
- Trial wording: the words trial offer, combined with a money-back promise, belong on cheap front-end offers priced around $49. Leave that wording off high-priced back-end offers, roughly $2,000 and up, because a buyer could download the full content and then request a refund. Back-end offers usually skip the trial framing and often offer no refund at all. [L1356.073]
- Service pattern built on a result guarantee and a pay-as-you-approve trial, with a bonus unit for paid work in a set window. [L1370.120]
  1. Guarantee a visible result or charge nothing for the service. [L1370.120]
  2. Offer a free consultation. [L1370.120]
  3. Let the buyer judge each unit of work as it is finished and pay only for approved work. [L1370.120]
  4. Give one free unit for every set number of paid units within a fixed number of days, such as 21 days. [L1370.120]
  5. Close with a numbered list of what the buyer does next. [L1370.120]
  6. Add a short testimonial that describes a specific bad situation and the fast result. [L1370.120]
  - Example 1, window cleaning: *We guarantee sparkling windows or the job is free. You approve each floor as we finish it and pay only for the floors you approve, and for every three floors you pay for within 14 days, the fourth is free.* [L1370.120]
  - Example 2, pool service: *We guarantee crystal-clear water after one visit or we return the fee. You inspect the first lane before we treat the rest, and every third visit you book in the next month is on us.* [L1370.120]

### Premiums, personalization and exclusivity

- Premiums often beat discounts for immediate response. According to the author's experience, a strong premium can change response by as much as 50 percent. Use a premium when the basic offer alone is not strong enough to make people respond immediately. [L1370.097]
- Case: a casino operator who had sold a lodging, drinks, show and gambling package to repeat customers stopped getting response from the usual deadline. He sent a test letter offering a free six-day, five-night Hawaiian vacation for two, paid for in advance through a bonded travel agency with over 1,000 packages. It went only to past guests who returned to the offer, with a deadline and a limit on the number of vacations. The offer combined a premium with limited availability and a deadline. [L1370.098]
- Test a double premium after a single premium proves successful, such as one item for an order of a set size or two items for a larger order. In one test the overall response rate stayed about the same while average order size rose by nearly 30 percent. [L1370.101]
- Attach a free premium with a stated retail value and a stated limited quantity to a main purchase. In one test, a $5,000 equipment buyer was offered a TV and VCR that would cost $499 to $899 in a store, and only 250 units were available from a special purchase with no reorders. The retail value anchors the perceived gift, and the limit justifies immediate response. [L1370.124]
- Limit an offer to a clear and specific group, such as members of an organization, veterans or executives, because people will act to keep an advantage reserved for their group. Narrow group offers often beat general ones. [L1261.095]
- A personalized item, such as a product with the buyer's name on it, adds perceived value and can increase purchases. People respond strongly to things that belong to them. [L1261.096]
- Example: adding a buyer's name in gold lettering on each book, with the same ads, sold hundreds of thousands of books. The personalization added value without changing the core offer. [L1261.097]

### Direct mail and historical cases

- Selling direct by mail cuts out middlemen and selling expense. This lets a manufacturer offer near-dealer prices and show a saving such as 25 percent on the same tires. Test a direct offer with a small group of buyers first to confirm demand. [L1369.017]
- Case (historical, older): a publisher had contracted for 100,000 sets of a newly revised four-volume history at a low price. Its earlier one-volume edition had sold more than 150,000 copies, and the author cut his royalty for the large order. [L1369.011]
  - The offer was the four library-size volumes, illustrated with 100 paintings, at 25 percent less than the original two-volume price. It was bundled with a one-year subscription to a monthly review magazine. [L1369.011]
  - Buyers sent a reply card without money within ten days, received the set for one week of free examination, and paid 12 dollars in 1 dollar monthly payments or 10.50 dollars cash. [L1369.011]
  - The letters reported that 95,000 of the 100,000 sets had sold, fewer than 5,000 remained, and orders had reached about 500 a day the previous season, so the low price could not be repeated. [L1369.011]
  - Buyers who returned the set within the week paid 25 cents for the magazine copy delivered. The seller claimed that scarcely one reader in twenty would part with a set once opened. [L1369.011]
- Case (1913, older): a publisher ran a series of mailings for a 50-volume classic reading set that salesmen had sold earlier. Six mail-order attempts from 1910 to 1913 had failed; one of them spent 25,000 dollars and sold 18 sets. [L1369.020]
  - In August 1913 the first mailing of 10,000 illustrated four-page letters went out without an enclosure and with a stamped post card. It went to advertising leads one to two years old that salesmen had turned down as unsalable, at about 4 cents per piece including postage. [L1369.020]
  - The first letter pulled 4.5 percent orders on a 39-dollar set, which meant under 1 dollar per order. The first six months showed a 34.5 percent profit. [L1369.020]
  - Follow-ups to the same leads produced these results: [L1369.020]

| Mailing | Response | Note |
|---|---|---|
| First letter | 4.5% | 39-dollar set, under $1 per order [L1369.020] |
| Second letter | 2.5% | Enclosed circular [L1369.020] |
| Third letter | 1.5% | [L1369.020] |
| Fourth letter | 1% | Large folder with only a post card [L1369.020] |
| Final mop-up letter | 2% | Four-page letter, pulled after shorter pieces had stopped working [L1369.020] |

  - Later efforts beyond that were nearly fruitless, so the remaining leads were filed. [L1369.020]
  - The offer was the full set of 50 volumes, with 418 works by about 300 authors and a 76,000-entry index. It was sent for a week of free examination, with payment as low as 2 dollars a month. The price was 39 dollars against 472.05 dollars for the same works in separate editions, a saving of 433.05 dollars. [L1369.020]
  - Later letters itemized retail prices volume by volume. They offered a silk cloth edition at one-eighth of the original sets' price, with 100 sets set aside for free examination. They warned that prices would rise because paper, ink and binding had more than doubled in cost. [L1369.020]
  - The mailings sold many hundreds of thousands of dollars of books over the next five years. [L1369.020]

### Sweepstakes, giveaways and draws

- Time between paid ad exposure and purchase is a risk. The ad spend is already committed, and every day without a purchase lets competitors win the same visitor. Convert the visitor as fast as possible. [L0797.003]
- Setup of an instant giveaway funnel (Source E 2025-26): [L0797.004]
  1. Build an instant giveaway landing page with a tool such as WooRise or another instant giveaway tool. [L0797.004]
  2. Run Meta campaigns with a purchase conversion objective pointed at that funnel. [L0797.004]
  3. Show every entrant an immediate result. [L0797.004]
  4. Nurture the leads by email and convert them over time. [L0797.004]
  5. Optimize the landing page continuously toward return on ad spend. [L0797.004]
- Entrant flow on the giveaway landing page: [L0797.008]
  1. The visitor sees a headline with the prize and the win odds, such as Win 1 of 10. [L0797.008]
  2. A live countdown shows when entries close. [L0797.008]
  3. The visitor enters first name, last name and contact details. [L0797.008]
  4. The visitor accepts the terms and conditions. [L0797.008]
  5. The page instantly shows whether the entrant has lost or won. A loser sees a discount code or a direct purchase offer. [L0797.008]
